# **Foreword**

The archaeology of the early state in Korea reflects a fascinating situation which strikingly exemplifies the problems of protohistory – that tantalizing realm of the past in many lands where the historic records (for early Korea, both contemporary records in China and later local and Japanese sources) have to be reconciled with the accumulating evidence obtained by the techniques of the prehistorian using the material record alone. It is fascinating for an archaeologist in the West to see how the same problems occur, working in a very different scholarly tradition, and how they begin to find resolution.

This collection of essays by Gina Barnes, one of the very few Western scholars who know the Korean material well (in addition to that of Japan and China), is a pioneering attempt to grapple with the issues and problems, employing at the same time the perspective of the anthropological archaeologist working in the context of the theoretical debates which have characterized Western archaeology since the 1960s. To the reader like myself, unacquainted with recent advances in Korean archaeology, these essays make a contribution which transcends their primary purpose of contributing to the prehistory and protohistory of the Korean peninsula. These papers bring into striking relief the issues which underlie many of the debates surrounding the emergence of complex societies in many parts of the world.

First, there is the difficulty which inevitably follows dependence upon historical records mostly written several centuries after the events in question. The Aegean prehistorian reading Homer and Herodotus, the student of the European iron age following Caesar and Tacitus and Strabo, the Mexicanist studying Father Bernard de Sahagun, and the Anglo-Saxonist perusing the Venerable Bede and *The Anglo-Saxon Chronicle* all undergo the same feelings of illumination and exasperation. The illumination comes, of course, from the wealth of circumstantial and generally plausible detail, the frustration at the difficulty of reconciling directly with the emerging archaeological picture an account written at a later period and with a mindset so different from our own.

Secondly, and even more problematic, is the use of such materials in formulating a view on the emergence of "complex society" or "the state". The modern anthropological definition of "the state" is far removed from the discussions by ancient Greek writers on the nature and origins of the polis on which it is nonetheless ultimately based. In the Korean case, the discussion here makes it clear that the contemporary Chinese term guo meaning "walled settlement" or "walled town" – was likewise used by many Chinese commentators to imply a certain level of socio-cultural organisation and is taken by some scholars today to equate with the emergence of the early state. But, as the discussion here leads us to enquire, to what extent can we follow this equation? The arguments at this point can display some structural homology with those constructed by many Anglo-Saxon archaeologists in the face of the burgh, and indeed by scholars emphasizing the significance of the oppidum in the north European iron age. But should we regard such simple urban or proto-urban societies as states?

Gina Barnes investigates these issues and others, and her well-informed essays offer a number of relevant approaches. The period in question here may be said to conclude with the unification of much of the Korean peninsula brought about through its conquest by the Silla polity in the year 668, a couple of centuries before the consolidation of state society in Anglo-Saxon Wessex by Alfred the Great. She chooses firstly for special consideration the early production of stoneware (requiring a firing temperature greater than 1000° centigrade), of interest not only for the possible linkage in the field of pyrotechnology between this and the production and use of iron but also for resolving the chronological problems involved in reconciling the historical records and the archaeological data. Secondly, she selects for discussion the remarkable finds of iron body armour from the Kaya tombs at the south of the Korean peninsula, dating from the early 3rd century ad. The use of prominent body armour at this time raises a number of questions which prehistorians working in other areas of the world will recognise as relevant to their own concerns: the conditions appropriate for the emergence of iron technology, the early use of the horse for military purposes and the social consequences of horse-riding elites, the interactions between "primary" and "secondary" centres, and the significance of conspicuously rich inhumations under great burial mounds.

For the student of the archaeology of East Asia, these collected papers will be an essential resource. For the archaeologist and prehistorian working in other areas and concerned with processes of social change in conditions of inter-regional interaction, they will provide a rich source of analogies and of potential explanatory pitfalls as well as putative solutions. I read this volume with a reinforced realisation that our current approaches to the emergence of state society and the use of such terms as "chiefdom" and "tribal society" remain problematic, just as they still seem for some purposes indispensable. For the Western reader (and in a different way for the Eastern reader also), the author has given a challenging account of a wide range of problems. She outlines with authority what is known. She makes clear how very much there is yet to learn about the emergence of complex society in Korea. Underlying all of this is the implication, never explicitly formulated here, that our understanding of the processes of state formation anywhere leaves much to be desired.

Colin Renfrew Cambridge

# **Preface**

## **History of the research**

This volume of collected articles traces an interest in Korean state formation from both historical and archaeological perspectives. The articles, many of them revised from their original presentations [see individual title footnotes], are arranged in approximately the chronological order in which they were first written, preserving the problem orientation with which I have approached the subject. The overarching problem has been an analysis of state formation and developmental processes on the Korean peninsula, as background to the complex social development on the Japanese islands – the latter being my primary research focus. Thus, the topics chosen for investigation in several of the chapters have direct relevance to the study of protohistoric Japan: the development of stoneware production – the technology which was transferred to Yamato in the early to mid-5th century; the production of iron – which impacted both on the supply of iron to the Yamato state and the outfitting of the militarists operating in the pen/insular area in the 4th and 5th centuries; and the formation of fortified sites – reflecting the dedication to warfare by early pen/insular rulers. Researchers with interests in Korean relations with China would have chosen a very different set of topics to pursue, but it is a fact that Japanese state development cannot be understood without reference to the development of the Korean peninsula. Thus, my involvement in Korean archaeology has come from a need to flesh out the background to my Japanese interests but has, I hope, resulted in a coherent set of studies about Korean state formation, valuable in its own right and offered here as such.

Professor Renfrew has rightly stated in his Foreword that this book does not directly address the questions of state formation in a broader, worldwide context. The theory of state formation is currently at an impasse, partly because of a shift away from social evolution as a framework for investigating long-term social change, and partly from internal definitional problems arising from the use of that very framework. How does one distinguish archaeologically between a chiefdom and a state? Are these terms accurate and useful for investigating the kinds of social transformation that occurs in the maturation of political complexity? Much of the Western work on state formation bogs down in the problems of establishing and maintaining justifiably discrete social units of analysis; and when researchers do not respect definitional terms but use them in idiosyncratic ways, then comparisons lose their meaning and become unenlightening.

Korean state formation in itself is not immune to such definitional problems, as described here in Chapter 1. Although most researchers in the field are historians and not anthropological archaeologists, Western schemes of thought on social development are making their way into the field of studies. Thus, one encounters a variety of views on the stages and critical processes of social development which must be understood in order to assess the developmental trajectory. I have attempted to make such an assessment implicitly within the framework of Western state formation studies and hope that this makes the Korean case more amenable to worldwide comparison.

## **Temporal and geographical context**

For those readers who are unfamiliar with the global situation of Japanese and Korean state formation, an analogy is readily available in the Roman Empire of the West. The Roman Empire (250 bc–ad 400) was approximately contemporaneous with another large empire of the East: the Han Dynasty of China (206 bc–ad 220). The Eurasian continent was fairly well dominated by these two large political systems at its western and eastern ends, respectively, and there was trade contact that served to funnel ideas and material objects between them. Both empires reached out to groups and cultures beyond their core areas and indelibly influenced the trajectories of these peripheral societies. In both the Korean and British cases, an indigenous Iron Age hierarchy and developmental trajectory was truncated by the incorporation of their territories into the larger empires. Britain was profoundly changed by its incorporation into the Roman Empire for four centuries (41 bc–ad 410), and the northern Korean peninsula was similarly brought into the dynastic administrative system through the establishment of Chinese military commanderies (108 bc–ad 317); chieftains in the Japanese Islands were also given status in the tributary system and court hierarchy for the lst–3rd and 5th centuries ad.

Thus, the early states in both Korea and Japan are secondary states, clearly building on their experiences with and knowledge of the Han court, its military and trade systems, and its Wei Dynasty successor. They did not appear, however, until the demise of dynastic power. In a process which I term 'political isostasy', the regions which had been suppressed and made subservient to the great empires bounded back after the empires' demise with complex social formations of their own. This occurred with the rise of the Anglo-Saxon kingdoms in the 6th and 7th centuries following the retraction of Roman troops from western Europe, and with the rise of the Korean and Japanese states in the 4th and 5th centuries following the fall of the Wei successor to Han.

Though we commonly use the terms Korean and Japanese to identify these early states, one must be careful not to equate them with the nationstates of today. Here, these terms are used in geographical and linguistic – not political – senses. On the Korean peninsula there eventually arose three strong states – Koguryŏ, Paekche and Silla – with the small Kaya chiefdoms sandwiched between the latter two. These states were involved in complex military and political relationships, alternately hostile and friendly. The shifts among them from enemy to ally status make it impossible to lump them all together as a holistic unit opposed to the Japanese state of Yamato. Yamato, in fact, was a firm ally of Paekche through many centuries and was also closely related to Kaya through its industrial and trade needs. Thus, the peninsula and islands were not as firmly divided by the Korean Straits as they are today.

Despite this early political multiplicity, the peninsula and islands were eventually taken over and ruled by individual nation states, Silla on the former and Nara in the latter. These 7th-century states indeed do form the basis for the modern nation-states, and Korea and Japan are two countries of the world which can claim long-term political continuity from ancient times into the modern world. As such, their techniques of political survival and perpetuation make an interesting topic for study in themselves, and the inhabitants of these modern states can be rightly proud of their heritages deriving from these early state societies.

The Korean peninsula is today divided into two: North and South Korea, which most Koreans take as an unnatural state of affairs. They look to the example of Germany to reunify themselves as a common geographic and political unit. However, history is quite revealing in showing us that the peninsula has never been one integrated unit of the type envisioned by its modern inhabitants. Partly this is due to the varying environmental and climatic attributes, which endow the north with a cold winter and limit the production of rice as a major crop, while the south has warmer weather and a different forest composition. These differences have encouraged different subsistence and social systems from 1500 bc onwards, when the north was recipient to millet farming regimes and the south accepted rice agriculture. Later, in the Early Bronze Age (from 700 bc), different traditions of bronze usage and especially dolmen burial characterised north and south. The north first received iron tools and technology from China, after 500 bc, and it was subjected to the establishment of the Chinese commandery of Lelang in 108 bc. The south retained its bronze-using phase until it developed its own iron and stoneware industries in the early centuries ad.

During the Han occupation of the northern peninsula, the Koguryŏ peoples of the northeastern mountains developed their own particular social and political identity, while the southern peoples were oppressed under the yoke of tribute and tax provision to the Han. Upon liberation from the Han and Wei courts, the peninsula hosted the Koguryŏ nation in the north and fostered the development of Paekche in the southwest, Kaya in the south, and Silla in the southeast. When Silla united the peninsula in 668, the new northern border did not incorporate all of the former Koguryŏ territory; in fact, much of Koguryŏ became the new state of Bohai (Parhae in Korean) with refugee Koguryŏ aristocrats ruling over Malgal commoners. Parhae's medieval successor, the kingdom of Liao, was ruled by the Khitan. This territorial split of Parhae/Silla then Liao/Koryŏ was perpetuated into early modern times when the Yi Dynasty arose after the Mongol retreat. Then the northern border of Chosŏn, the pre-modern state of Korea, reached only as far north as the Taedong River, where the capital of North Korea today is located.

Thus throughout history, the Korean peninsula has hosted multiple states – often organised in a north/south split, although the border marking the divide between northern and southern states was not stable but was drawn across the mid-peninsula at different places in different eras. One might take the view that the DMZ dividing the two Koreas today is merely the latest manifestation of a historical tendency toward north/south partitioning of the peninsula, and that re-unification of the Chosŏn-period territory is not necessarily more natural. It is in such historical questions that the study of the early Korean states becomes relevant to modern-day concerns, and the elucidation of early state formation and development will equally enlighten us on the social processes and mind-sets of the peninsular inhabitants and what they have bequeathed their successive descendents.

#### **Conventions**

Each of the following chapters has an abstract which places the article in the research trajectory. Bibliographies for the chapters are included with them rather than compiled at the back of the book. Please note that Chapter 1 is keyed to both Appendices which appear immediately after it, as well as having its own reference list.

East Asian names in the text appear surname first followed by initials. Full personal names are given as much as possible in the bibliographies, but differing spelling conventions make it extremely difficult to unify references. A unique referencing system has been adopted in Appendix 1B to cater to this difficulty, and the format of that bibliography differs from the rest in several respects due to its special function.

## **Acknowledgements**

Support for the research for each of the various projects undertaken is acknowledged at the end of each chapter. Here, I would like to add to those a general acknowledgement to the University of Durham, whose support was critical to the latest stages of research and formulation of the volume. Thanks are due, in particular, to Margaret Greenhalgh, Mark Taylor, Jeongyoung Kim, David Hughes, Joshua Wright and Jonathan Price for their help in presenting this material in an integrated fashion. Finally, I owe a great debt to many of the Korean scholars mentioned herein, whose generosity in time, materials, and guidance through the archaeological and historical records enabled me to come to my present understanding of Korean protohistory. I hope I may pass on this understanding to the next generation of scholars and stimulate their interest in some of the still enigmatic aspects of early Korean state formation.

## **Chapter One**

# **Early Korean States: A Review of Historical Interpretation\***

#### **Abstract**

This opening chapter lays out the chronology and processes of state formation on the Korean peninsula. Seven case studies of states as recognized in the Korean historical literature are reviewed and evaluated against a framework of Western anthropological theory of chiefdomstate transformation. The mainstream historical perspective based on the concept of walled city (guo) as a basic definition of an Eastern state is clarified and challenged anthropologically. With this framework in place, the different periods of state formation can be archaeologically assessed in succeeding chapters. The review, first written and published in 1990, has been revised and brought up to date through 1999. Western-language works on Korean state formation are given in an extensive bibliography (Appendix 1B) and are bolstered by references to Korean- and Western-language background works in the chapter references, marked in the text as [in Ref. List]. A chronological chart for state events is given in Appendix 1A.

## **Introduction**

Instances of state formation on the Korean peninsula between 500 bc and ad 500 (see Table 1) were multiple and varied in character. All, however, were secondary states which formed at the periphery of the more powerful large states of China. References to some of the earlier of the Korean entities occur in the Chinese dynastic histories, where the character guo is used to describe them. 1 Guo was the term applied to the Zhou-period polities in China (1st millennium bc) and is usually translated as "state"; many of these Zhou polities became "principalities" under the succeeding Han Dynasty (206 bcad 220). The character guo as applied in Chinese works to early Korean polities has been conceptualized in a similar manner by Korean and Japanese scholars: all but a few dissenters translate it into English specifically as "state".

Thus, some of the earlier members of the universe of Korean "states" studied by historians are assigned to that category by virtue of the use of guo in describing them, not by any internal criteria of organization or archaeological evidence of developmental status. Others are included by virtue of the fact that they are known from Korean, Chinese and Japanese historical sources as well as archaeological evidence to have become powerful, centralized polities which played crucial roles in the political relations of 5th- and 6th-century East Asia. In two cases, the origins of these states are traced back to particular guo mentioned in the Chinese histories, so some sort of developmental continuity through time is assumed.

Much of the basic data on Korean state formation resides in the Chinese dynastic chronicles and historical treatises. The most relevant of these are:

- 1.the Shiji, written by Sima Qian (145–c. 90 bc) during the Han Dynasty (206 bc–ad 220) but covering Zhou-period China (1027–221 bc) and its peripheral relations;
- 2.the Weizhi (Chronicles of the Wei Dynasty), compiled by Zhen Shou (233– 297) as a record of events during the Wei Dynasty (ad 220–265). The Weizhi exists as part of the Sanguozhi (Chronicles of the Three Kingdoms), the official histories of the Chinese dynasties of Wei (220- 265), Shu Han (221–263), and Wu (222–258) which comprised the Chinese Three Kingdoms period (220–280).

The original Korean accounts of the states' early histories, however, have not survived, and only later works incorporating this material cover these periods retrospectively. These works are the 12th-century Samguk Sagi (Kim P. S. 1145 [in Ref. List]; Yi H. D. 1962; Gardiner 1970; Ch'oe J. S. 1987 [in Ref. List]) 2 and the Samguk Yusa, written by the monk Iryon (1206–89) in the 13th century. The latter has been translated into English (Ha & Mintz 1972; Yi P. D. 1975); and the flavour of the former can be known from Rufus (1946) and Gardiner (1982b) and from translations or paraphrases of Yi Dynasty (1392–1910) histories based on the Samguk Sagi, published serially in the earliest volumes of the journals Korean Repository (from 1892) and The Korean Review (from 1901). All of these basic historical sources are reviewed briefly by Gardiner (1966), and research problems deriving from these sources are discussed by Yi H. J. (1987) and Yi P. D. (1985).

3.the Houhanshu (Chronicles of the Later Han Dynasty, ad 23–220), compiled between 398 and 445 and based on the Weizhi despite the Han Dynasty having occurred earlier than the Wei. These records were all written approximately contemporaneously with the phenomena on the Korean peninsula which they describe.

The historical data found in these sources have been woven into differing versions of individual state origins by scholars in the field. Some of these have engendered extreme reactions in nationalistic contexts (cf. Centre for East Asian Cultural Studies 1963; Lee S. K. 1967; Kim C. C. 1970a; Lee S. K. et al. 1967). It is the present-day variation in interpretation that will concern us here. Each case of state formation will be examined for the ways in which it has been treated and, especially, presented to the Western audience. Some attempt will then be made to give an anthropological assessment of the developmental status of these societies according to the social evolutionary framework used in anthropological archaeology.

#### **Issues in Korean state formation**

Four major and several minor syntheses of Korean history are reviewed here, 3 revealing three extremely controversial issues that are relevant to state formation on the peninsula. These issues are: the developmental status of the early polities; whether they were created by foreign populations or developed as native institutions; and the main technological or organizational factors in the emergence of these societies. In this section, each of these issues shall be inspected in turn to provide a broad background of approaches against which individual studies of state formation can be contrasted.

## **Developmental status**

The Samguk Sagi, relied upon by many Korean scholars as a true version of history, gives founding dates for the Korean "Three Kingdoms" of Silla, Koguryŏ and Paekche (Figure 1.1) as 57, 37 and 18 bc, respectively, and it gives king lists from those dates onwards (Grayson 1976; Best 1979; Li O. 1981, 1986). The state of Silla subsequently conquered Paekche (in 660) and Koguryŏ (in 668), thus unifying the peninsula as United Silla (668–935). Whether the Three Kingdoms existed as early as their putative founding dates imply, however, is a matter of great divergence of opinion – especially since archaeological evidence for social stratification, in the form of sumptuously furnished mounded tombs, does not appear until the 4th century ad. The attributes of longevity and coherence bestowed on these early states by the Samguk Sagi may be understood from the historical milieu in which these records were compiled. Rodgers (1988) writes of the compiler's need to create a native identity for his own Koryŏ Dynasty (935– 1392), the successor state to United Silla, because of the fragmentation of the Chinese state which had previously been relied upon for Koryŏ's legitimization. By elevating the status of Silla, partially by fixing its founding date earliest among the Three Kingdoms almost exactly twelve sexagenary cycles backwards from the pacification of Paekche (cf. Gardiner 1966: 87), Kim Pu-sik (1075-1151) as compiler of the history was able to create a legitimate native ancestry for the Koryŏ state – an ancestry which has been adopted by modern scholars.


**Figure 1.1** Hypothetical territories of the Three Kingdoms and Kaya at the height of Koguryŏ power in the late 5th century ad with modern city and river names (after Han W. K. 1970)

Following the Samguk Sagi, some scholars attribute full-fledged state status to these Three Kingdoms from their designated founding dates. Recently, some efforts have been made to define developmental stages within these societies; in particular, the notion of "tribe" (K. pujok) 4 has been employed in contrast to or in conjunction with "state". In most ofthese cases, however, the word "tribe" is left undefined (e.g. Henthorn 1971); the same is true of the term "tribal state" (K. pujok kukka), which is in common use among historians (Hatada 1969; Sohn et al. 1970).

According to the Treatise on Period Divisions in Korean History, published by the Society for Korean Economic History (1970 [in Ref. List]), the term "tribal state" was introduced into the scholarly literature by Paek N. M. in 1933. It did not come into regular usage until 1948 when employed by Son C. T. in his Outline of Korean Ethnic History. In this and subsequent works, the designation "tribal state" was generally applied to Korean Bronze Age society (ca. 1st millennium bc), whose dolmens were thought to be tangible indications of status enjoyed by Bronze Age rulers.

In 1964 Kim C. C. proposed an evolutionary sequence from "tribal states" to "tribal leagues" (K. chebujok nyŏnmaeng wangguk) – in other words, a confederation of several tribal states. Kim C. C. was the first to offer a systematic explanation of what this form of organization consisted of: primogeniture in the ruling clan, collection of taxes, mobilization of labour, redistribution of crops, reallocation of land, control of ritual, and the use of imported iron and other products. This evolutionary scheme was modified by Han W. K. (1970: 25) who proposed that "tribal leagues" grew out of "tribal society" (K. pujok sahoe) and went on to become "kingdoms" (K. kodae kukka, lit. "archaic states"). Han thus originally eliminated the "tribal state" stage. However, in the revised edition of his work (Han W. K. 1986) he has added a large section on the "tribal state" in place of his "tribal society" – just when other authors are backing away from the tribal concept altogether.

In 1976 Ch'eon K. W., writing in Korean, created the terms sŏngup kukka (lit. "walled-town state") and nyŏngyŏk kukka (lit. "regional state") to describe the stage of development of some of the polities mentioned in the

Korean chronicles before they adopted the Chinese territorial system of government. The historian Lee K. B. has seized upon the idea of "walledtown state" as an antidote to the concept of "tribe". He makes the former his first evolutionary stage (1984: 13) and eliminates "tribal" from the second stage by naming it "confederated kingdoms" (K. nyŏnmaeng wangguk) instead of "tribal leagues" (K. chebujok nyŏnmaeng wangguk). His last stage is the same as Han W. K.'s kodae kukka, though it is translated into English as "aristocratic state" rather than "kingdom". Lee K. B.'s emphasis on the settlement as the focus of the early "state" reflects his assumption that a walled town was occupied by the ruling elites, with commoners living outside. He considers the walled-town stage of polity formation to have been characteristic of Bronze Age society in Korea, with dolmens fulfilling the same status-indicating function as envisioned by Son C. T. for his "tribal states".

Kim J. B. (1974, 1987) also takes issue with all authors who use "tribal" in connection with the early Korean polities. He cites Western anthropological work among tribal societies organized on the basis of kinship groups, specifically lineages. These societies, he relates, are most often uncentralized – that is, acephalous – in great contrast to all cases of supposed Korean "state" formation, in which "leaders" or "rulers" are the main focus of organization. In contrast to those using "tribe", Kim J. B. (1987) specifically employs the anthropological category of "chiefdom", as does Choi M. L. (1984); but Kim translates it as kunjang in Korean while Choi translates it as chokjang.

The work of Choi M. L. embodies the most extensive application of Western evolutionary concepts to state formation archaeology. He distinguishes clearly between "chiefdom" and "state" in theory; but unfortunately, he bases his identification of the latter on historical documentation rather than archaeological criteria. He assigns the Late Bronze Age dolmen society of the southwestern peninsula, his main subject matter, to chiefdom status by interpreting the dolmens as representing social stratification by virtue of the labour and specialist craft skills invested in their construction and furnishing (Choi M. L. 1984).

## **State origins and ethnogenesis**

The second issue in Korean state formation studies concerns the question of whether the states in question were created by foreign refugee populations or whether they developed as native institutions. The traditional tendency to assign all political and cultural development in Korea to Chinese sources has been well reviewed by Kang & Macmillan (1980). However, the current swing of the theoretical pendulum encourages efforts to assign indigenous origins to these societies; in fact, it has even led to the denial of the existence of the historically documented Chinese administrative units on the peninsula. Archaeological remains and historical accounts traditionally attributed to the Chinese commandaries are now viewed as native phenomena, particularly by North Korean scholars (cf. Pearson 1979).

This shift in ideological preference seems to stem from two causes: on the one hand, rising national consciousness, and on the other, the adoption of anthropological viewpoints according to which the demonstration of indigenous rather than imposed origins seems to bestow more scholarly value and native prestige on the society being studied. As a result, there is a growing body of literature which resuscitates earlier work treating state formation and the emergence of a Korean ethnic identity as part and parcel of the same phenomenon, 5 a trend which is deplored by Kim J. B. (1987). In these works, and also in current textbooks (cf. Korea Herald, 9 June 1987), the mythical founder of the first Korean state named Tan'gun6 who was born of a bear and a heavenly prince, has been elevated to historical status (Ryu S. G. 1987 [in Ref. List]), giving unwarranted time depth to the state he represents and obscuring the complicated mixing of peoples throughout the turbulent periods of peninsular history. The recurrent phrase "5000 years of Korean history" derives directly from this use of Tan'gun – nothing more.

#### **Organizational stimuli**

The third issue in Korean state formation concerns the differing opinions among scholars as to the main factors in the emergence and development of these societies. Han W. K., for example, places primary stress on the catalytic effects of metals: first bronze, which "stimulated the development of tribes, and subsequently of tribal leagues" (Han W. K. 1970: 12); then iron, which stimulated the "tribal communities ... to develop into integrated political units" (Han W. K. 1970: 22). Apparently these "integrated political units" are to be understood as "tribal leagues" (Lee J. W. pers. comm.). In contrast to the use of metals as technological stimuli to socio-political development by these authors, 7 Choi M. L. (1983b, 1984) assigns important roles to trade and military force in at least one case of state formation. His study utilizes the flow diagrams of systems theory in postulating multivariate causation of state formation while also utilizing the Renfrewian concept of the "multiplier effect" (Renfrew 1986 [in Ref. List]).

In contrast to the above studies, which focus mainly on individual causes, Hatada (1969: 7) develops a more generalized but very consistent thesis that societies dominated by the Chinese had their native social organization destroyed and were therefore handicapped in further development, whereas those on the periphery of Chinese control kept their native tribal organizations intact and were thus able to strengthen and "develop to maturity". This thesis is exactly the opposite of the traditional view that all social and political development occurred because of Chinese presence and influence, 8 and it seems to be particularly relevant to the individual cases of state formation on the Korean peninsula – although it might not be universally applicable.

## **Seven candidates for statehood, and the Chinese commanderies**

There are seven protohistoric societies in Korea which can be evaluated in terms of the above three issues: Chosŏn, Chin, Koguryŏ, Samhan, Kaya, Paekche and Silla. These comprise the candidates for protohistoric state formation, which is our present concern, but a further two might be mentioned for the early historic period: United Silla and Parhae. It is impossible to describe at least the first seven societies without touching on controversial points. They will therefore be described below, together with discussion of such controversy as occurs in the literature. It must berecognized, however, that the very selection, ordering and evaluation of these controversial points here serve to create an image of each society that is the product of this author's judgment rather than any emergence of the "truth" from behind the controversies.

Before proceeding, let us note that these protohistoric societies span the millennium from approximately 500 bc to ad 500 (Table 1). The crucial historical event affecting peninsular development during this period was the occupation of the northwestern part of the Korean peninsula by the Chinese between 108 bc and ad 313. This Chinese presence is now being questioned, as mentioned above, but there is no doubt that the economy and political organization of the peninsula was greatly affected by close intercourse with successive Chinese states during the millennium under consideration.

Let us also note that this millennium coincides approximately with what can tentatively be called the Korean "Iron Age". There are many difficulties in using this term because of differential regional development on the peninsula as well as the problem of social status in metal usage (Barnes 1983a see [Chapter 2, this volume]). Nevertheless, iron is hypothesized to have appeared on the peninsula around the 4th century bc. Thus the latter half of the 1st millennium bc is called both the Early Iron Age (IA-I) and the Late Bronze Age (BA-II). The period between ad 0 and 300 also has several names: the Proto-Three Kingdoms period, the Late Iron Age (IA-II), the Samhan Period and the Lelang period.9 The Three Kingdoms period, 10 beginning in ad 300, takes its name from the states of Silla, Paekche and Koguryŏ as known from the chronicles; the starting date for this period, however, is not historically but archaeologically determined by the advent of the mounded tomb culture (Kim W. Y. 1983a).

The mounded tomb culture is one of two aspects of early Korean state society (the other being Buddhist culture) that has had repeated exposure in the Western literature. 11 It is beyond the scope of this essay to provide detailed descriptions of material cuture, but in general it can be stated that the tombs fall into the easily recognized tradition of royal burials: monumental earthen mounds covering stone-built or stone-enclosed chambers in which a variety of sumptuous prestige goods were deposited with the dead. These goods include bronze mirrors, gilt-bronze horse trappings and other ornaments, gold jewelry, jade beads, rare gold and glass cups, iron armour and weapons, etc. The labour invested in the tomb construction, the isolation and general individualization of the tombs from other contemporaneous burials, and the access to both precious resources and specialized labour embodied in the grave goods all signify a highly stratified society in which the elite participated in a distinct material subculture not accessible to the general population. Unfortunately, the archaeological data from the tombs is not well integrated with perspectives on Korean state formation, which are mainly based on historical texts. This problem of integration between the two forms of data will be returned to in the Discussion below, after we have examined the individual case histories of state formation on the Korean peninsula.

#### **Chosŏn**

Chosŏn might be most easily characterized as a border polity which developed to the east of the Zhou states of China (Figure 1.2). It is thought to have attained a specific identity by at least the 2nd century bc, and it was destroyed by the Chinese Han Dynasty invasion of the peninsula in 108 bc. Thus, it is the only example among the seven cases considered here that completely pre-dates the traditionally recognized formal Chinese presence on the peninsula.

Very little is known about Chosŏn, but care must be taken to distinguish between two possible aspects of it as recorded in the Chinese histories: firstly as a place name (Cbaoxìan in Chinese; this spelling will be retained here when referring to the geographical area) and secondly as a polity. The early references in the Zhanguoce (Crump 1979), Shanhaijing (Henderson 1959) and Shiji (Watson 1961; Choi M. L. 1983c), works of the late Zhou (475–221 bc) and Han (206 bc–ad 220) periods respectively, seem to treat Chaoxian as a region until 195 bc, from which point it is referred to indirectly in the text and notes of the latter work as a guo. The region of Chaoxian is described in Zhou times as lying east of the state of Yan (Crump 1979: 506), then in the next two periods as bordering on the Liaodong commandery of the Qin and Han states (Watson 1961: 258). From these descriptions, Chaoxian might be understood to have included at one time the lower Liao River basin in Manchuria, 12 but most commentators place it east of the Yalu River, the current boundary between China and North Korea. The interpretation of the name Chaoxian as a place name is in concordance with the meaning of its characters: "dawn-fresh", that is, the region in the east where the sun rises from the perspective of north China. This interpretation is confirmed for ad

325 when a native leader received the rank of Duke of Chaoxian, long after the demise of the Chosŏn polity (Ledyard 1977: 14). From 300 bc until 195 bc the northeastern state of Yan reportedly exerted great influence in the Chaoxian region (Watson 1961: 258):


**Figure 1.2** The location of Chaoxian in relation to the Zhou state of Yan, 3rd century bc (after Herrmann 1966)

When Yen [Yan] was at the height of its power, it invaded and conquered the regions of Chenp'an [Zhenpan] and Ch'ao-hsien [Chaoxian], appointing officials to rule the area and setting up fortifications along the frontier. ... When the Han arose, however, it regarded the region as too far away and difficult to guard, and rebuilt the fortifications at the old border of Liao-tung [Liaodong], leaving the area beyond, as far as the Pei ... River, to be administered by the King of Yen.

Han W. K., on the other hand, turns the tables and states that "Ancient Chosŏn was ... able to compete successfully with the state of Yen" (1970: 12). Although we do not know the true nature or extent of Yan political rule in the Chaoxian region, finds of Zhou knife money at sites in the river valleys of the northern peninsula attest to economic interactions extending throughout the area, and many refugees from the warring states and unification wars in China are thought to have poured into Chaoxian, giving it a culturally mixed population (Li Chi 1932; Henderson 1959; Han W. K. 1970: 20).

As regards the polity of Chosŏn, there are three different founding myths, which are described and evaluated by Gardiner. The first concerns a man named Qizi of China's Shang Dynasty (1700–1027 bc), who allegedly fled to Korea in 1122 bc during the fall of the Shang to the Zhou. Qizi's flight is actually mentioned in the Shiji but, as Gardiner points out, is presented in that document separate from any connection with Chosŏn; therefore the founding of Chosŏn by Qizi (K. Kija) must have been a later conflation (Gardiner 1969a: 3,9). Henderson and Mc Cune both suggest by implication that the Qizi legend has nothing to do with the founding of Chosŏn, the former relating it to "the coming of metal culture to Korea" during Yan influence in the area, and the latter assigning it to the Han conquest of the peninsula in 108 bc (Henderson 1959: 152; Mc Cune 1962).

The second founding myth concerns Tan'gun, the son of a union between a heavenly prince and a bear-turned-woman. According to the 13th-century Korean work Samguk Yusa, Tan'gun established Chosŏn in 2333 bc. He is then stated to have been succeeded by Kija (C. Qizi), who was "placed on the throne [by] king Wu of Chou [Zhou]" in 1122 bc (Ha & Mintz 1972: 32–3). Kang and Macmillan comment on the fact that this myth appears to be a native product (Kang & Macmillan 1980: 49), but Kim Chewon has attempted to trace the myth itself to Chinese sources. He proposes that two stone slabs in the Han Dynasty tomb of the Wu Liang family in Shandong depict in bas relief the essence of the Tan'gun story, thus forming the "Chinese version of, and probably the Chinese prototype for, the Tan'Gun legend of Korea" (Kim C. 1948–9: 43). Sohn et al., on the other hand (1970: 20–2), try to maintain the Korean identity of the myth by pointing out that Shandong as well as Chaoxian was anciently inhabited by the Dong-i (a Chinese term meaning "eastern barbarians"; cf. An H. S. n.d.), implying that the Tan'gun myth belongs to the Dong-i ancestors of the modern Koreans, not the Han Chinese. Finally, Kim J. B. (1980 [in Ref. List], 1987) disassociates the Tan'gun myth from Chosŏn altogether and instead attributes it to the Neolithic inhabitants of the Korean peninsula.

The third "myth" concerns Weiman, a military officer of the Zhou state of Yan who is said to have fled to the northern Korean peninsula around 195 bc and "founded a principality with its capital at Wang-hsien [Wangxian], a town which apparently occupied the site of modern P'yŏngyang" (Gardiner 1969a: 9). The Weilue, a mid-third-century text quoted in the preface to the Sanguozhi, records that Weiman (K. Wiman) actually usurped the rulership of the existing Qi [Qizi] Dynasty from a king named Zhun (K. Chun) and thus took over the kingship of the state of Chosŏn; but Gardiner has questioned the authenticity of this report, saying, "... it contains so much [information] that is additional for which no earlier sources are known that it seems somewhat suspect" (1969a: 10). Gardiner nevertheless mentions that "As early as 203 bc, a group of socalled Northern Maek [inhabitants of eastern Manchuria/northern Korea] had campaigned on behalf of the founder of the Han dynasty" (1979: 71), illustrating that there was both Han-Chosŏn interaction and social unrest in that time and place. Kim C. W. dismisses Tan'gun and Kija as legendary but speculates that the immigration of Weiman and his followers initiated the Iron Age in the northern Korean peninsula (Kim C. W. 1966). This is not entirely fanciful, as Yan was the largest northern producer of iron in late 1st millennium bc.

Chosŏn history is therefore often divided into three phases, each named after one of the legendary founders: Tan'gun Chosŏn or Old/Ancient/Ko-/ Chosŏn, then Qizi/Kija Chosŏn, and thereafter as Weiman/Wiman Chosŏn.13

There is incontrovertible evidence of the existence of the Chosŏn polity by 109 bc, when the alleged grandson of Weiman, Youqu (K. Ugo), came into conflict with the Han Dynasty and precipitated the Han invasion of the peninsula. The relations leading up to the invasion are detailed in the Shiji (Watson 1961: 258–63). In historical terms, then, the examination of the Chosŏn polity must begin here with an evaluation of the structure of that polity as recorded in the documents, and then work backwards with archaeological data to understand the development of the polity in socioeconomic terms. Although such a task is beyond the scope of the current paper, let us review how other authors have dealt with Chosŏn origins and used archaeological data to support their arguments.

Hatada (1969) subscribes exclusively to the Qizi story, but he moves it up from 1122 bc to the third century bc in order to account for the emergence of the Chosŏn "state" at that time and to give it a Chinese identity. He even declares that the territorial extent of Kija Chosŏn was equivalent to the modern provinces of Hwanghae and P'yŏngan in North Korea (1969: 4). Han W. K. also proposes a late date – in the 4th century bc – for the emergence of "Ancient Chosŏn"; however, he locates Chosŏn at this time in the Liao River basin of Manchuria, only to have it ousted when Yan annexed this area around 300 bc (Han W. K. 1970: 12–6). He ascribes only legendary status to Tan'gun as the founder of Chosŏn, but then he reifies the legend by stating that the dominant clan of Chosŏn was the "bear totem family". Han accepts Weiman's usurpation of rule from Chun, King of Ancient Chosŏn, although he rejects the idea of an alien dynasty. Instead, he avers (1970: 16):

It seems likely that Wiman and his followers made common cause with the patriarchs of these [Ancient Chosŏn] clans in their revolt against the King [Chun]. Wiman would thus have had loyal followers among the Korean population of Ancient Chosŏn, so that his rule was at least partly supported by his subjects and thus did not appear completely alien.

Although Choi M. L. also accepts the historicity of Weiman as a person and dates Ko-Chosŏn to 194 bc, he postulates that military force and trade were the "prime movers of the establishment of the Wiman state" (1983a [in Ref. List], 1984, 1985, 1992). Choi does not deal with Qizi, nor do Han W. K. (1970) and Henthorn (1971), in their accounts of Chosŏn origins; and Weiman is not mentioned in Hatada's version. In contrast to the above authors, the writers of the two other major historical syntheses as well as Yun N. H. (1985) give Chosŏn a much longer life span. Firstly, Sohn et al. believe that the "tribal group" of Ko-Chosŏn was extant, complete with rulers, as early as the 10th to 7th centuries bc; Ko-Chosŏn "conquered the neighboring tribes, using ... bronze arms to emerge as a big power in Korea", finally coming into conflict with Yan in the 3rd century bc (1970: 22–6). These scholars accept that the Tan'gun story is only a founding myth and that the Qizi story is a Chinese fabrication. The story of Weiman's usurpation of the Chosŏn kingship, on the other hand, is taken in stide: "With Wi Man as ruler, Ko-Chosŏn succeeded in uniting many tribes in the Taedong River basin into a strong union of tribal states, based on the traditional tribal organization" (1970: 26).

Secondly, Lee K. B. (1984: 14) proposes that Old Chosŏn was a "walledtown state" which originated in the Taedong River valley and:

proceeded to combine with the other walled-town states scattered throughout the region between the Taedong and Liao Rivers to form a single large confederation, the head of which came to be designated as its king.

Lee assumes this process to have taken place before the 4th century bc, and he links the early polity to the Tan'gun myth by proposing that "Tan'gunwanggŏm" was a title borne by successive leaders of Chosŏn. Lee rejects attempts to interpret either the Qizi or Weiman legends as reflecting the extension of Chinese political control over the peninsula; and he stresses, as does Han W. K., the continuity of features between Old Chosŏn and Weiman Chosŏn, claiming that Weiman was not "a man of Yen but [of] Old Chosŏn" because of his unusual clothes and hairstyle (Lee K. B. 1984: 16–7).

Kim J. B. (1987) also equates Chosŏn with the Bronze Age but rejects all association with Tan'gun and the Chinese. In fact, he pushes Tan'gun back into the Neolithic period, thus disassociating him from state formation altogether. Kim sees in Chosŏn a two-stage developmental process from "chiefdom" to "state" (as defined in Service's evolutionary scheme: Service 1962, 1975 [both in Ref. List]), the divide falling at the time the Chinese title of "ruler" was changed from hou ("marquis") to wang ("king") in the 3rd to 4th century bc. Therefore, even the entity ruled by King Chun (before Weiman's takeover) is considered by Kim J. B. to be a full-fledged state, although the nature of the state then changed when "Wiman Chosŏn ... emerged driving out [Old] Chosŏn by force of arms" (Kim J. B. 1987: 38).

Depending on the centuries and areas assigned to the existence of Chosŏn by these authors, different archaeological assemblages are also attributed to the inhabitants – particularly to the rulers of the supposed polity. Kang I. G. (1987) reviews all archaeological materials found in the northern Bohai Bay region and concludes that the lute-shaped dagger was associated with a strong Chosŏn state. Sohn et al. (1970: 23–4), who assign the earliest dates to Chosŏn, interpret the burials with fine bronzes in the Taedong River valley as the graves of Chosŏn rulers. Lee, who likewise treats bronze daggers and mirrors as authority symbols, assigns the bronzes and animal-shaped belt buckles found in the Liao and Taedong River valley areas to Old Chosŏn (Lee K. B. 1984: 12–3, 16). Sohn et al. then state that the Chosŏn rulers "introduced megalithic culture, characterized by dolmens and cists" (1970: 23–4); and the dolmens of at least the 3rd century bc in the P'yŏngyang region are attributed by Henthorn to Old Chosŏn (1971: 22). As for the bronze and iron carriage parts and horse trappings, Lee K. B. says they "must have been possessed only by a small elite stratum" (1984: 15). Han W. K. agrees, attributing horse-drawn coaches, iron swords, bows and arrows to the ruling class, part of whom he interprets as refugee groups from the north (1970: 13). Kim J. B. is also of the opinion that at least some of the horse trappings and carriage fixtures discovered in the pit-burials of the Taedong basin belonged to the social elite, specifically of Weiman Chosŏn (1978: 87). Thus, we have a general situation in which almost any archaeological remains in the northwestern peninsula that can be interpreted as linked to status differentiation prior to the establishment of Lelang are assigned to the polity of Chosŏn, regardless of how widely these features and artifacts are distributed.

Returning to surer footing, the political organization of Weiman Chosŏn can be glimpsed through the Shijfs account of the Han conquest of the northern peninsula in 108–9 bc (Watson 1961: 258–63). The "king" (C. wang) of Chosŏn, Weiman's grandson Youqu, had subordinate to him several "prime ministers" (C. xiang), "high ministers" (C. dachen) and "generals" (C. jiangjun). One general was also termed an "assistant king" (C. biwang) in the manuscript, and of course there was the "crown prince" (C. taizi), Youqu's son Zhang. The son of one of the prime ministers was also mentioned as having cooperated with the crown prince. There is no assurance that these terms are any more than Chinese descriptions from the perspective of the Han court; still, at the least it can be supposed that the polity was ruled by a hereditary elite whose members were differentiated to some degree by both rank (prime vs. high ministers) and function (minister vs. general). The Shiji states that some of these personages were enfeoffed as "marquises" (C. hou) for helping with the defeat of Chosŏn, and therefore we may assume some continuity in local elite between the Chosŏn polity and the Lelang commandery established there in 108 bc.

Choi M. L. (1983a, 1983b, 1985 [in Ref. List], 1989) goes further than these documents in hypothesizing that the Weiman state was composed of four social classes and encompassed a high degree of labour specialization for the production of bronze and iron goods. He also postulates that the government controlled a trade system based on iron, copper, tin and horses, and that it sponsored small-scale irrigation works.

In assessing the developmental status of Ko-Chosŏn, it is all too apparent that we are dealing here with a non-pristine border situation in which there was much interaction and exchange of objects, ideas and people with the Zhou states to the northwest. This interaction seems to have extended to at least the Han River basin of the central Korean peninsula prior to 108 bc. Knife-coins of the Zhou states, however, are also found in western Japan, so direct or down-the-line trading could have extended even further.

At any rate, within the region demarcated by the Liao and Han Rivers – that is, the region containing Chaoxian – at least one historically attested polity having a ruling hierarchy had emerged by the 2nd century bc. Whether the ruling families themselves were natives or immigrants from the Zhou states is a highly sensitive question not easily resolved in today's political climate. 14 But regardless of the exact ethnic identity of these people, it is clear from the Shiji that there was considerable population movement from the Zhou states into the region, so that the elite group probably ruled over a culturally if not also an ethnically mixed population.

It is also fairly clear that the ruler, stated in the Shiji to have been located in the the walled town called Wangxian, derived much of his power from dealings with the Zhou and Han states. If his territory was large enough by 108 bc to have been divided into four commanderies upon the conquest of Wangxian, then this maximal territory was the culmination of trends in political development through interaction and cannot be projected backwards into the 1st millennium bc without a corresponding contraction in size. It is possible that there were several Early Iron Age polities in the Chaoxian region in the 1st millennium bc; probably organized as centralized but simple chiefdoms, these are easily envisioned as having been dominated by a fellow ruler whose own polity became known as Chosŏn. In all likelihood, this ruler's power derived partially from his ability to deny his rivals access to the Han court – an obstructive activity which brought on the Han conquest of 108 bc. That Chosŏn was not entirely successful in its attempted hegemony is shown by the rebellion of a "tribal leader" called (K.) Namnyo who changed his allegiance – and that of 280,000 of his people, so it is said – to Han China in 128 bc. This realignment is recorded in the Chinese histories as the establishment of the first of their commanderies on the peninsula, but that commandery only lasted two years (cf. Gardiner 1979:

71). No firm territorial hierarchy seems to have existed, either within Chosŏn or between Chosŏn and other polities. In describing the subsequent conquest of Wangxian by Han troops, the chronicles make no mention of any other towns at all in the Chaoxian region. Chosŏn thus seems to have been a "citystate", with a simple ruling structure, no territorial administration, but dominating a large hinterland that contained other chiefly figures who lacked the advantages of the Chinese connection.

#### **Chin**

The entity of Chin has been neglected in past historical works and does not play a large role in Korean history, but it has recently attracted a certain amount of scholarly attention. Both Han W. K. (1970: 31) and Lee K. B. (1984: 24) accept the existence of the Chin "state" and interpret it as having occupied the entire southern half of the peninsula, below the Han River. Lee calls it a "culturally homogeneous entity" (ibid.) represented by the southern-style dolmen burials containing polished stone daggers. Chin leaders are recorded as having interacted with the officials of the Lelang commandery in its early years, but its quick dissolution is attributed both to the "divide and rule" policy of Lelang and to the flow of immigrants from the north after the Han conquest of the region (Sohn et al. 1970: 28,30). In its place rose the Samhan polities of the 1st to 3rd centuries ad [see Samhan section below].

Two views of Chin origins obtain. In one, it is said to have been founded by the refugee King Chun, the last ruler of Ko-Chosŏn, who had employed Weiman to guard the border and was then overthrown by him. As Weiman grabbed power in Chosŏn, Chun supposedly fled south of the Han River and set up his new "state" of Chin, calling himself "King Han". However, Lee K. B. and Han W. K. assume the state of Chin to have been in existence before "Wiman Chosŏn occupied the Taedong River basin" in the 2nd century bc (Lee K. B. 1984: 24); as evidence, Lee cites a Chinese memo stating that Chin had contacted Han China during that period, seeking to import its iron culture. This liaison between Chin and Han China is said to have been obstructed by Weiman Chosŏn – one of the reasons for the Han Dynasty invasion of Chosŏn. In this version, the already extant Chin received King

Chun and attendant refugees from Weiman Chosŏn before the latter's downfall, thus obtaining its knowledge of iron culture indirectly (ibid.: 26).

Certain scholars have made efforts to identify this Chin entity with the later King Chin, ruler of the Mahan "state" of Mokchi – one of the Samhan societies to be discussed below. Han W. K. presumably can be included in this category, since he declares that the most powerful of the Chin tribes "inhabited an area called Mokji-kuk or Wŏlji-kuk" (1970: 31). These efforts are resoundingly rejected by Kim J. B. (1974: 5) on the basis that the similarity in names between the Chin king and the Chin state is not enough to reveal a connection between them, especially when they were separated by 350 years' time. Nevertheless, the full significance of these names – the Chin "state", King Chin, and the Samhan division of Chinhan [see Samhan section below] all rendered with the same Chinese character (chen) in Chinese documents – has not yet been thoroughly dealt with.

Issue can be taken with Chin's supposed state-level attribution. Lee's reliance on the existence of a "culturally homogeneous entity" as proof that the entire southern half of the peninsula was integrated under one polity is unacceptable: cultural boundaries seldom equate directly with political boundaries. Small centralized chiefdoms may indeed have existed in that Bronze Age society, with the bronze burials signifying elevated social status. But if this were the case, Chin could have been any one of them, and without much more historical data, it is impossible to determine the exact location of Chin and equate it with particular archaeological remains. The nature of the Bronze Age culture and chiefly polities of the southern peninsula are topics dealt with in detail by Choi M. L. (1984) and Rhee S. N. (1984).

#### **The Chinese commanderies**

Despite recent suggestions by North Korean scholars that Lelang was not a Chinese commandery (cf. Pearson 1979), the traditional view will be adhered to here. Lelang was one of four commanderies newly instituted by the Han Dynasty in 108 bc in the former region of Chaoxian (Figure 1.3). Of these four commanderies, only two (Lelang and Xuantu) survived successive reorganizations; and it seems that even these had their headquarters relocated once or twice (Gardiner 1969a).

Lelang's historical course was varied. During the Wang Mang interregnum in China (ad 9–23), its fortunes declined sharply with the interruption of the central node of the bureaucratic hierarchy. Han Dynasty authority was reasserted over Lelang in the late 1st and 2nd centuries. With the ultimate waning of Han rule, Lelang passed in ad 204 into the hands of a Chinese warlord family by the name of Gongsun based in Liaodong which was gaining power in the region (Gardiner 1972). The commandery was recovered in ad 238 by the Wei Dynasty which succeeded the Han in ad 220. Some time between 204 and 220, the Gongsun warlord in charge of Lelang had created yet another commandery called Daifang out of the southern portions of Lelang (Ikeuchi 1930; Chōsen Sōtokufu 1935 [in Ref. List]); when Wei gained control of the region in 238, it also took over this new commandery (Ikeuchi 1929). The final demise of Lelang is attributed to attacks by the Koguryŏ state in ad 313.

The commanderies of Lelang and Daifang were organized along the same bureaucratic lines as Han Dynasty provinces with local subdivisions. This was the jun-xian system, variously translated as "country" and "prefecture" by Han W. K. (1970: 19,30), and as "commandery" and "prefecture" by Hatada (1969: 3) and Ikeuchi (1929); the latter terms will be used here. The commandery heads were short-term appointees from the central Han and Wei courts, but it is generally understood that immigrant Chinese families from the former Chosŏn polity supplied the ranks of senior and junior officials in the commandery and that local officials were drawn from the native peninsular population (Gardiner 1969: 20); Pai H. I. 1989a, b; see also Mc Cune 1950). Pai H. I. has examined burial evidence from the region for the effects of acculturation on local populations. Thus, the bureaucratic hierarchy incorporated and emphasized ethnic differences, perhaps utilizing previous Chosŏn rankings for bureaucratic placement and extending Chinese administrative patterns into areas beyond former Chosŏn control.

![](_page_44_Figure_0.jpeg)

**Figure 1.3** The Chinese commanderies, Koguryŏ and Samhan in the Proto-Three Kingdoms period (after Gardiner 1969)

Although Lelang was ostensibly established as a military commandery, its function was more economic in nature (Lee J. W. 1982a). Through outposts such as this, the Han court pursued the policy of reverse tribute: buying peace from border peoples with sumptuous gifts of Chinese origin and with seals and other ritual paraphernalia of status for use within the local status hierarchy (Yu 1967). Voluntary or cooperative submission to the Han court meant great material gains and prestige for outlying chieftains. Thus, the headquarters of the Lelang commandery – believed located at least in its later phases within the walled enclosure of Chosŏn's former capital, Wangxian, on the left bank of the Taedong River south of modern P'yŏngyang – served as a node in an extensive diplomatic trade network extending from the central Han and Wei courts to the Japanese islands.

However, in addition to the outgoing diplomatic trade involving items such as bronze mirrors, silk, brocade, jade, vermilion, and gold seals, a second trading network operated to supply both utilitarian and precious goods to the commandery elite. Lacquerware and fragrant timbers, jade, bronze and silver seals, beads and other personal ornaments and weapons were brought in from China, while local products such as hardwood timber, fish, salt, iron and agricultural produce were extracted from the local peninsular populations (Han W. K. 1970: 21).

The peninsular groups under varying degrees of domination by the Chinese commanderies included the Koguryŏ in the northern mountains and the Samhan of the southern peninsula. It is known that Koguryŏ participated in diplomatic trade with the Xuantu commandery in eastern Manchuria, receiving royal titles, court robes and caps (Gardiner 1964: 154, 180). But it is not easily known to what extent the Samhan were involved in formal diplomatic relations with Lelang. The general view of historians is that Chinese influence took a more intimate and insidious form in dealing with the southerners, extracting tribute under threat while expending very few precious gifts in the process. However, recent excavations at Choyang-dong (Ch'oi C. G. 1982; Kim W. Y. 1986a: 44; Pearson 1985) and Taho-ri (Anon. 1988 [in Ref. List]) indicate that precious goods were also going to individuals of status in the Samhan region, and Lee J. W. (1982a, b) views the Samhan polities as being integrally involved in the prestige good network.

Sohn et al. have characterized Lelang's policy towards Samhan as "divide and rule" (1970: 28). This policy was begun with the Chin "state" which preceded Samhan in the southern peninsula; the Lelang commandary (Sohn et al. 1970: 30):

confered Chinese government titles on Chin tribal chiefs. And only these were authorized to trade with China through Lolang [Lelang]. Those who held Chinese titles were thus pitted against those who did not, and the fall of Chin eventually ensued.

Among the Samhan, according to Hatada (1969: 13), this policy was implemented by:

conferring official seals to Han tribal chiefs in addition to titles such as district lord and district chief. ... This created new power, but one which could not be consolidated because of the number of seals issued. The system served simply to spread more widely the petty authority of those working in the service of the commandery-prefectures.

The opposite view is held by Lee J. W. (1982a, b) who develops a picture of very strong, centralized polities in the Samhan region that were not subject to Lelang's domination but thrived on the Lelang trade to the extent of subjugating their own neighbors [see Silla section below].

The strength and influence of Lelang varied through time, fluctuating with the fortunes and finances of the sponsoring dynasty. During the Wang Mang interregnum of the Han Dynasty, chaos is said to have prevailed in the northern peninsula with Chinese occupants fleeing the area and peninsular natives moving in (Gardiner 1969: 21). The prosperous periods are manifested in sumptuous Chinese-style burials for the commandery elite, 15 but it is an open question how much Chinese cultural or administrative influence remained during the periods of decline. Those scholars who believe that Lelang was never under Chinese administration interpret the Chinese-style materials as evidence of linking into the Chinese trade networks by peninsular elites (Pearson 1979), and Chinese documents dealing with the commandery are simply discounted. Lelang's final weakening has been attributed to the Xianbi tribes of Manchuria moving into western Liaoning and cutting off Lelang from the Chinese court in the late 3rd century (cf. Watson 1959: 83).

## **Koguryŏ**

## **Brief history**

The polity of Koguryŏ developed among the peoples of the eastern Manchurian massif during the first few centuries after Han China established its commanderies on the peninsula (Rhee S. N. 1992a, b). With the weakening of Lelang in the late 3rd century, Koguryŏ grew stronger; and in ad 313, Koguryŏ forces attacked and destroyed the commandery (Figure 1.4). Koguryŏ was the first Korean state to accept Buddhism; though known as early as 313, it was officially adopted in ad 372.16 The 4th century also saw the importation from China of the technology of writing, Confucianism, astronomy, medicine and the system of state education for the sons of nobles (cf. Watson 1959). The earliest extant written material from the Korean peninsula consists of a stone monument erected near the Kungnaesŏng capital on the occasion of the death of the nineteenth king of Koguryŏ,

Kwanggaet'o, in ad 414. Now located in the northeastern Chinese province of Jilin this monument has engendered heated debate over its contents due to accusations of Japanese defacement during the colonial period (Kim J. Y. 1996). 17 Throughout the 5th and 6th centuries, Koguryŏ was in constant interaction with other states in Korea, China and Japan – as partially attested to by this inscription (Szczesniak 1946). In 427, the Koguryŏ moved their capital from Kungnaesŏng in the Donggou region of the Yalu river to the former Lelang commandery headquarters, naming their new capital P'yŏngyang. The several Koguryŏ capitals (Sekino et al. 1929 [in Ref. List]; Barnes 1991 see [Chapter 6, this volume]) are marked by the presence of mounded tombs, many with painted murals in their chambers. 18

The mature Koguryŏ state is thought by Han W. K. (1970: 52–3) to have taken shape during the reign of King Sosurim (371–84) and reached maturity after the move to P'yŏngyang. Han sees the state administration as having developed through three stages. At first there were twelve grades of nonspecialized officials. These officials were later divided into two groups, the (K.) Hyŏng ("Elder brothers") taking over "the powers formerly exercised by the various tribes", the (K.) Saja ("Messengers") being concerned with communications and tax collecting. Finally fourteen governmental posts were established, each of which had its own rank and title and the top five of which handled the important affairs of state. Li O. has written at length on Koguryŏ myths and social customs in addition to political and administrative matters (1980, 1988). The historical and archaeological materials upon which such studies are based have been recently compiled into two volumes by Yonsei University's Research Institute of National Learning (Yonsei Taehakkyo 1987–8 [in Ref. List]). Lee K. B. furthermore discusses the formation of the Koguryŏ state with regards to ethnicity, territory and politics, evaluating the utility of the concepts "wall-town state" and "tribal state" (1985 [in Ref. List]). The Koguryŏ military system and martial arts have also come under scrutiny (Asmolov 1992; Anon. 1971b). In 598, Koguryŏ was attached by the newly centralized Sui dynasty in China (Wong 1980) and a later alliance between Tang China and Silla finally overcame and destroyed Koguryŏ in ad 668. Upon its defeat, the southern part of Koguryŏ was incorporated into United Silla and the northern part emerged as the state of Parhae. 19

![](_page_48_Figure_0.jpeg)

**Figure 1.4** Koguryŏ territory and major military movements between ad 242 and 341 with archaeological sites

#### **Developmental stages**

At least four stages in the development of Koguryŏ can be ascertained from research based on the Chinese dynastic histories. 20 During the earliest stage, between 75 bc and ad 12, the Koguryŏ are known only as a branch of the Fuyu peoples of the northern Manchurian Basin (K. Puyŏ) (cf. Ikeuchi 1932; Lee K. B. 1963). The Koguryŏ branch was under the administration of the Kaogouli (K. Koguryŏ) prefecture of the Xuantu commandery in southeastern Manchuria (Gardiner 1979). It is not known whether the prefecture took its name from the people, or whether the people came to be known by the place name of the prefecture. Although the Koguryŏ had a leader who was titled "marquis" (C. hou) (Gardiner 1964: 101) they do not appear to have comprised a territorial polity in their homeland, the Hun River drainage. Gardiner sees them as mere "clients of the Han empire who were recruited to fight in the border conflicts against the steppe nomads. In ad 12, the Koguryŏ rebelled against the Han and in 32/33 sent an embassy to the Han court under a chieftain" who styled himself "king for the first time" (Gardiner 1979: 67). Han W. K. attributes much more power to Koguryŏ in this first stage than does Gardiner: he sees the Koguryŏ as the agents of the fall of an earlier "Chinese colony" in the area in 128 bc and also states that Koguryŏ insurgency was responsible for the collapse of the original Xuantu commandery in 75 bc (1970: 38–9).

The second stage of Koguryŏ development (ad 12–207) covers the period after they established themselves independently of Han China in the Hun River drainage. They were reportedly unable to eke out a satisfactory living in the steep mountains and thus resorted to raids on surrounding tribes and lowland settlements (Sanguozhi, quoted in Gardiner 1964: 162). Some scholars postulate that by the mid-1st century ad the Koguryŏ may have advanced from periodic raiding to demanding regular tribute from their peninsular neighbors, thus dominating them politically and economically. Tribute is said to have been collected by "tribal nobles of [Koguryŏ] families [that] were stationed amongst the ... Okjo communities of northeastern Korea" (ibid.: 172). Koguryŏ attacked the Chinese commandaries of Xuantu and Liaodong in ad 105–6, 121 and 167–9, although it also sent tribute to the Chinese court in 109. Sohn et al. treat Koguryŏ as a coherent power from ad 12 when, they state, it "attacked Han China"; and the raiding activities of ad 47 they portray as Koguryŏ having again "attacked Han and advanced deep into mainland China to occupy Peking" (1970: 38–9).

The third stage (ad 207–45) begins with the removal of the Koguryŏ capital from the Hun River valley to the Yalu River valley near Mt. Wandu (K. Hwando) after retaliatory attacks by the Liaodong commandery. Their stone-walled capital city in the Donggou region of China's Jilin province has tentatively been identified by Japanese archaeologists near the modern town of Jian (Sekino et al. 1929). Koguryŏ may have sent tribute to the Chinese court in 220; but it again attacked the Xuantu commandery from this new base, and it also gained access to the sea by establishing dominance over the settlements at the mouth of the Yalu sometime before 233. In 238 an alliance between Koguryŏ and the Wei Dynasty effected the overthrow of the Liaodong commandery, which had been run as a warlord's fief for half a century (Gardiner 1972). But with Liaodong now occupied by Wei, Koguryŏ's plundering of the western edge of the Yalu estuary in 242 incited a Wei counterattack in 244. On this occasion, Wei reached and destroyed the Koguryŏ capital at Wandu and sent the king fleeing. This account of the routing of Koguryŏ (cf. Ikeuchi 1929) represents the end of direct Chinese information on Koguryŏ development until the 6th century, and the ethnography of Koguryŏ recorded in the Sanguozhi must depict the society as it was organized just prior to the invasion of 244–5.

The Sanguozhi and the Houhan-shu 21 suggest that Koguryŏ was undergoing moderate sinicization in the mid-2nd century. They describe the internal organization of Koguryŏ as consisting of five "tribes" (C. bu), including a royal one which supplied the kings and a formerly royal one which still retained the rights of the king's ancestral "tribe". Cross-cutting these "tribes" were two social classes, the "upper orders" (C. dajia) and the "lower orders" (C. xiagu). The upper orders did not work in the fields and ate at raised seats. Moreover, those of the royal tribe and the tribe supplying the queen held both the title of (C.) guzoujia – a translation equivalent of which might be "aristocrats" (cf. Shiratori 1926; Gardiner 1964) – and the rights to build ancestral temples and to worship the spirits of stars and the national gods of earth and harvest. Each tribe had its own officials, but these were of lower rank than the same officials for the royal tribe. In addition, there were six other ranks or titles: court minister, tribal elder, deputy tribal elder, keeper of records, clan head and assistant. Of all these various ranks and titles, three were of Chinese derivation; Gardiner (1979) notes that despite the existence of these terms, no native word for ruler has survived, suggesting that the ruler position was a Chinese creation for purposes of border administration.

Gardiner states that the greatest change in Koguryŏ structure during this time implied in the Sanguozhi is that of an increase in kingly power from primus inter pares to a central ruler no longer relying on a hierarchy of clan nobles but on a nexus of appointed officials subject to his word alone (1964: 200–2). Gardiner himself divides up the phases of Koguryŏ somewhat differently, seeing ad 12–100 as a transitional phase leading into the positive leadership which characterized Early Koguryŏ (100–245); such positive leadership came into being with the succession of the (K.) Kyeru tribe to the monarchy (1979: 80). In connection with questions of organization within Koguryŏ at this time, there is considerable scholarly disagreement whether the five Koguryŏ tribes (bu) were purely social groups or whether they also occupied specific territories (Gardiner 1964: 187, 1979: 75).

The fourth stage of Koguryŏ development (245 onwards) comprises Koguryŏ's re-establishment at Wandu and its gaining of enough strength to attack the Lelang commandery in ad 313. In that year the Lelang commander, named Zhang Tong, is said to have fled northwards with many families to the territory of a branch of the steppe nomads located in the southwestern Manchurian basin. There the steppe ruler "established a commandery of Lo-lang [in his own territory] for them, appointing T'ung [Zhang Tong] governor ..." (Gardiner 1969c: 571). This establishment of a second Lelang commandery is more important for understanding Paekche than Koguryŏ history [see Paekche section below] but is mentioned here in sequence nevertheless.

The year of 313 is often taken as the end of Chinese domination of the peninsula and the establishment of Koguryŏ suzerainty in the Taedong River basin. However, Gardiner makes a good case for the former Lelang area having continued after that date under the administration of a Chinese "governor", Dong Shou, even though he might not have remained in direct contact with the Chinese court (Gardiner 1969a: 41–2). It was not until Dong Shou's death in 357 that Gardiner suspects the former Lelang area's annexation to Koguryŏ. Ledyard (pers. comm.) on the other hand, sees Chinese control ending on the peninsula around 291 and the area of Lelang after Koguryŏ's attacks in 313 being contested between Koguryŏ and the Murong, a powerful group of steppe nomads who were responsible for the second sacking of the Koguryŏ capital on the Yalu in 341 (cf. Hatada 1969: 16). In any case, the area did not become fully integrated into the Koguryŏ polity until the removal of the capital in 427 from the Yalu to P'yŏngyang in the Taedong River basin (Lee K. B. 1984: 38; Han W. K. 1970: 47).

Gardiner implies that the internal organization of Koguryŏ in the 4th and 5th centuries was different from before the Wei conquest of 245, noting that the Wei "shattered the tributary system which had played a major role as the economic basis for Koguryŏ's power" (1969a: 34).
