Gardiner (1964: 428) has characterized the late 3rd century as one in which Koguryŏ was transformed:

from a Chinese border state, existing mainly by the plunder of the Chinese outposts in the north-east, to a kingdom centred in Korea proper, in which the formerly independent tribal communities of the ... Wo-chu [K. Okcho] and others had been merged.

He also pinpoints ad 300, when King Ulbul ascended the Koguryŏ throne, as the beginning of Koguryŏ florescence, entailing the "glorification of the royal house ..., a very high standard of cultural achievement" based on the adoption of the Puyŏ founding legend and the employment of Chinese immigrants at court (1979: 81).

Within this sequence of Koguryŏ development, one fact stands out. This is the avowedly complete routing of Koguryŏ by the Wei Dynasty in 244–5. The regrouping of Koguryŏ in the succeeding seventy years must have entailed enormous reorganization, especially in terms of its economic base. The mountainous area inhabited by the Koguryŏ was incapable of supporting large population densities without outside input. The traditional histories have described this input first in terms of Koguryŏ's raids on the Xuantu commandery (as if raids documented at ten- to twenty-year intervals could sustain a population!), and secondly in terms of tribute extraction from the occupants of the eastern peninsular coast. I have argued elsewhere that early in their history the Koguryŏ were most likely hunters, fishers and horsebreeders who depended on upland-lowland trading links with coastal agriculturalists to provide them with sufficient sustenance to stay in the upper mountain valleys (Barnes 1988b). The development of this symbiotic trade – with ponies and mountain products such as furs moving against agricultural produce from the coastal Okcho peoples – is probably what has been described as one-way agricultural tribute in the Chinese documents.

Once this symbiotic relationship with the Okcho was destroyed in 245, it was not resuscitated even though the Koguryŏ did re-emerge on the political scene in 313. Thus, the means of subsistence must have been reconstructed on a different basis than before. It is conceivable that Koguryŏ's interest in the Lelang territory was stimulated by the area's agricultural potential – consisting essentially of the coastal plains in the lower Taedong River basin. It should be remembered that Koguryŏ's major dealings had theretofore always been with the Xuantu commandery in Manchuria, not Lelang (Gardiner 1969b: 163); but their forays into the lower Yalu River valley might have been part of earlier searches for agricultural land – which in that case brought on their destruction by the Wei. The eventual colonization of the Taedong basin in 427 might have been the culmination of this interest in obtaining an agricultural base broader than what was available to the Okcho on the narrow plains of the east coast. And obtaining such a base is perhaps one of the main factors which led to the resurgence of Koguryŏ as a powerful force in peninsular history in the 5th and 6th centuries.

Another factor of potential importance in Koguryŏ state development was the expulsion in 286 of the Puyŏ peoples from the upper Manchurian basin by the Murong – the same branch of steppe nomads that later attacked the Koguryŏ capital in 341 (Schreiber 1949–55). The Puyŏ refugees have usually been cited as a crucial element only in Paekche state formation [see Paekche section below], but their possible relationship to Koguryŏ needs to be explored more fully. Chinese sources suggest that Puyŏ refugees migrated into the eastern peninsula after the Murong attack on their capital – thought to have been located in the Sungari River basin (Ikeuchi 1932) – seeking refuge with the Okcho peoples who had formed part of Koguryŏ's tribute/trading network prior to 245 (Gardiner 1969a: 71, 1969c). That the Koguryŏ and Puyŏ peoples were ethnically related is attested by statements in the Chinese chronicles to the effect that the two groups had similar languages and customs although differing in temperament and clothing (Gardiner 1964, 1979: 60). Despite their affinities, however, the Puyŏ and Koguryŏ are known to have been extremely hostile to each other. If Koguryŏ had still been powerful, the migration of Puyŏ peoples into Okcho lands probably could not have occurred. But after the Wei routing of Koguryŏ in 245, the Okcho were put under direct control of Lelang, which subsequently regulated local affairs through native chieftains. Since the Puyŏ and Chinese were old allies, the former's presence in Okcho territory was probably not viewed as a threat by Lelang.

Given the traditional hostilities between the Puyŏ and Koguryŏ, it is all the more curious that the Koguryŏ historically claimed descent from the Puyŏ and that the two groups shared a state-founding myth. The myth is first recorded in the Chinese chronicles and ascribed to Puyŏ (Ikeuchi 1932; Shiratori 1938); the Koguryŏ myth can be viewed as a transformed variant.

The Puyŏ myth concerns a man named Dongming (K. Tongmyŏng) who migrates in from a country further north and founds the country of Puyŏ; in the Koguryŏ version, the man is a Puyŏ youth named Chumong, who is urged by his mother to go south to her homeland and found his own country in order to avoid ostracization by his peers because of his miraculous birth from an egg. 22 Most scholars assume that the Koguryŏ founding myth relates to the settlement of the Hun River valley and is appropriately linked to the traditional founding date for Koguryŏ at 37 bc. Gardiner, however, believes that the Puyŏ myth was adopted only in the 4th century as part of the strategy in for glorifying the Koguryŏ royal family (Gardiner 1979, 1988).

The equation of archaeological materials with the various phases of Koguryŏ development is rendered difficult by inaccessibility of data and a lack of a secure archaeological chronology. Almost all references to Koguryŏ archaeological materials in Western-language sources are quite out of date or misleading (cf. Hatada 1969; Gardiner 1969). In a 1974 survey, Li O. presents data on Bronze Age archaeology in the northern peninsula and examines ethnographic references to Tungusic peoples called the Ye and Maek in search of Koguryŏ origins. Kim J. B., on the other hand, conforms more to the time scale for Koguryŏ known from the Chinese chronicles, attributing four pit-graves containing horse bits to the Koguryŏ at ad 0 (1978: 42). Koguryŏ archaeology is in great need of systematic elucidation (cf. Rhee 1992a, b), and Korean-language texts are beginning to address this need based on Chinese excavations (cf. Ch'oe MJ. 1982 [in Ref. List]).

The most pressing issue is the nature of early mounded tomb construction in Koguryŏ and its relation to the stratification of society. Stone cairns with internal (rather than underground) burials are thought to have been built from the 2nd century ad. These were later transformed into pyramids constructed of rectangular stone blocks and containing an elevated burial chamber. Much later, probably in the 5th century, earthen mounds with internal stone-block chambers began to be built on the Chinese pattern as known in the former Lelang area. Very few of any of these types of burials have yielded grave goods – most were looted long ago. Some iron horseiding equipment and gold ornaments are known to have come from tombs but are poorly dated. Without stricter chronological controls, it is impossible to elucidate changing status relations in Koguryŏ society; however, some insight on stratification can be gained from the mythological evidence as presented in the Discussion section below.

## **Samhan**

The Samhan, or Three Han, were described in the Sanguozbi as being situated south of the Daifang commandery on the Korean peninsula (cf. Ch'eon K. W. 1976 [in Ref. List]). These were Mahan, Bianhan (K. Pyŏnhan) and Chenhan (K. Chinhan). Their exact geographical relationship to each other was not specified in the text; but as related by Kim J. B. (1974: 5) the Samguk Sagi, a medieval Korean work, proposed that Mahan was situated in the north, Pyŏnhan in the southwest, and Chinhan in the southeast. Yi Dynasty scholars rejected this scheme, however, and it is now generally accepted that Mahan was located in the southwest, Pyŏnhan in the south, and Chinhan in the southeast \(Figure 1.5). Nevertheless, some scholars place Chinhan in the Han River basin (Lee K. B. 1984: 26; Sohn et al. 1970: 27).

Efforts to determine the locations of individual guo have been controversial and largely unsuccessful. For example, the guo called (K.) Mokchi has been hypothesized to have existed in modern Chiksan, Iksan, Kongju, Kwangju and even northern Korea (Kim J. B. 1974: 6). Despite these geographical problems, some population data is available to reflect on the sizes of both the guo and the Samhan themselves. Mahan is said in the Sanguozbi to have contained more than 100,000 households, with large guo having some 10,000 households each and small guo having a few thousand each. Kim J. B. (1974) has converted these data into a population estimate of more than 500,000 for Mahan, based on an assumed five-member family for the Bronze Age.

![](_page_56_Figure_0.jpeg)

**Figure 1.5** The Samhan and major sites in the Proto-Three Kingdoms period

The Sanguozhi states that the 3rd-century Samhan of the southern Korean peninsula were subdivided into guo; Mahan housed 54 guo; Pyŏnhan, centred on the Naktong River basin in the south, consisted of 12 guo; and Chinhan, wherever it was located, also had 12 guo. Several of the names of these guo and their "kings" (C. wang) are also known from the Sanguozhi. Most authors translate guo as "state" or "kingdom"; Gardiner cautions against this, calling them "tribal units" (1969a: 21), and Han W. K. also calls these entities "tribes" (K. pujok) (1970: 33). Kim J. B. relates that Mahan at least has traditionally been understood as "a tribal society or a league of tribal states" (1974: 4), but he rejects this interpretation because no clear definitions of these concepts have been given. The most comprehensive work on the archaeological evidence pertaining to the development of these gwo, regardless of how they have been defined, is by Yi H. (1984).

As for the internal organization of the guo, Lee K. B. (1984: 28) assumes that each had a walled-town centre and therefore calls them "walled-town states". However, the earliest walled sites currently known in the southern peninsula date to the end of the 3rd century ad, so that such an organizational basis cannot be assumed (Barnes 1988d see [Chapter 6, this volume]). Gardiner sees the guo of Mahan as "probably clan settlements, probably no more than large villages" (1969a: 43). Hatada likewise considers them to have been merely "a collection of a few villages whose inhabitants were bound together by blood ties", headed by chiefs who were tribal chieftains or "were appointed by Chinese officials"; he further perceives that there was no "fixed relationship between the chiefs and the village people" (1969: 11). This view of Hatada's is consistent with his understanding of the Han entities as politically underdeveloped due to interference by the Chinese commanderies in their internal affairs.

The internal organization of the Samhan guo focused on the ruler, called (K.) shinji in the larger polities and (K.) upch'a in the smaller polities (Lee K. B. 1984: 28) or (K.) shinji, kanji, upch'i, (Sohn et al. 1970: 30), or (K.) shinji (great chief) and (K.) upch'a, hopch'ik, punye, sarhae (lesser chiefs) (Hatada 1969: 11). Members of the ruling class are said to have worn silk garments and leather shoes instead of hemp clothes and straw sandals, and they decorated their bodies with necklaces and earrings (Han W. K. 1970: 36). In Mahan at least, priestly duties were entrusted to a second independent figure, interpreted as a shamanic figure surviving from earlier in the society's development (Sohn et al. 1970: 29). Lee K. B. calls this figure a "Heaven prince" (K. ch'ŏn'gun), one who contributed to the separation of political and religious power at that time (1984: 33). This priestly figure oversaw rites at a designated sanctuary or "political asylum" (Sohn et al. 1970: 29). Kim T. J. called a (K.) sodo, which is referred to as a "secondary capital" (Kim J. B. 1970: 29) or a "separate settlement" (Kim T. J. 1985a [in Ref. List]) but which Han W. K. describes as taking the form of "groves of trees" (1970: 36).

As for the nature of the Three Han themselves, nothing is very clear. One early Western scholar has proposed that there were at least dialectical differences between these groups (Hulbert 1895). The names, on the other hand, may have originally been geographical designations; but there is a tendency among historians to treat them as social or political entities. Hatada (1969: 10) refers to them as three branches of the "Han tribes", while Han W. K. calls them "tribal leagues" (1970: 33). Lee J. W. (1982a) treats each of the Samhan as a "league of small states". Han W. K. (1970: 33) extends Samhan political development back to the 3rd or 4th century bc and attributes it to the "chieftains of the southern tribes ... importing and adopting the bronze and iron cultures of the north as a means of consolidating their power". This brings his Samhan polities into contemporaneity with his postulated state of Chin, but he does not deal with this contradiction. Lee C. W. agrees with this postulated early existence of Samhan (Lee C. W. 1978: 36). Several historians find causes for southern social developments not only in the diffusion of metal-working but in the actual migration of peoples with experience in polity organization.23 Among the stimuli for movement cited are the Weiman usurpation of Chosŏn in 194 bc and Chosŏn's subsequent conquest by the Han Dynasty in 108 bc.

There is some suggestion of unification or agglomeration of the guo among the Three Han through time. Lee J. W. (1982b) believes that (K.) Saro began to subjugate the other Chinhan "states" as early as the middle 1st century ad [see Silla section below]; while Lee K. B. writes (1984: 29), without giving specific evidence, that:

the numerous walled-town states of the Samhan had entered into a confederated relationship in the third century ad – although to be sure the twelve states of the Pyŏnhan region at some point removed themselves from this association.

The Houhanshu, written later than but compiled mainly from the Sanguozhi, states that King Chin of Mokchi was the nominal ruler of Samhan (Lee K. B. 1984: 29). This statement, however, is rejected by Kim J. B., who suggests that King Chin probably ruled only Mahan at the most (1974: 60); and Lee K. B. adds the qualification that his rule was accomplished only by the "common consent of the Mahan people" (1984: 29). On the other hand, Gardiner emphasizes that the sources never refer to a "King of Mahan", only a "Lord of Mahan" who was appointed by the Chinese after ad 280 to collect tribute (1969a: 43). Nevertheless, in the 3rd century ad, Mahan reputedly attacked Wei-controlled Daifang; and Mahan and Chinhan then established contact with Wei's successor state Jin (Han W. K. 1970: 33). Kim J. B. proposes that Mahan itself was a "semi-state" since it had an "urbanized culture" and fought Paekche as an equal (Kim J. B. 1974). Iksan has been suggested as "the capital of Mahan during its declining days" (Yi P. D. quoted in Kim J. B. 1974: 6).

If the "divide and rule" policy followed by Lelang was successful, then perhaps we should not expect to see polity agglomeration until after the commanderies disappeared on the peninsula circa 313. In this respect, it is important to note that the Kaya confederation of states is said to have grown out of the Pyŏnhan polities, and the two states of Paekche and Silla which became powerful in the 4th and 5th centuries claimed to have developed from individual *guo* within Samhan.

It is significant on a broader scale to note that there were so many polities documented for the relatively small area of the southern Korean peninsula. This situation mirrors the organization of the western Japanese islands at the same time, for which the *Sanguozhi* also refers to the existence of over a hundred *guo*. In the case of Japan, the Chinese also documented the grouping of these *guo* into a larger confederation under the hegemony of one particular *guo* called Yamatai (cf. Tsunoda & Goodrich 1951). Complex social development on the Korean peninsula was thus part of a larger regional process that also included the Japanese islands (Barnes 1986, 1988c).

Much of the stimulus for such development has been seen in economic terms (Barnes 1992 [see Chapter 3, this volume]). The *Weizhi* specifies that the Samhan *guo* produced iron which was traded to Lelang and Japan, and archaeological work gives credence to these documentary claims. Ironworking sites of the 1st and 2nd centuries ad are known from excavations at Majang-ri24 and Chungdo. And many shellmound sites yielding evidence of iron-working such as Kimhae (Hamada & Umehara 1923 [in Ref. List]) and Chodo (National Museum of Korea 1976 [in Ref. List]) dot the southeastern Korean coast around Pusan. On balance, importation of Chinese goods from the Chinese commanderies into the Samhan is apparent at the recent Choyang-dong and Taho-ri excavations [as mentioned above in the Lelang section].

The similarities of the names Samhan (Three Han), described in the Chinese sources, and the Samguk (Three Kingdoms), described in the Samguk Sagi and Samguk Yusa, caused Yi Dynasty scholars to equate the two developmentally, saying that the Three Kingdoms of Koguryŏ, Paekche and Silla emerged from the Samhan: Koguryŏ from Mahan, Paekche from Pyŏnhan, and Silla from Chinhan (Kim J. B. 1974: 5). These conclusions are now recognized to be totally erroneous, but there is a corresponding trend among present-day scholars to derive the early state societies of the southern peninsula from the Three Han – to wit: Paekche from Mahan, Silla from Chinhan, and the Kaya confederation from Pyŏnhan. This trend means that the Samhan data are often incorporated into the developmental sequences of the later state societies, being treated in a teleological sense as early manifestations of the state. Whether this is an appropriate approach is examined in the Discussion below.

## **Paekche**

The state of Paekche occupied the southwestern portion of the peninsula until ad 660, when it was defeated by an alliance between its eastern neighbor, Silla and Tang China. Its first capital is commonly recognized as having been at (K.) Hansŏng, where Buddhism was allegedly received in 384. 26 Hansŏng, meaning "Han walled site", might refer to one among several walled sites known in the lower Han River basin near present-day Seoul (Barnes 1988d, 1991 see [Chapter 6, this volume]), but it is not clear which one. As Koguryŏ expanded in the 5th century, however, Paekche was forced to move its capital southwards \(Figure 1.6): to Ungjin (modern Kongju) in 474 and then in 538 to Sabi (modern Puyŏ – not to be confused with the name of the Manchurian polity/ethnic group). The 6th century marked Paekche's cultural and political florescence, with contacts to China and Central Asia. 27

Lee C. W. (1978: 35) sees two schools of thought concerning the formation of Paekche: archaeologically-minded scholars date Paekche presence to the 1st century bc, based on settlement and iron-working remains in the Han River basin; but certain historians pinpoint the political reforms of King Koi (r. ad 234–85) as marking the establishment of the Paekche state proper. 29 A third opinion declares that the "structuring of Paekche into a centralized, aristocratic state appears to have been completed in the reign of King Kŭn (346–75)" (Lee K. B. 1984: 37). History records that at the end of King Kun's reign, a Chinese scholar introduced the Chinese writing system to Paekche on which a developing bureaucracy could be based (Inoue 1989: 33; Renondeau 1959).


**Figure 1.6** Hypothesized Puyŏ movements relating to Paekche state formation between ad 286 and 352

All the above views, however, accept and reinforce the traditional Korean account of Paekche state formation given in the 12th-century Samguk Sagi (cf. Lee J. W. 1976, 1977 [both in Ref. List]). The Samguk Sagi records that Paekche developed out of one of the Mahan guo called (K.) Shipche (Lee C. W. 1978), although another of Mahan's 54 guo called (C.) Boji in the Sanguozhi is also a candidate. Boji, Gardiner declares convincingly, is "clearly a form of Pai-chi, i.e. Paekche" (1969a: 43). Other scholars have argued that "Kudara" is the original name of Paekche, as it is known in Japan (cf. Watson 1959: 83; Anselmo 1974). The Samguk Sagi, in the Paekche annals (cf. Chabanol 1990) provides a king list back to 18 bc and a description of the reigns. That these kingly records are not infallible, however, is shown by Best (1979) and Gardiner (1969c); and Best (1996) has further undertaken re-datings that put many of Paekche's major activities later in time than recorded in the Samguk Sagi [see Appendix 1A].

According to Paekche tradition, recorded in several Chinese and Korean historical sources, Paekche kings claimed descent from the royal house of Puyŏ – as did the Koguryŏ (Gardiner 1969c; Kim C. W. 1966: 4). This contention is argued by Choi H. W. (1994: 34) as supported through animaltotem ruling titles among the Paekche as among the Puyŏ. Han W. K. (1970: 43) even ascribes the name Paekche to "a northern tribe which migrated south, probably one of the Puyŏ tribes". However, the Paekche founding myth recorded in the Samguk Sagi states that the founders were two sons of the first king of Koguryŏ; according to legend, these youths, named (K.) Onjo and (K.) Piryu, fled to the southern Korean peninsula and were given land there in 18 bc by the "King of Mahan" (Gardiner 1969a: 43). The change of ethnic affiliation from Puyŏ to Koguryŏ in the Samguk Sagi is attributed by Gardiner to a lapse of memory over the intervening centuries, but there may be more to the problem than this, as we shall see below.

Han W. K. (1970: 43), Lee C. W. (1978: 38) and Sohn et al. (1970: 38) accept this migration of Onjo and Piryu and the 1st century bc date of Paekche emergence. As stated above, however, Gardiner rejects the account by pointing out that there never was such a person as "King of Mahan"; so if the brothers received land from anyone, it must have been from the Mahan lord appointed in ad 280 to collect taxes for the Chinese. By Gardiner's reasoning, any migration involving the Lord of Mahan must have occurred after this date (1969c: 573); and Shiratori (1947) had long ago suggested that it took place after the Puyŏ defeat of 286. Ledyard (pers. comm.) concurs on the grounds that the Chinese never mentioned the presence of any Puyŏrelated peoples south of the commanderies; any such presence would hardly have escaped their attention, for the Puyŏ were great allies of the Chinese. Perhaps more to the point is that the Samguk Yusa quotes the Houhansbu in describing the ceding of land by Mahan to refugees to the Chinese state of Qin – not Puyŏ or Koguryŏ (Ha & Mintz 1972: 48). This could be one source of the Paekche founding myth components.

Nevertheless, if there was a Puyŏ migration via Okcho lands [as described above in the Koguryŏ section], it would have been doubly possible if Chinese influence was indeed on the wane after 290. The reason the Puyŏ left the Okcho for the Han River basin is merely given as the resurgence of Koguryŏ power (Gardiner 1969c: 585). In line with this argument, Henthorn (1979: 21) unabashedly dates the migration of the Puyŏ clan to the Han basin to 285 ad. If a 3rd-century date is accepted, how do we view the traditional 18 bc founding date of Paekche?

The name Piryu appears in various historical sources in association with at least three possibly distinct figures with different chronological implications. Firstly, Onjo and Piryu are portrayed as sons of the Koguryŏ founder Chumo (K. Chumong); Gardiner (1969c) has presented very convincing evidence that the Paekche founding date was fixed as early as it was merely to allow the linkage between these personages. Secondly, the eleventh king of Paekche is named King Piryu; Gardiner identifies this Piryu as probably the true founder of the Paekche state (1969a: 45). King Piryu is also known as the father of the first king of Paekche who is historically attested in the Chinese and Japanese chronicles; and he may be identical with the Piryu said (in only one source) to be a direct descendant of the royal family of Puyŏ. Gardiner (1969c) proposes that it may have been his name that was borrowed to create the traditional founding myth, and at the same time, that the reigns of his next two descendants were duplicated earlier in the imperial genealogy as fillers.

In addition to the Puyŏ migration of 286, Ledyard suggests documentary evidence that there was a second, later Puyŏ migration which he believes was more instrumental in the establishment of the Paekche state. 30 He notes the fate of a King Hyŏn of Puyŏ who is stated in the Sanguozhi and Houhanshu to have been captured in Murong raids at an unspecified date, taken back to Murong territory in the southwestern Manchurian basin, invested with the title of General and given the chief's daughter in marriage. In 352 this former Puyŏ king, now a Murong general, was apparently sent to be a governor of an area in eastern Manchuria recently taken over by the Murong. It is after this date, in the 350s, that Ledyard postulates a southward movement of Puyŏ peoples from the General's territory into the Han River basin, where they would ostensibly have met the Puyŏ of the first migration via Okcho lands. Ledyard notes that the first appearance of the name Paekche is in the Chinese chronicles associated with the land held by the Murong west of the Liao River in Manchuria – not in the Han River basin – and so dovetails nicely with the story of the captured Puyŏ king.

That such a second migration could have occurred in the mid-4th century could have been due to the weakening of the Koguryŏ after the Murong attack on their Yalu capital in 341. Nevertheless, by 355, Koguryŏ is said to have made peace with the Murong; and in 369 it began its attacks on Paekche, finally causing Paekche to move its capital southwards out of the Han basin in 474 (Gardiner 1969a: 46, 71). That Paekche was able to do this without severe disruption was due to the fact that it had completed its conquest and incorporation of Mahan between 366–9, dates which are attested in the Japanese chronicles but which are given as ad 6–9 in the Samguk Sagi.

This research by Gardiner and Ledyard is very forceful and brings us to several possible views of Paekche state formation, each of which emphasizes continuity or discontinuity of certain elements: the Paekche name, Puyŏ identity, and polity existence. The various combinations of these elements in continuous or discontinuous forms give us different scenarios of state formation as follows:

1. The traditional view espoused by most Korean historians and archaeologists, which postulates the lst-century bc founding of Paekche (as Boji?) incorporating Puyŏ descent/affinity from the founding myth. This view thus implies early appearance and continuity of the Paekche name, Puyŏ identity and polity existence.

- 2. The traditional but modified view postulating the lst-century bc founding of Paekche (as perhaps Boji?), but the incorporation of Puyŏ elite only in the late 3rd or early 4th century ad. Hatada (1969: 19) provides a historical context for the Puyŏ affiliation by speculating that Paekche had been so weakened by tributary relations with the Chinese commanderies that it was necessary for it "to unify the Mahan through an outside tribe. ... [Thus] a branch of the royal clan of Puyŏ was made the royal house of Paekche". This version makes the Puyŏ not the founders of Paekche but the nucleus of a new dynasty which effected changes in the polity's organization. Even if actual Puyŏ people were not incorporated into the ruling group, Kirkland offers that a claim of Puyŏ affiliation could have been made by Paekche elite specifically to intimidate Koguryŏ during their mutual border conflicts (1981: 121).
- 3. The reactionary view which rejects the possible existence of Paekche before the 4th century ad by disassociating it with Boji and by postulating that Paekche came into being only with the Puyŏ (doublepronged?)migration(s) into the Han River basin in 286 and 352. This view argues for discontinuity in all three elements of Paekche existence, and it assumes a virtual vacuum in the Han River basin during the existence of the Chinese commandery, Daifang, established shortly before 220 by the warlord from Liaodong (Ikeuchi 1930).

In archaeological terms it looks as if the latter scenario is most appropriate. There are very few Proto-Three Kingdoms remains in the Seoul region; the most notable are the dwelling sites of Majang-ri and Chungdo, which have produced evidence of iron-working [see Samhan section above]. Some archaeologists equate these with the early Paekche state, but evidence for social stratification is available only from the 4th century onwards in the form of elaborately structured terraced stone tombs of the Koguryŏ tradition. These are contemporaneous with the recently excavated 4th-century walled site of Mongch'on (cf. Seoul National University Museum 1975, 1986 both [in Ref. List]) – the earliest site yielding elite remains, located in the Seoul Olympic Park (Barnes 1988d see [Chapter 6, this volume]).

The northern affinities of the stepped stone tombs in the Han River basin fit in well with the legendary derivation of the Paekche elite from Koguryŏ or Puyŏ stock. Whether the similarities are emulative or genetic in nature, however, is a matter for future research. The archaeology of the Sungari River basin in Manchuria is crucial in this endeavor (Pak YJ. 1996), for three-way comparisons must be carried out between Puyŏ, Koguryŏ and Paekche material remains to shed light on the relationships suggested in the founding myths.

#### The mature Paekche state and material culture

As with all Three Kingdoms polities, mounded tombs provide copious information on the material culture and status systems of the early state (Kim W. Y. 1961). King Muryŏng's 6th-century tomb was one of the few in Korea to have been discovered and excavated intact.31 The Paekche capitals have been mapped out in art historical and urban studies.32 Buddhism in Paekche (Lancaster & Yu 1989) is a well-researched topic, particularly from the art historical point of view. Temples and stone Buddha sculptures have received considerable attention,33 ceramics and metalwork somewhat less.34 Finally, the relationship between Paekche and the Yamato state (in the Japanese islands) has engendered much, often controversial, research. These studies concentrate on two areas: 1) political relations including the 4th-century Yamato-Paekche alliance and the 5th-century "horsender theory" proposing the Paekche conquest of Yamato;35 and 2) the transmission of Buddhism from Paekche to Yamato and the Paekche contribution to Buddhist material culture in Japan.36

#### **Kaya**

Kaya is the collective name for a federation (*nyŏnbang* or *nyŏnmaengcbe*) (Grayson 1976; Kim T. S. 1987 [in Ref. List]) of small states or a "six-tribal union"37 in the Naktong River basin of the southeastern peninsula (Figure 1.7). Contemporaneous with the other three great Korean kingdoms of Koguryŏ, Silla and Paekche, Kaya is believed not to have consolidated itself into one great state before it was overtaken in ad 532 by Silla. Six small "states" comprised the federation, for which a founding date of 42 ad is usually given; these were: (K.) Kŭmkwan-Kaya (or Pon-Kaya); Tae-Kaya;

So-Kaya; Sŏngsan-Kaya; and Konyong-Kaya. Of these Pon-Kaya (Main Kaya) in the lower Naktong basin and Tae-Kaya (Great Kaya) in the middle Naktong basin are identified as the most powerful by modern scholars (Sohn et al. 1970: 41; Han W. K. 1970: 34).

Historical sources differ on what preceded the Kaya "states" in the region. The Karak Kukki, a non-extant 11th-century document (cf. Ha & Mintz 1971: 158) records names of nine chiefs for the "pre-state" period, but other sources often describe the states themselves as having developed from the earlier guo of Pyŏnhan, the south central member of the Three Han. Some of what are believed to be the royal tombs of Tae-Kaya have been excavated in the upper Naktong River basin (Grayson 1985b; Chabanol 1991). Kim W. Y. (1983a) uses the scale of these mounded tombs with their supposed mass interments to attribute considerable wealth and power to the royal family; these attributes, he posits, were due to the presence of "rich iron ore" and "fertile land suitable for rice cultivation" in the middle Naktong River basin Beyond these statements, there is little exploration of Kaya origins see [Chapter 7, this volume]. However, the Samguk Yusa states that King Suro, the ostensible first ruler of the Kaya Kingdom, married an Indian princess (Ha & Mintz 1972) – a theme recently taken up by Kim B. M. (1988 [in Ref. List]).


**Figure 1.7** The Kaya region in the mid-5th century

In contrast to the paucity of research on Kaya's development, much has been made of the claim in the Japanese Nihon Sboki chronicles (cf. Aston 1896; Kim J. G. 1987 [in Ref. List]) that an outpost of the Japanese Yamato government, called "Mimana Nihonfu" [fu = government headquarters], was established in the Mimana (K. Imna) region [location unknown] of Kaya territory in the 4th century. This 8th-century account of Mimana has generally been interpreted in terms of the presence of a Japanese colony on the southern Korean peninsula between the 4th and 7th centuries ad (Suematsu 1958; Hatada 1969: 16, 22), and it was used as partial justification for Japan's annexation of Korea in 1910 (cf. Grayson 1977)!

The Korean scholarly reaction has traditionally been to ignore the Japanese claims and deny the historicity of Mimana altogether. Those Koreanists who deign to discuss the problem usually take a rejectionist view. Chŏn K. U. (1974) acknowledges the existence of a place known as Mimana on the Korean peninsula but identifies it with the Pyŏnhan guo of Mioyama. He further opines that the documented raiding, conquest and occupation of the Mimana area originally represented Paekche intervention in that area and that these records were redefined in the Nihon Shoki as Yamato activities.

Grayson (1977: 66) dismisses the recorded attacks from Japan (Wa) on the peninsular societies as mere small-scale incidents of piracy "reminiscent of the ... raids conducted by the Vikings on early mediaeval Europe"; he supports instead Egami's suggestion that colonization actually occurred in the opposite direction, i.e. from the peninsula to the islands (Egami 1964; Ledyard 1975). Grayson ends with a plea "that Mimana, that affront to the Korean people and their history, be removed once and for all from serious historical consideration" (1977: 68).

Among select groups of Japanese and Korean scholars, however, there has been a tentative rapprochement resulting in a more flexible view of the Mimana problem. In brief, it is now generally accepted that the Nihon Shoki references to Mimana Nihonfu are based on a no longer extant text called the Paekche Pon'gi (known as the Kudara Hongi in Japanese), which formed one of the sources of the Nihon Shoki compilers. In the original Paekche text, written from a Paekche perspective whether on the Korean peninsula or by Paekche scribes at the Yamato court, the name of the headquarters was Mimana Wafu – "Wa" being the common continental designation for Japan at the time. This name was apparently changed by the Nihon Shoki compilers to Mimana Nihonfu – an anachronism at the time of substitution since the name Nihon was not adopted for Japan until the 7th century and it was being applied to something that was supposed to have existed between the 4th and 7th centuries.

Given the claimed Paekche derivation of the Mimana records, Mimana Wafu is now acknowledged by many scholars as a real, rather than a fictitious, entity. Still, considerable disagreement remains concerning to whom this headquarters belonged and how it functioned. For example, Inoue proposed that it belonged to the local aristocracy of the Wa peoples, who are said in Chinese documents to have inhabited both sides of the Korea Straits (Inoue 1973); Kit? (1976 [in Ref. List]) agrees and goes further to state that it disappeared with the emergence of Kaya in the 5th century. Okuda postulates that it was an international facility within Kaya territory manned by delegates from Paekche, Silla and Yamato for official communication among these states (Okuda 1976 [in Ref. List]); and Kim H. G. (1987) argues that Mimana was a Paekche outpost where Yamato troops were stationed at Paekche's request. Whatever its affiliation, Mimana Wafu ceased to exist if not before, then certainly after, the Silla conquest and incorporation of Pon-Kaya in 532 and Tae-Kaya in 562. And it was this disappearance of Mimana Wafu that the Nihon Shoki chroniclers portrayed in the 8th century as Nihon's "loss" – when, in fact, it might never have belonged to Japan, much less Nihon. Their purpose was simple: to provide a pretext for "reviving" Mimana for taxation purposes. Needless to say, many Japanese scholars still hold to the view inscribed in the Nihon Shoki as the proper historical one.

The problem of Mimana underscores the strategic importance of the Naktong River valley for the emerging states of Paekche, Silla and Yamato in the 5th century ad. It is interesting that this area so rich in productive resources, and such a powerful commercial centre in the 3rd and 4th centuries [see Chapters 3 and 5, this volume], was unable to achieve regional unification but remained fragmented into the various Kaya polities until it was overrun first by Paekche and then Silla in rapid succession (Lee K. B. 1984: 41). The tug-of-war over the region by surrounding polities must have contributed to this fragmentation. From archaeological remains, especially the recent excavations of the Pokch'ŏn-dong graves yielding much armour and horse equipment (Chŏng & Shin 1982–3 [in Ref. List]; Barnes 1994 see [Chapter 5, this volume]), it is known that material connections with Japan were especially close in the 5th century.

Kim T. S., in the most recent review of the history of Kaya research (1988 [in Ref. List]), deplores past studies which consider Kaya history only in terms of Mimana or Paekche territorial structure. He then offers a new interpretation of the textual and archaeological sources by dividing Kaya history into Early and Late phases. Early Kaya (1st to 4th centuries), centred on Pon-Kaya, is proposed to equate with the Pyŏnhan guo mentioned in the Chinese sources together with the shellmound and iron production remains on the southern coast. Late Kaya (5th to 6th centuries) is equated with the mounded tomb remains of the interior Naktong River valley (Kim T. S. 1988). Kim sees a shift in power and territorial importance between these two phases, thus giving considerable time depth to the importance assigned to these two Kaya states and bringing Kaya studies into temporal conflict with traditional renditions of Samhan political development.

Increasing archaeological excavation in the Kaya region of both tomb (Anon. 1986; Nishitani 1993) and non-tomb (Shim P. G. 1982) remains are also allowing the construction of an independent picture of Kaya society, politics, trade and technological development [see Chapters 3, 4 and 5, this volume]. 38

## **Silla**

Silla developed in the Kyŏngju Basin in the southeastern corner of the peninsula (Figure 1.1). Scholars are fairly unanimous in accepting the traditional account of Silla origins recorded in the Samguk Sagi, according to which Silla grew out of (K.) Saro, one of the twelve Chinhan polities. Kim W. Y. (1982: 26) further specifies the emergence of the state as dating from the middle 4th century, when the Kim clan became the hereditary rulers of the polity. Han W. K. (1970: 44) and Lee K. B. (1984: 40) narrow it even further to the ruler Naemul (r. 356–402) of the Kim clan, who took the new title (K.) maripkan and initiated hereditary rulership; Han W. K. (1970: 45) states that the actual name Silla was not adopted for the kingdom until the reign of King Chijung (r. 500–13), when the title of the ruler was again changed – this time to the Chinese wang – and the Chinese system of reigndates was adopted (Kim C. W. 1966: 4). But Gardiner notes that tribute was being received at the Chinese court from Xinluo (K. Silla) at the end of the 4th century (1966: 89).

The name Saro apparently applied to one or all of any three different entities: the political unit which preceded Silla, the region this unit occupied, and its capital city or centre. Saro is proposed by Kim W. Y. (1982: 27) to have been an abbreviation of a longer name, Sŏnabŏl/Sŏrabŏl, which he interprets to have meant "ox plain" in reference to the area around modern Kyŏngju city – considered to be the heart of the polity. Kim further suggests that the modern word Seoul, now the name of the capital city of the Republic of Korea, derives from Saro, which at some point came to mean "capital". Other scholars propose that the name Saro came from the word soe ("metal") since iron as well as gold was extremely important to Silla (Kim W. Y. 1986a: 245); indeed gold artifacts such as crown and earrings, pendants and gilt bronze horse trappings are distinctive of Silla culture (Anon. 1973; Bailey

1994; Ito 1974). Two other names that are often associated with the Kyŏngju region are Kŭmsŏng and Kyerim (cf. Sohn et al. 1970; Haguenauer 1928), the former also referring to gold – or at least metal.

Saro is further described, however, as a "tribal village" or "village state" by Kim W. Y. (1982: 26); as a "walled-town state" by Lee K. B. (1984: 40); as a "tribal union of clans" by Hatada (1969: 20); or as a "tribe" by Han W. K. (1970: 35). It is said that this socio-political unit was composed of "six villages ... each of which had its own ancestors" (Hatada 1969: 21). The social grouping based in each village is variously called tribe, family, clan or lineage by the above authors, and their six names survive in the Samguk Sagi as Kŭmnyang, Saryang, Ponp'i, Moryang, Hanji, and Suppi (Lee K. B. 1984: 28). It is not clear if these names apply to the villages or to the postulated descent groups. Saro apparently came into being through the voluntary union of these six descent-group/villages by the election of a common leader. The man chosen to head Saro was Pak Hyŏkkŏse, a member of Kŭmnyang, while his "queen", Aryŏng, was a member of Saryang (Lee K. B. 1984: 29). Three of the descent groups, which came to be known as the Pak, Sŏk and Kim clans, thereafter rotated the leadership of Saro among themselves. Han W. K. states furthermore that the Kim and Pak families were refugees from the east and north (Han W. K. 1970: 35); and it is to be noted that the founder of Saro, Hyŏkkŏse, and the founder also of the Sŏk clan, are said to have been born from eggs (Kim W. Y. 1982: 27).

Although most authors assign Saro development to the Proto-Three Kingdoms period because of the attested connection with Chinhan, Lee J. W. (1982b) gives it a much longer lifespan by dating the six villages, which were allegedly unified to form Saro, back to the 7th century bc. He divides the ensuing centuries into five hypothetical stages of Saro/Silla development, the first of which is that of chiefly society. Each of the six Saro "villages" is designated as a chiefdom comprised of a conical clan; and each clan lineage is postulated to have occupied different hamlets and burial grounds – the latter of which he identifies as Bronze Age dolmen clusters.

In the 2nd century bc (stage two), Lee postulates (1982: 272), these village chiefdoms received many immigrants:

mainly from the north-eastern part of China, ancient Chosŏn and Weiman Chosŏn. ... They brought in and made iron weapons and farm implements, and they rode horses. They also had

"Pak, Sŏk and Kim clans emerged" from among these immigrants and "grew enough to establish the Saro state by unifying the six [villages]" (ibid.: 275).

Stage three was marked by the emergence of the Saro state and its ascendance to the leadership of the Chinhan league from the middle 1st century bc. Through engaging in long-distance trade with Lelang, Saro mediated trade and other relations among the other Chinhan "states" (guo). Beginning in the mid-1st century ad, however, Saro began to subjugate its fellow Chinhan polities, making them "feudal" vassal states during stage four. In stage five, at the end of the 3rd century ad, Saro made each conquered "state" a "prefecture" in its own centralized administration system.

Lee's proposed five-stage scheme of development derives its chronology from the entries in the Korean histories, the Samguk Sagi and Samguk Yusa, leaning especially heavily on the the idea of immigrants from the Han conquest of Chosŏn in 108 bc. His scheme ends just before the advent of the mounded tomb culture in the Kyŏngju Basin (4th century ad). This telescopic chronology, however, is not necessarily warranted by the data, especially since another source of immigrants, if needed, can be found in the fall of Lelang to Koguryŏ in ad 313. Silla tombs and elite material culture are known to have slight Koguryŏ affiliations – although founding myths are not shared between these two polities as between Koguryŏ and Paekche – so there may have been some transfer of communication, people or organizational principles as well as craft technology from the late 4th century onwards. Silla mounded tomb culture is the latest to have developed on the peninsula, dated by some as beginning in the early 5th century (cf. Pearson 1985); thus the archaeological evidence for social stratification postdates Lee's postulated emergence of Saro by a full five centuries (Pearson et al. 1989). Archaeologically, Silla cannot be distinguished from Kaya in its early stages of development see [Chapter 8, this volume].

In the middle of the 5th century, occult Buddhism was first introduced to Silla by four monkish missionaries, but the cult was resisted by the aristocracy. 39 The beginning of the 6th century, however, saw many reforms along Chinese lines, with Silla adopted as the official name of the country, the Chinese title of wang (king) introducted, and the reign-year system adopted. By mid-century, a new capital near the old walled capital site began to be laid out following the Chinese gid-city pattern, modeled on Chang'an (Kim C. W. 1966: 5; Barnes 1988d, 1991 [see Chapter 6, this volume]). Perhaps these changes helped breakdown the resistence to foreign influences since in 527, King Pŏphŭng officially instituted Buddhism as the state religion (Kim J. M. 1992; Mc Clung 1978; Rhi K. Y. 1989); Lee K. B. (1989) recalculates the official date as 535. From this time onwards, Silla pursued expansionist policies, gradually absorbing the Kaya polities (Pon-Kaya in 532 and Tae-Kaya in 562) and occupying the Han River basin in the mid-6th century (Figure 1.8). Conversely, in the late 6th and early 7th centuries, it suffered hostile intentions from Paekche and Yamato in the Japanese archipelago (Wong 1980). Finally, an alliance with Tang China enabled Silla to conquer Paekche in 660 and Koguryŏ in 668 (cf. Jamieson 1969, 1970), finally unifying the Korean peninsula below the Taedong River into one state, United Silla, 40 which lasted until 935.

![](_page_75_Figure_0.jpeg)

**Figure 1.8** Expansion of Silla from its capital at Kŭmsŏng, 6th and 7th centuries ad (after Sohn et al. 1970)

Much of the early historical research on Silla has concerned royal genealogies (cf. Kim T. J. 1983), and kingly matters continue to be of interest to scholars (Li O. 1986; Rodgers 1960; Grayson 1976). An important exercise in redating some of the events in Silla history has brought the relationships between Silla and Paekche especially into focus (Best 1996); the chronological chart in Appendix 1A incorporates Best's redatings. And new inscriptional material has been discovered (Sasse 1991). Detailed work on organizational aspects of mature Silla society and politics have been undertaken by numerous scholars. 41 New areas of research are gender studies and the use of archaeological materials to reconstruct status hierarchies (Nelson 1991, 1993; Pearson et al. 1989).

The material remains of the Old Silla (of the Three Kingdoms, 300–668) and United Silla (668–935) periods in Kyŏngju are especially well represented in the literature. 42 Particularly popular subjects have been the ancient observatory and Silla astronomy 43 and Buddhist relics and monuments. 44 Silla pottery has also attracted much art historical attention.45 Of course, the spectacular tombs of Silla have also been rather extensively presented, 46 and the Kyŏngju capital area itself is the subject of many discussions and guidebooks, often beautifully photographed (Adams 1979, 1991; Ban Y. H. 1995; Kim C. 1966).

The United Silla period came to an end in the early 10th century (Unruh 1975) with the establishment of the Koryŏ Dynasty (935–1392). However, the northern part of the peninsula, which had belonged to Koguryŏ before unification by Silla, was not incorporated into Koryŏ at this time but became the separate state of Parhae (Figure 1.9) which consisted of a ruling class of Koguryŏ refugee aristocrats and commoners of Malgal extraction.47

## **Discussion**

The foregoing sections have reviewed the current variety of opinion concerning state formation on the Korean peninsula. Given the different interests and expectations of scholars from many countries and disciplines, we should not expect agreement on such issues as the definition, timing and origins of the various peninsular states. The views of these scholars must stand as given, possibly never capable of complete synthesis.

Nevertheless, there does seem to be a consensus in the usage of the term "state" in the Korean literature which differs fundamentally from the way it is used in anthropological archaeology. It is applied by most Korean authors to politically centralized entities mentioned as guo in historical documents. Since many of these guo existed prior to the mounded tomb evidence for social stratification, it can be proposed that the Korean use of "state" includes the commonly recognized though ill-defined anthropological categories of both "chiefdom" and "state" (cf. Earle 1977 [in Ref. List]). The term "chiefdom" is reserved in Korean studies primarily to denote those potentially centralized but anonymous entities recognizable only through archaeological remains (e.g. Bronze Age dolmen/cist burials) by authors who have had anthropological training abroad (cf. Choi M. L. 1984; Rhee S. N. 1984); such entities are usually called "tribal societies" by traditional historians.

## ![](_page_78_Figure_0.jpeg)

**Figure 1.9** United Silla and Parhae territories and regional capitals in the 8th century ad (after Han W. K. 1970)

One notable result of the above use of the term "state" is an increasing tendency among Korean scholars to view all political development subsequent to the establishment of the Lelang commandery in 108 bc in terms of the strong states extant in the 5th and 6th centuries ad. Not only is such development seen ideologically to culminate in these states, but the names of the states are projected backwards in time to encompass the formative stages. Thus we have the anomalous situation wherein Samhan history is co-opted under the histories of the mature Paekche, Silla and Kaya states. This trend can be constrasted with other approaches by non-Korean scholars such as Inoue who examines the formation of the "primitive states" (J. genshi kokka) of Samhan and early Koguryŏ as separate from the Three Kingdoms (Inoue 1981 [in Ref. List]).

Such co-option of the Samhan data by Korean authors begs the question of whether the centralized entities of the Proto-Three Kingdoms period are organizationally continuous with or even related to the large states of the Three Kingdoms period. In positive terms, by dealing with the evidence in such a holistic way, Korean scholars are able to avoid the definitional morass that plagues Western studies in distinguishing between chiefdom and state. In negative terms, however, the emphasis on coherence and continuity obscures a significant and potentially disruptive aspect of peninsular sociopolitical development that occurs in the late 3rd or early 4th century ad. This is the phenomenon of social stratification as seen in the advent of the mounded tomb culture.

Social stratification as a concept is not without its own problems of definition (cf. Barnes 1983b [in Ref. List]), and there is conflict of use among scholars of Korean prehistory. Although Nelson (1985, 1987) sidesteps the issue altogether in her assessments of peninsular burial customs, both Rhee S. N. (1984) and Choi M. L. (1984) view the dolmen and cist burials of the southwestern peninsula in the Late Bronze Age as evidence of stratification in chiefly societies. I would prefer to reserve the term stratification to mean the division of society into clear status categories of elites and commoners, with the former manifested in the archaeological record through monumental tomb construction (mounded tombs) and a sumptuary, elite, prestige-goods sub-culture. In any case, if incipient stratification occurred among Late Bronze Age chiefdoms, the process was most likely truncated by the establishment of the Chinese commanderies in 108 bc. Indeed, neither Choi nor Rhee claims that the Three Kingdomsperiod states grew out of these chiefdoms, even though the traditional founding dates of the major states do fall at the end of the Late Bronze Age.

The disjunction between the traditional founding dates of the Three Kingdoms of Koguryŏ, Silla and Paekche and the first material evidence of their elite sub-culture in the form of mounded tomb construction continues to be the greatest problem in assessing state formation on the peninsula. However, the point I would like to make in this Discussion is that this disjunction may be illusory: there is considerable evidence that the founding of the Three Kingdoms did not occur in the 1st century bc but four centuries later. The very same founding myths cited by Koreanists to illustrate the longevity of the peninsular "states" (Obayashi 1984) can be used in alternative ways to support a quite different claim: that the late 3rd century ad entailed a hiatus in development which facilitated social stratification and political reorganization leading to the emergence of the Three Kingdoms soon thereafter. The fact that this mythological evidence dovetails with the mounded tomb evidence strengthens the proposition for late state formation. Let us consider how this is so.

Gardiner himself postulated a late 3rd-century date for Koguryŏ's adoption of the Puyŏ founding myth, and he specified the historical context allowing it: the meeting of refugee Koguryŏ and Puyŏ leaders in the northeastern peninsula after their respective defeats and expulsions from their territories in 245 and 286. The Koguryŏ king list further supports a postulated break and reformulation at this point by starting in 300 a new dynasty with tenuous links to past Koguryŏ leaders after what Gardiner calls "the most obscure period of history in independent Koguryŏ" (Gardiner 1979). Gardiner similarly argues that elements of the Paekche founding myth must, if they reflect any reality at all, postdate 280; and his analysis of the use of the name Piryu as the father of the first contemporaneously attested Paekche king independently supports his redating of the myth.

What Gardiner did not do, however, was tie these findings to the material evidence within a systemic view of social development. I would like to propose, therefore, that the founding myths served as an ideological tool facilitating the social isolation and elevation of sub-segments of several peninsular societies. The acquisition of such a tool – the acquisition of an elite ideology – formed the basis of social stratification, which was subsequently manifested materially in the ability to construct mounded tombs among the different elite groupings. With the final fall of Lelang, not only did broad geographic areas become available for exploitation and expansion by these newly formulated elites, but the material status symbols – such as mounded tomb burial – previously monopolized by the Lelang administrators also became available for emulation.

The evolution of Koguryŏ burials from cairns to stepped pyramids could well have occurred at this date and have been transmitted almost simultaneously to the Han River basin through the founding of Paekche. In the Kaya and Silla areas as well, mounded tombs were also built from the 4th century albeit in a local style. The adoption of tomb symbolism as well as the renaming of Saro as Silla and the ascent of the foreign-derived Kim clan to power all suggest substantial internal reorganization accompanying stratification. In the case of Silla, there is no need to look for outside stimuli as far back in history as the 1st century bc conquest of Chosŏn to supply the traditionally cited foreign input; the early 4th-century ad fall of Lelang could have supplied the nuclei of the elite Kim and Pak clans, especially appropriate since archaeology is showing that the elite sub-culture in Silla developed quite late.

In conclusion, it is very significant that the formative sequences leading up to all three major peninsular states show the same pattern of developmental hiatuses at approximately the same time. This strengthens the case made here for late state formation on the peninsula more than arguments for any one case could do. If these various data can be accepted as indicating the occurrence of social stratification in the late 3rd century, then it is a matter of definition that the large states of the Three Kingdoms period did not exist prior to that time, and Samhan history should not be discussed in terms of state-level organization. Thus, the term guo as applied to Chosŏn, the Samhan polities, and early Koguryŏ should properly be translated into anthropological jargon as "chiefdom". Despite these qualifications, however, it is not being suggested here that the Samhan data are irrelevant to state formation in Korea. The economic development of the peninsula during the Proto-Three Kingdoms period was crucial to the successful formation of the large states subsequent to the disappearance of Lelang, and much more research into this little known period is needed before the emergence of Koguryŏ, Paekche and Silla will be fully understood.

#### **Appendix 1B**

#### Early Korean States: A Western-Language Bibliography\*

#### Note on style

The references in this list are reviewed in Chapter 1, and any letter designations of publication date are coordinated with the Reference List in Chapter 1. Since the spelling of Korean names is so variable, with Mc Cune-Reischauer transcription being the least commonly used, a unique listing system had to be adopted. All works by Korean scholars are referred to by surname and initials in the text and listed so below; the full personal name is given in parentheses following the author entry in the bibliography. Those authors who have more than one entry are listed separately, underlined and

- in bold; and any spelling differences in their names on individual publications are given with that publication. Note there are two Cho Y. J.s, which are distinguished by numbers (–1, –2). The style of the bibliographic entries in this Appendix differs somewhat from the chapter reference lists due to the pre-published source of this bibliography.
- Anon. 1962a. Bells of Silla Dynasty. Korea Journal 2.6: 33–35.
- Anon. 1962b. Discovery of Palkong cave temple: largest archaeological event since 1945. Korea Journal 2.12: 30–1.
- Anon. 1962c. Paekche relic: oldest stone pagoda. Korea Journal 2.10: 33–5.
- Anon. 1962d. Three stone images of Buddha. Korea Journal 2.8: 37–8.
- Anon. 1962e. World's first observatory: Chŏmsŏngdae. Korea Journal 2.2: 34.
- Anon. 1964. Temple arrangement of Sŏkkulam stone grotto. Korea Journal 4.3: 39.
- Anon. 1968. The Art of the Korean Potter: Silla, Koryŏ, Yi. Greenwich, CT: New York Graphic Society.
- Anon. 1969a. Kyŏngju: glories of ancient Silla culture. Korea Journal 9.9: 6–11.
- Anon. 1969b. Sŏkkulam: stone cave temple. Korea Journal 9.9: 15, 23, photos.
- Anon. 1971a. Excavation of the tomb of Paekche King Muryŏng. Korea Journal 11.8: 48–51.
