## **Chapter Two**

# **Thoughts on Pre-state Cultural Development on the Korean Peninsula from an Archaeological Point of View\***

## **Abstract**

This is a think piece that resulted from my first exposure to state origins archaeology in Korea in 1978. Although formulated two decades ago, many of the same concerns detailed herein are still with us today in the 21st century, and anyone who delves into the Korean archaeological literature will face the same problems of chronological overlap, multiple period names and regional diversity. Thus this paper is offered again with two purposes. It provides a baseline for my own development in viewing state formation in Korea, and it warns future researchers of the inherent problems in researching state origins on the peninsula. The chapters following this inspect some of the issues in detail, and the case studies also serve to update the material given here from twenty years ago.

## **The study of Korean states**

Historians have long been interested in the early states of Korea since there is considerable mention of the movement of peoples and the founding of dynasties in the Chinese and Korean records (cf. Gardiner 1969). The archaeological documentation of these states was activated on a large scale in the early 20th century (mainly through the work of Japanese scholars) on the discovery and excavation of such monumental remains as the Koguryŏ, Lelang and Silla tombs (cf. Hewes 1947 and Gardiner 1969 for annotated bibliographies in English). After a hiatus of several decades, excavations which occurred under modern Korean governments during the 1970s have added greatly to our knowledge of the Koguryŏ and mature Silla states; earlier stages in the development of the southern states – Paekche, Taekaya and particularly Old Silla – have also been elucidated.

In 1978 I was fortunate to have visited some of the excavation sites of early state burials. Among them was the Tumulus Park in Kyŏngju where excavations at the Ch'ŏnma-ch'ong tomb (of the Heavenly Horse, #155) and the Twin tombs (#98 north and south) have produced fabulous gold ornaments and other precious objects of the 5th- to 6th-century Silla dynasty (Archaeology in Korea 1, 2, 3; National Museum 1975; Bureau of Cultural Properties 1974). The construction of these tombs was demonstrated to be wooden chambers covered with a mound of stones and then earth. This kind of burial facility contrasts with a native type of pitchamber lined with stone slabs. The excavations in the Hwangnam-dong area of Kyŏngju, south of the Mich'u tomb, have shown that these two types of burial facilities were used contemporaneously (Archaeology in Korea 1: 14). This suggests that different burial customs existed within the Silla aristocracy, and it may reflect different levels of social status which were related to the chronological elaboration of the political hierarchy.

The stone-lined chambers were constructed under mounds of earth which were surrounded by stone kerbs. This type of chamber construction represents an earlier stage in the development of the state. Tombs of this kind have recently been excavated also in the Inwang-dong area of Kyŏngju (Om & Hwang 1974; Ji 1977) and in the Koryŏng area south of Taegu (Yun & Kim 1979), the latter being the center of the Taekaya state in the 5th century. The positioning of these tombs in Koryŏng, on a high idge overlooking the upper valleys of the Naktong River, signifies the dominance of their owners in the political landscape. Excavations in 1978 revealed a central plaza where funerary rituals, signified by crushed jars, had taken place. The ceramics at the site consist of a hard stoneware of the same tradition as Silla pottery, and numerous vessels were deposited in the stone chambers. One of these chambers also yielded a gilt-bronze crown, which is of a very different and older technological tradition than the gold ornaments from the Silla tombs.

At both Koryŏng and Inwang-dong, mounded tombs have been excavated that contained several of these stone chambers and also some cist burials – at least one for a child. Tomb #44 at Koryŏng is the largest one of this kind yet known. The mound was 32 meters in diameter and contained three main stone chambers surrounded by 32 smaller stone-lined pits or cists. These auxiliary burials have been interpreted at Koryŏng as sacrificial slave burials, based on documentary records (Archaeology in Korea 5: 5), but in Inwang-dong, the same kind of burials at Tomb #19 have been interpreted as a family tomb (Archaeology in Korea 2: 14). This difference in interpretation is significant because it illustrates our poor understanding of the basis of power and social structure in these early states. But from the data now at hand, we can certainly see an evolutionary difference in the social systems that produced these multiple-chamber tombs and the fabulously accoutred wood-chamber tombs (containing single burials) of the Silla Dynasty.

These excavations of the past decade represent the first solid advances in our archaeological knowledge of the early states in southern Korea; however, our archaeological knowledge concerning the period of state formation in Korea is still quite inadequate. It is as yet totally unclear how these early states, manifested through the large richly furnished tombs, developed from and relate to the "Iron Age" societies that apparently preceded them. Many of the difficulties encountered in researching the transition from the poorly understood Iron Age to the visible remains of the early states are, in my opinion, due to problems in coordinating and making sense of the various periodization schemes and competing material cultures in the protohistoric periods. I will elaborate on this opinion below.

## **Periodization problems**

The first periodization problem is the conflict between the traditional historical schemes and the archaeological schemes for the Three Kingdoms. While visiting Korean museums in Seoul, Kyŏngju, Pusan and Puyŏ in 1978, I noted that objects on display from the time of the Three Kingdoms were invariably dated from the 5th to 7th centuries ad – even though the museum labels stated that these Kingdoms ostensibly stretched back to 77 bc in the case of Koguryŏ, 57 bc in the case of Old Silla, and 18 bc for Paekche, not to mention ad 43 for Kaya (Puyŏ National Museum 1977; Yŏngnam University 1977; Korea University 1971; National Museum of Korea 1972: 122; Kyŏngju National Museum 1975). Virtually no materials were dated to the early centuries of these Three Kingdoms, and any exhibits which were dated from the first three or four centuries ad were not included under the headings of the Three Kingdoms but were usually assigned to the Prehistoric period. This situation brought the traditional historical dates of the Three Kingdoms into serious question for me, and I felt that the conflicts in dating material remains with reference to these traditional Kingdom dates were essentially ideological conflicts which were being given expression through nationalistic archaeology.

It is worth noting that the four museums mentioned above are relatively new facilities, being built during the 1970s when the government made massive investments in retrieving and protecting Korea's early cultural heritage. This heritage reflects on Korea as a nation: the government apparently perceiving her worldwide status to be partly dependent on the longevity of indigenous political systems. To admit publicly through museum labels, therefore, that the first Korean polities did not date from as early as traditionally stated would be unacceptable.

The retention of the historical dating scheme on a formal level causes much confusion regarding the issue of state formation – but no more confusion than is caused by the current periodization schemes from archaeology see Table 1, [Chapter 1, this volume]. The span of time from ad 1 to 300 is variously known as the Proto-Three Kingdoms, Samhan, Iron Age or Kimhae period (Kim W. Y. 1977: 12); it is also sometimes called the Lelang period (Gardiner 1969: 18; Korea University 1971: 3). All of these period designations are based on the type of artefacts found and their affinities with other phenomena of known or supposedly firm dates. For example, artefacts such as a lacquered basket, excavated from a wooden chamber tomb near modern P'yŏngyang, belong to the "Lelang period" due to their association with the Lelang commandary which supposedly existed in the northern Korean Peninsula between 108 bc and ad 313. The basket is approximately contemporaneous with the materials excavated on the western slope of the Kimhae shellmound on the southern Korean coast, which, nevertheless, do not belong to the "Lelang period" but to the "Iron Age".

The Iron Age was originally defined and dated at Kimhae, where the Japanese archaeologists Hamada and Umehara (1923) excavated iron knives and arrowheads together with a Chinese coin minted in ad 14 during the Wang Mang interregnum between the Early and Late Han Dynasties. The coin dated these deposits to the first century ad at the earliest. These finds were accompanied by paddled earthenware pots which have subsequently come to be known as Kimhae pottery; now, wherever this pottery is discovered, the sites are assigned to the "Kimhae period". The use of the

"Kimhae period" separate from the "Iron Age" allows the possibility of Kimhae ware occurring without iron.

In one sense it is admirable that, in archaeology, period names other than the Three Kingdoms are used for the first few centuries ad, because such usages reflect a disciplinary reluctance to identify early remains with the kingdoms known to have existed later in time. But it is quite clear that the differences between the multiple "periods" of this time span are not temporal but cultural in nature. The use of temporal designations, in these cases, is entirely inappropriate. Moreover, the situation has worsened in the past five years because new discoveries have extended some of these "period" designations in time, bringing them into conflict with still other era divisions.

At the Sŏngsan and Jodo Island shellmounds, also near Pusan, remains of iron-working activities including primitive furnaces have been dated respectively to the 1st and 2nd centuries bc (Archaeology in Korea 1, 2.4). At Sŏngsan, the date derives from the presence in the deposits of Wushu coins minted between 61 and 58 bc. At the shellmound on Jodo Island, iron was found with pottery closely resembling Early Yayoi Itazuke II pottery in Japan (Han & Yi 1976). Because of the similarities, the Yayoi vessels are thought to have been derived from this southern Korean pottery; and in return, the Korean pottery is thought to be at least the same age as, if not older, than the Yayoi pottery (4th to 3rd centuries bc). This early date for iron on the southern Korean coast is supported by the recent acknowledgment of Japanese archaeologists that iron was imported into Japan in the Early Yayoi period (Kawagoe 1979), rather than much later as originally thought.

These discoveries from the early 1970s have resulted in the division and extension of the Iron Age into phases I and II, the former incorporating the first three centuries bc and the latter the first three centuries ad. However, this re-definition has not eliminated problems but created them, since now Iron Age I overlaps almost completely with the Late Bronze Age, which is conceived of as occupying the last three centuries bc. As with discoveries from the ad centuries, bc finds are assigned to a "period" on the basis of their artefact composition rather than their absolute date. Therefore, if iron or paddled pottery is included, they belong to the Iron Age; or if the metal goods are entirely of bronze and Plain pottery is included, then they belong to the Bronze Age, even though these "periods" may be generally contemporaneous.

Finally, there is increasing evidence that the exclusive use of bronze at some sites may have lasted into the first few centuries ad, extending the Late Bronze Age forward to overlap even with Iron Age II. Kim Won-Yong has stated as much for the Kyŏngju area in southeastern Korea (Kim W. Y. 1981: 34); and Kim Jong-hak has singled out the importance of the P'onamdong site on the eastern coast as confirming "that the Plain Pottery was still being used in central and southern Korea after iron was introduced in the fourth and third centuries bc" (Kim J. H. 1978: 103). Plain pottery of the folded – rim type has also been discovered in Japan at Late Yayoi sites, such as Otekata in Nagasaki (Wajinden no Michi Kenkyūkai 1980), dating between the 1st and 3rd centuries ad.

This Plain pottery is exactly the kind that was recovered from the Koejong-dong stone-lined grave near Taejon. This grave contained absolutely no iron but several magnificently made bronze implements as well as Black pottery (Kim J. H. 1978). Black pottery can be compared again with Late Yayoi ceramics of the Nara Basin in Japan, which may have some affinities with the Korean materials given the long-necked jar shapetype and paddled pot surfaces. Such affinities are not unexpected, since we know from a variety of sources that Japan was at least obtaining iron from the peninsula in the early centuries ad (Tsunoda & Goodrich 1951; Kiyonaga 1982), and other goods such as ceramics and beads may have formed a part of or been influenced through the trading network. Kim Won-Yong (1977: 9) dates the Koejong burial to the late Early Bronze Age, and Han Byong-sam (1977: 16) assigns it to 300 bc. However, on the basis of these cross-cultural comparisons with Japan, it seems that a later date is more appropriate; indeed, Pearson (1976: fig. 2) places the burial's pottery at ad 100.

## **Material heterogeneity**

All these arguments begin to point in a single unavoidable direction: that there existed simultaneously a multiplicity of material repertoires in the

Korean peninsula between 400 bc and at least ad 100. There is a possibility that some of this variation was regional in nature. Bronze finds do indeed form regional clusters: a distribution map of bronze dagger discoveries indicates that there were four foci of bronze-users on the peninsula (Kim J. H. 1978: fig. 55). Among these foci, only the northwestern one around modern P'yŏngyang and the southeastern one around modern Kyŏngju have been traditionally recognized by archaeologists; the southwestern one in the Kŭm river drainage has only been recently defined (Kim W. Y. 1981: 33–4).

A tabulation of iron discoveries through 1976 by Nishitani & Azuma (1978) reveals that the first two bronze foci around P'yŏngyang and Kyŏngju also contain the majority of sites yielding iron: 30 in the former and 23 in the latter. In at least these two regions, therefore, bronze and iron must have been integrated into the cultural systems in the last few centuries BC.

In contrast, the southwestern Kŭm river region appears to have yielded very little iron, and it is possible that this region remained primarily bronzeusing until the establishment of Paekche. This is doubly suggested by similarities between the types of pottery in the bronze sites at least one Paekche tomb. The Black pottery jar from an early Paekche tomb in Karakri, Seoul (Korea University Museum 1971: 19), is of the same ceramic tradition as the Black pottery jar excavated from the Koejong-dong bronze burial in Taejon. Although the former is not a long-necked jar like the latter, it replicates very closely the closed-neck jar shape-type of Karak-ri pottery attributed to the Bronze Age. The continuity of the ceramic manufacturing techniques and shape-types strongly argue for temporal proximity; if Pearson's placement of the bronze burial at ad 100 is correct, then it quite plausible that a Bronze Age culture in the southwestern peninsula was directly succeeded by Paekche.

The situation on the Kŭm River, however, is quite different from the kind of regionalization of bronze and iron "cultures" which is being postulated for the Pusan-Kyŏngju area. As mentioned above, Kim Won-Yong has suggested that pockets of bronze-users may have existed in Kyŏngju into the 1st century ad. The geographical compactness of the Pusan – Kyŏngju area, however, would hardly allow the co-existence of independent social or political entities based differentially on bronze or iron. Perhaps some of this variability is contextual in nature, relating both to status levels and to differential functions or activities within a single social system.

Iron production can be viewed as a specialized resource exploitation activity. Carried out by villagers in complement with other production activities such as ceramic manufacture, the making of iron tools and weapons in itself does not entail any advanced social status. In fact, the very nature of iron products – being utilitarian rather than ceremonial – sets them below bronzes, which are known to have been highly esteemed. Therefore, it is quite proper to argue that bronze and iron fulfilled different functions in society and that the initiation of iron tool production probably did not disrupt traditional ritual practices based on bronze, especially outside the iron producing communities themselves. Furthermore, the objects which the iron producers acquired through trading their iron out to distant areas, such as the Lelang commandary and Japanese Islands, need not have been transferred into more traditional sectors of the regional society.

If we allow for the development of intra-regional differentiation in terms of resource exploitation and ceremonial customs, it would not be surprising to find so-called "Bronze Age" and "Iron Age" finds interdigitated across the landscape. Even the development of a "nouveau riche" based on the iron trade would not have required the disappearance of the "old money" and their customs. This interdigitation of "cultures" may perhaps explain some otherwise perplexing characteristics of the archaeological record in the crucial period just preceding the emergence of the southern Korean states.

One such problem is an apparent absence, as Kim notes, of "tombs of the ruling class, either in structure or contents" in Iron Age II; the "small stone-lined pit-tombs that replaced dolmens of the preceding period as the main burial system are furnished very poorly" (Kim W. Y. 1977b: 13). Such poorly furnished burials are represented by the Oryundae and Yean-ri excavations near Pusan (Archaeology in Korea 1, 4; Kim J. R. 1977). The latter cemetery was located on the alluvial flats within an area of shell deposits. Many stone-lined graves, cists and jar burials were discovered, with skeletal remains preserved by the high calcium content of the soil. Little other than rough Kimhae pottery accompanied them.

Since Kim's 1977 comments, however, new pit-burials without stone chamber facilities but with relatively rich goods have been discovered at Choyang-dong in Kyŏngju (Korea Newsreview 2 Jan 1982: 22). This burial(s) [the number was unclear] consisted of earthen pits containing: iron weapons and tools, iron shields similar to those from Japan, Early Han Dynasty mirrors, a lacquered knife sheath and bronzes (dated to ad 100). This kind of pit-burial has been hailed as the representative tomb construction of the early centuries ad.

Gradually, an evolutionary scheme of dolmen> stone-lined graves> pitburials> stone-lined chamber tombs> wooden double chamber tombs is being constructed for the southern Korea coast. However, it will be noted that not all pit-burials are richly furnished and represent higher-status deceased; earthen pit-burials with no grave goods have recently been excavated again at Yean-ri (Archaeology in Korea 8). Moreover, there seem to be certain continuities between the early dolmens and stone-lined graves with the later stone-chamber tombs, so that perhaps the pit-burials and the exotic Han Dynasty grave goods represent an evolutionary sideline that never fully developed.

Based on the idea of interdigitation of material repertoires, it is possible to construct an alternative evolutionary scheme that keeps the two lines separate and also might account for the differential furnishing of burials. One line would be the Bronze Age dolmens splitting into the rich stonechamber tombs and the poor stone-lined graves; and the other line would be the rich and poor pit-burials. If the latter possibly contained wooden chambers as well as wooden coffins – as earlier pit-burials in the northern half of Korea were shown to do (Tamura 1965) – then they may have been the source of the wooden chamber construction of the mature Silla tombs.

Much of the foregoing is speculative, but I have tried to look at the archaeological materials in a light that does not require the holistic and uniform transformation of societies – an unrealistic ideal in times of social stratification and regional resource exploitation. The inability of archaeologists in general to deal with intra-cultural variation is related to the development of the discipline as mainly concerned with simple, nonstratified prehistoric societies. Of course, excavations of great civilizations have always been a major part of archaeological research and exploration, but very often they have either been conducted in historically dated situations or they have concentrated only on the "civilized", meaning upperclass, portion of the remains. For societies in the early stages of social stratification where no documentation exists, it may be very difficult to identify and coordinate all parts of the cultural system because of drastically different material assemblages manipulated by different groups in the society.

Archaeology has heretofore mainly operated within a time – space framework, where the dimensions of only time and space were thought to account for variability in cultural assemblages. With more work being done on societies undergoing the process of social stratification and hierarchization, we must define a third source of variability: social status and status as it related to differential function and activities. With the development of social heterogeneity, we can no longer expect homogeneity of material culture, either in quantity or quality. Thus, we should refrain from talking not only in terms of the Bronze and Iron "Ages" but also in terms of Bronze and Iron "Cultures" (Kim W. Y. 1977a), since these artefact types do not represent complete and independent cultural systems.

Instead we must begin to think in terms of sub – cultures which are interdigitated across the landscape. These sub – cultures may relate to multiple strata of individuals or they may relate to specific subsets of activities within those strata. The possibility of ecological differentiation within a single region should definitely be recognized. Already, the coexistence of groups using different subsistence strategies has been suggested by Townsend (1975: 220) in accounting for upland/lowland distributions of Comb-patterned pottery and Plain pottery in the late 2nd to early 1st millennium bc. Such differential ecological, or resource, exploitation patterns may also account for pockets of iron producers within essentially bronze-oriented status groups in the millennium spanning ad 1.

As an example of trying to elucidate material heterogeneity in stratified societies, I have rejected the view that the Kofun Period society of 4thcentury Japan was represented by a single ceramic ware – Hajiki. Examination of the contexts in which early Haji occurred revealed it to be often accompanied by artefacts and eco-facts that can be recognized as elite objects. These include peach and walnut remains, wooden combs, talc beads, and a small mini jar, all used in a ritual or ceremonial setting. By using these Haji assemblages to date the Kofun period, what we may be doing is merely equating the Kofun period with an elite cultural stratum, without recognizing a complementary commoner stratum and material culture (Barnes 1983).

If the Korean bronzes are acknowledged to have lasted well into the early centuries ad, then perhaps the problem is one of shifting material repertoires according to status and activity context. Commoners who were involved in iron production could well have been buried in poorly furnished tombs. Since iron was mainly used to make utilitarian goods such as knives, it should not be expected that it would usurp the role of other ceremonial metals. Therefore, it is quite plausible that depositing bronzes in burials could still have been the accepted procedure among elites following traditionally prescribed rituals.

With such different materials occupying such different roles in society, it is essential to have firm datings of the objects in absolute, not just relative, terms; there are too few overlapping spheres of distribution for these objects to allow any significant degree of relative dating. It is often argued that radiocarbon datings are much too expensive to obtain as a matter of course, but the cost of the work hours needed to develop a chronology in the absence of absolute datings is surely greater. A few well-placed carbon dates could save years of data shuffling and dissension over chronology. Our concern, however, is for more than merely developing cultural chronologies. It is directed at understanding how all these elements fit together as interacting parts of operating cultural systems. If indeed there was a division between iron utilitarian objects and bronze ceremonial objects, what does this imply about the relationships between the owners and/or producers of those objects in terms of social organization?

## **Summary**

Our approach to the problem of state formation in the southern Korean peninsula hinges on the integration of so-called Bronze Age and Iron Age data, and their integration requires the use of a settlement pattern concept which focuses on actual communities and their relation to each other in a broader political and social network. Until now, almost all archaeological discoveries have been mortuary in nature, and only a few Bronze and Iron Age settlement remains have been excavated (Nelson 1982: 137; *Korea Newsreview* 11 June 1983: 24). States are complex forms of social organization which are characterized by more than mere status differences signified by burial accoutrements. In particular, the actual variables involved in state formation – such as demographic change, urban-rural dichotomies, and craft specialization – are usually monitored through settlement pattern studies.

The investigation of iron-producing communities and their distribution is an essential first step in monitoring changes in settlement pattern, but these communities cannot be considered in isolation. It will be necessary to document the usage patterns of the entire regional scape, looking at other resource exploitation and artefact usage strategies as well. It is not enough to assume that "a proper state and government existed" just because, for example, the Choyang-dong burials showed that "economic activities were brisk" (Korea Newsreview 2 Jan 1982: 22). Indeed, archaeologists generally accept this burial as evidence for the Chin Han state (Kim J. B., pers. comm.). I do not, no more than I would accept the burials in Kyushu, Japan containing Han Dynasty materials as indicating state level organization. These burials all reflect the integration of those societies into a large Yellow Sea trading network; and on the southern Korean coast, participation was based on the production and exchange of iron. This may have led to some individuals accumulating considerable personal wealth, but the real question to be asked is how did these individuals and the iron-producing communities themselves relate to other communities in terms of social structure and particularly their ritual systems. It will be necessary to activities came under specialized demonstrate how the economic administrative decision-making, rather than just specialized resource exploitation, in order to demonstrate the existence of a state.

The gap between the remains of the first centuries ad and the large mounded tombs of the mid-first millennium is rapidly being filled in as a result of continuous archaeological excavation and discovery such as at Yean-ri and Choyang-dong. We will, however, be no closer to understanding the emergence of state-level society until we can come to terms with material heterogeneity within a single time span from all its various sources. The adoption of new period and cultural names which would allow for the full recognition and integration of such heterogeneity, and the obtaining of absolute dates for all site materials, are, I feel, absolutely crucial to this endeavour.

## **Chapter Three**

# **The Development of Stoneware Technology in Southern Korea\***

#### **Abstract**

Ceramics are the foundation of chronological schemes of development. It is impossible to assess the processes of Korean state formation without an accurate ceramic chronology. Here, two schools of ceramic chronology are critiqued with reference to scientific advances in ceramic paste analysis and new kiln excavations. The two schools comprise those that postulate the beginnings of stoneware production around 1 ad and those who think it began in the 3rd century ad. These different views profoundly affect the interpretation of state formation processes, and other works must be read with this problem in mind. The author subscribes to the later dating system, which is used in subsequent chapters.

## **Introduction**

Kimhae pottery is usually conceived of as a grey, hard-fired paddled ware associated with the beginning of the iron industry on the Korean peninsula. Its name derives from its initial discovery and identification by Japanese colonial archaeologists in 1921 at a shellmound in the town of Kimhae, a few kilometres west of Pusan on the southern coast (Hamada & Umehara 1920). It occurred there together with iron slag and a Chinese Wang Mang coin minted in ad 14. The manufacturing techniques of the pottery, manifested as paddle marks on the body and incised lines encircling the shoulder or body circumference, as well as its dominant grey colour resulting from reduction firing, suggest that the ware derived from contemporaneous Chinese ceramic traditions – particularly, Han Dynasty grey ware. It is generally understood that this new ceramic technology was introduced into the peninsula together with iron manufacturing techniques, possibly via the Chinese commandery of Lelang which was established in the region of modern P'yŏngyang in 108 bc. Until recently, Kimhae ware has been considered to be the representative ware of the Proto-Three Kingdoms period from ad 1–300.

The date of the Wang Mang coin from Kimhae has traditionally been used as the terminus ante quern for the introduction of both paddled-ware ceramic technology and iron manufacture to the southern coast of the peninsula (Shin K. C. 1982: 7); however, the beginning date of Kimhae ware has been much disputed. In 1976, the authors of the excavation report on the Chodo Shellmound (Han & Lee 1976), which is located near Kimhae and which yielded Kimhae ware and iron remains as at many other shellmound sites along the southern coast, suggested that Kimhae ware could not have started later than the 2nd century bc because it has been found together with Middle Yayoi (100 bc–ad 100) pottery of Japan. Though this reasoning is faulty, since the ware could equally well date from the end of the 1st century ad on the same grounds, the early dating was seized upon by many other authors. Several of the southern shellmound sites as well as the Taesin-ri site in the mid-peninsular Han River basin have subsequently been given dates of the 4th to 3rd centuries bc based on the Chodo report (Shin K. C. 1980: 212– 3).

In 1980, Shin Kyung-cheol undertook the redating of these shellmound sites by comparing the Bronze Age Plain Pottery of Korea – which occurs in these shellmounds as well as in Japanese Yayoi sites – with the chronology of Japanese Yayoi pottery. He discovered that roll-rimmed (with circular cross-sections) Plain Pottery pots occur with terminal Early Yayoi wares (ca. 100 bc), while fold-rimmed (with triangular cross-sections) Plain Pottery pots occur with early Middle Yayoi to terminal Late Yayoi wares (ca. 100 bc–ad 300). The immediate import of these findings was that Bronze Age Plain Pottery was recognized not to be confined to the Korean Bronze Age, which nominally ended at 1 ad. It existed into the Proto-Three Kingdoms period as well!

Moreover, based on his shellmound reassessments, Shin revised the definition of Kimhae ware to refer only to stoneware pots with the traditional paddled and incised surface. All those earthenware pots of the Kimhae style, Shin put into a new category called "Wajil" pottery. He found that foldrimmed Plain Pottery and Wajil pottery both occurred in the lower layers of the shellmound sites he examined; thus he reasoned that Kimhae stoneware must date subsequent to these pottery types, which must have occupied the 1st to 3rd centuries ad according to the comparisons with Japanese Yayoi pottery. By Shin's reckoning, therefore, Kimhae pottery (i.e. stoneware) did not appear until the 4th century ad.

The category of Wajil pottery was quickly adopted by Ch'oe Chŏng-gyu to describe the pottery he excavated from burials in the Kyŏngju region, particularly at the Choyang-dong site (Ch'oe C. G. 1982a, b, 1983). He also became convinced that Wajil, not Kimhae, was the representative pottery type of the Proto-Three Kingdoms period. The immediate reaction to this statement of Kim Wŏn-yŏng, the doyen of Korean archaeology, was to question Wajil's representativeness, noting that Ch'oe had found it only in Kyŏngju graves and that its shape and surface finishing was inconvenient for daily use (Kim W. Y. 1983: 519). Kim proposed that Wajil might have been a special funerary ware while Kimhae was the contemporaneous, functionally more important, utilitarian ware. In this way, he suggested that Wajil and Kimhae pottery existed together in the Proto-Three Kingdoms period. It must be assumed here that by Kimhae ware, Kim meant both earthenware and stoneware; for if only earthenware was meant, the statement becomes nonsensical since earthenware Kimhae ware is equivalent to Wajil by Shin's definition.

Thus, in the archaeological literature, there are currently two opposing schools of thought concerning the dating of Kimhae ware: the Traditionalists, who consider Kimhae ware 1) to be composed of both earthenwares and stonewares all using the same paddled formation technology, and 2) to be representative of the Proto-Three Kingdoms period; and the Revisionists, who 1) assign earthenware and stoneware paddled ceramics to the different categories of Wajil and Kimhae, and 2) consider Wajil, not Kimhae, as representative of the Proto-Three Kingdoms period. It is interesting to note that the arguments for defining and dating Kimhae ware cleave both on generational and regional lines. Traditionalists Han Byŏngsam and Kim Wŏn-yŏng are long-established and well-respected scholars in the field, while Revisionists Shin Kyung-cheol and Ch'oe Chŏng-gyu are younger scholars who appear to be challenging establishment ideas. The former two are also now located mainly in Seoul in the bastion of Paekche culture where the category of Wajil pottery is not used. The latter two have been until recently based in Yŏngnam where Wajil is now an accepted ceramic category.

The problems surrounding Kimhae and Wajil pottery, however, are more than just definitional: they concern basic technological transformations of protohistoric Korean society. The main point of contention is exactly when stoneware production was initiated on the peninsula. Was it contemporaneous with iron manufacture as the Traditionalists assume, or did it postdate iron technology by up to four centuries as postulated by the Revisionists?

This chapter seeks to assess the possibilities for stoneware manufacture on the Korean peninsula from a technological and cross-cultural point of view. It is planned to follow up this exposition of the current problems with scientific analyses of sherds of the individual wares themselves so that these problems can be dealt with quantitatively and definitively.

## **Wajil pottery**

Following Shin K. C.'s reassessment of the southern shellmound culture (1980), both Shin himself, in 1982, and Ch'oe C. G., in 1982 (1982a), set out definitive statements on the new Wajil pottery. It is viewed as an outgrowth of Late Bronze Age pottery on the peninsula under the influence of new Chinese ceramic technology. Its connections with previous Korean Bronze Age ceramics are apparent in initial shape-type continuations; and the Chinese influences are apparent in massive changes in body paste, firing, and manufacturing technique. Wajil is a grey or greyish white earthenware made of finer paste than Plain Pottery. Two techniques characterize its surface finishing: paddling and/or clay slipping. Paddle marks occur in both cordwrapped and lattice-carved designs; and many vessels are clay slipped on both their inner and outer surfaces. The even coloration of the ware implies fine control over a reducing atmosphere during firing, and this implies the existence of a fairly sophisticated kiln. Wajil is assessed to have been fired at approximately 900°C – not quite high enough to cause the formation of stoneware but higher than most other earthenwares. Its lower firing temperature, and fine paste coupled with the slip, gives Wajil vessel surfaces an almost powdery texture distinct from that of the hard, ringing and sometimes glassy texture of the later stonewares. The name "Wajil" was adopted by Shin from the Japanese "Gaki", the name of an early Medieval pottery in Japan of similar manufacture. Both names employ the character for "tile" as their first syllable, linking these wares to the paste type and reduction firing techniques for roof tiles.

Wajil pottery can be divided into three stylistic phases: Old, New and Three Kingdoms. Three Kingdoms Wajil is characterized by a rougher texture and changes in shape to resemble the Three Kingdoms stoneware and earthenware (Shin K. C. 1982: 73). The other two categories both belong to the Proto-Three Kingdoms period. In addition to this tripartite classification of Wajil pottery, Wajil of the Proto-Three Kingdoms (ad 1–300) has been divided into six stages by Ch'oe C. G. (1983). Japanese archaeologists see problems with the utility of this fine chronology (Haraguchi, in Narasaki 1984: 230), and my impression is also that this seriation seems premature and the grosser categories of Old and New are more useful at this point.

The shapes peculiar to the Old Wajil assemblage \(Figure 3.1) are a longnecked jar with flaring rim and horn-shaped handles (a, b); a small pot or jar with no sharp break between shoulder and neck (c, d); and a globular pot with a short, flaring rim (e). The first two derive from the burnished black and red wares (respectively) of the Late Bronze Age, but the third appears to be an introduced shape-type imitating the typical Han Chinese pot. The paste of these vessels is finer than that of Plain Pottery, and the surface slip might have derived from the Han painted pottery tradition. The colour of most of the Wajil vessels is grey, but a few red ones reflect the usual oxidizing firing of Plain Pottery vessels. Old Wajil vessels were made on a turntable with the aid of a spatula and/or paddle. The surface texturing resulting from the use of these tools consists of cord-wrapped paddle marks, lattice-carved paddle marks and horizontal incised lines. When such paddle marks and incised lines occur on the globular jar shape-type, the result is a close resemblance to the Han Dynasty pot, and it is this kind of vessel that has traditionally been regarded as Kimhae ware.


**Figure 3.1** Old Wajil (a–e) and New Wajil (f–h) shape types and decoration (after Shin K. C. 1989)

The datings for Old Wajil shape-types were undertaken in detail by Shin K. C. (1982), based on comparative materials across the southern Korean peninsula and co-occurrences with Plain Pottery and Yayoi pottery, the latter from the Japanese archipelago. His findings were as follows:

- 1Old Wajil and Plain Pottery often occur together, therefore it is clear that Plain Pottery was not wholly transformed into Wajil under Chinese influence.
- 2Plain Pottery occurs in Japan in Middle and Late Yayoi contexts, therefore this tradition of pottery manufacture lasted at least until the 3rd century ad.
- 3The earliest Wajil pottery as determined by shape seriation occurred together with a Yayoi vessel from Kyushu dating to the mid-1st century AD.

From this evidence, Shin concludes that the Chinese influence on southern Plain Pottery began no earlier than the middle of the 1st century ad to cause the emergence of Wajil pottery. Ch'oe C. G. (1983) concurs with this dating but puts the starting date of Wajil in the first quarter of the 1st century ad on the basis of various documentary evidence for communications with Lelang. He furthermore dates the beginning of New Wajil to the third quarter of the 2nd century ad (1982a: 232).

New Wajil pottery, according to Ch'oe C. G. (1983), consists of vessel shapes recognizably continuous from Old Wajil but with the loss of hornhandles on the jars and the addition of pedestals and lids (Figure 3.1: f, g). Pedestaled jars have either flaring or straight rims, the former accompanied by a shallow saucer-like lid and the latter by a deep, handled lid. Bands of finely incised parallel lines decorate the long-necked jars in vertical, horizontal or checkerboard fashion. With these also occur a kind of squashed-bodied pot mounted on a pedestal (Figure 3.1: h), whose burnished mesh-decoration on the shoulder is thought to be of direct Chinese influence (Kim W. Y. 1982).

Finally, concerning the geographical range of Wajil pottery, this ware was defined on the basis of regional occurrences of the sets of vessels described above as Old and New Wajil. These sets occur mainly in the southeastern (Yŏngnam) region of N. Kyŏngsang and S. Kyŏngsang provinces. The specific shape-types in these sets do not occur outside Yŏngnam; however, the Wajil techniques of manufacture do. These include formation by paddling and reduction firing to the hardest earthenware level. In defining Wajil pottery, Shin considered the manufacturing techniques more diagnostic than the shape-types (Shin K. C., pers. comm. 1988); and from the beginning, he intended his definition to extend to all pottery manufactured by such techniques regardless of their shape or place of occurrence (Shin K. C. 1986: 121). Thus there is scope within Wajil pottery for the assignment of various regional potteries to this ware, requiring an expansion of the shape-categories described above for Old and New Wajil for each region.

## **Kimhae pottery**

The Traditionalists take a very loose approach to the definition of Kimhae ware by retaining the original Japanese usage of the term. The original application of Kimhae to the pottery found in the shellmound included both soft- and hard-fired earthenwares and stonewares. Moreover, the category covered a broad time range without internal divisions (Oda, in Narasaki 1984: 216): from the 1st century ad (based on the Wang Mang coin) to the Three Kingdoms. Without refining the chronological succession of these differently fired vessels occurring in the shellmounds (which is the Revisionists' greatest criticism of the Traditionalists), the Traditionalists have adopted this diffuse category of Kimhae pottery as representative of the Proto-Three Kingdoms period. In doing so, they have allowed the interpretation to develop that stoneware existed together with soft- and hardfired earthenwares throughout the Proto-Three Kingdoms period. In 1982, Kim Wŏn-yŏng established a new transitional phase for the transformation of Kimhae stoneware into Three Kingdoms stoneware of the Kaya and Silla traditions. This is his so-called Puwŏn-dong Phase, based on the Kimhae and Kaya/Silla stonewares co-occurring in strata I-III at Location A of the Puwŏn-dong shellmound site near Pusan (cf. Shim B. K. 1981). With that transformation, Kimhae ceased after four centuries of postulated existence on the peninsula.

The Revisionist proponents of Wajil pottery, namely Shin and Ch'oe, adopt a very strict definition of Kimhae ware. First and foremost they see it as a stoneware; secondarily, the paddle marks resulting from its formation are thought to be distinctly different from Wajil paddle marks although some overlap is recognized. Kimhae stoneware has mainly parallel-line paddle marks, whereas Wajil is characterized by cord-wrapped and lattice-carved paddle marks; these latter occur occasionally on Kimhae vessels (Ch'oe C. G. 1983: 2). In contrast to the Traditionalist view that Kimhae stoneware existed throughout the Proto-Three Kingdoms period, the Revisionists believe that stoneware did not emerge until the late 3rd or early 4th century ad and that Kimhae was its first manifestation. They also use the materials at Loc. A of Puwŏn-dong to illustrate the transformation of Kimhae stoneware into Three Kingdoms stoneware; but in their case, Kimhae is envisioned to have been an ephemeral initial stage of stoneware manufacture which lasted no longer than fifty or a hundred years. The relationships between the different datings of these wares are given in Table 3.1.

#### **The advent of stoneware**

Kim Wŏn-yŏng has written that is impossible to imagine that techniques for iron production, requiring high kiln temperatures, could have existed in the Proto-Three Kingdoms period without that technology being transferred to the ceramic industry (1983: 521). Thus, the postulated absence of stoneware during Proto-Three Kingdoms iron manufacturing is incomprehensible to the Traditionalists: even if iron and stoneware were not introduced as a technological package from China, the early introduction of iron manufacturing sometime between the 4th and 1st centuries bc is envisioned as having quickly influenced ceramic production through the adoption of high-firing kilns. Shin Kyung-cheol's countering view is that iron was produced in China for almost one millennium before stoneware technology was developed; therefore it is quite possible for high kiln temperatures to be used for one craft but not for another (Shin K. C. 1986: 120). He maintains that it was not until what he calls the "climbing" kiln was developed in the 3rd century AD that even in China stoneware was produced, and that this technology was most likely introduced into the Han River basin soon thereafter (Shin K. C., pers. comm. 1988). This "climbing" kiln is assumed to be equivalent to the Long kiln discussed below and not the true "stepped" kiln, which was not developed in China until the 14th century (cf. Liu 1986). There are, however, several flaws in this counter-argument which will be examined below.

Both Traditionalists and Revisionists view 300 ad as a watershed in ceramic development on the peninsula, but they do so for different reasons. Kim's emphasis on the Puwŏn-dong phase as transitional – between Kimhae and Three Kingdoms stoneware – refers only to the shape repertoire since he believes stoneware technology was common to both. Shin, on the other hand, postulates the introduction of stoneware technology itself at this time with accompanying changes in shape. In either case, it is necessary to enquire where the new shape repertoire came from. At least for the southeastern coast, it does not seem to be an indigenous transformation for the following reasons:

- 1. Wajil and early stoneware occur together, indicating the co-existence of separate traditions rather than the transformation of Wajil into stoneware (Shin K. C. 1982);
- 2.the earliest Three Kingdoms stoneware occurs at Puwŏn-dong fully developed, without an experimental state; and
- 3.the shapes of this stoneware are mostly alien to the Wajil/Plain Pottery shape-types preceding it.

Indeed, Ch'oe Chŏng-gyu believes that judging from vessel shape details, stoneware developed first in the central Korean peninsula (1982a: 223), and this opinion accords well with that of Shin's concerning the location of the introduction of the kiln technology. Other scholars look beyond the peninsula to China, where they see the prototypes of peninsular stoneware shapes in the stamped pottery of the Geometric tradition of southern China (Ch'oe C. G. 1982a, 1983).

In evaluating these views on the advent of stoneware production on the Korean peninsula, it is necessary first to present some of the new understandings in ceramic technology in general, and then to look at the new data from China, from where stoneware technology is thought to have been introduced to Korea.

## **Advances in ceramic technology**

The above arguments of both the Traditionalists and Revisionists for Korean stoneware development hinge on the development and introduction of highfiring techniques from China. Shin makes the case that stoneware was not present in China until the 3rd century ad, therefore, its technology could hardly have been imported before that time. There are several problems with this statement, not the least the difficulty in defining "stoneware", and secondly, the difference between the existence of technological means and their general adoption as a widespread technology.

#### **Earthenware versus stoneware**

In distinguishing between earthenware and stoneware, most Korean scholars draw the line at a firing temperature of 1000°C, the standard rule-of-thumb dividing point between hard-fired earthenwares and stonewares. However, for the Puwŏn-dong site report, most relevant to the debate over the emergence of stoneware in Korea, the excavator has chosen a dividing point of 600°C between soft and hard-fired wares (Shim B. K., pers. comm. 1988). Such a division lumps all hard-fired earthenware and stonewares together, so that the evolution from Kimhae to Three-Kingdoms stoneware that is postulated to have occurred at the site is completely obscured.

Recent ceramic research, however, has demonstrated that even this old-fashioned rule of thumb is only partially accurate and less than useful in distinguishing between earthenware and stoneware. The new state of knowledge concerning the difference between these wares can best be described by a four-square of firing temperature versus clay composition (Table 3.2). The important thing to note in this scheme is that a distinction is drawn between the words "earthenware" and "earthenware clays" and "stoneware" and "stoneware clays". The result is that within the "earthenware" category are vessels made of two very different kinds of clays with radically different potentials.

As earthenware clays are heated, the iron in the clay begins to act as a flux and contributes to the hardening and strengthening of the body. However, when the temperature reaches a threshold of 1100°C, the iron has the reverse effect on the clay body and begins to melt it. Thus, because of their chemical composition containing a lot of iron, most earthenware clays cannot be fired above 1100°C without melting, although fairly hard ceramic bodies can be produced up to that temperature. Earthenwares made of earthenware clays come in a continuum of hardness, from very soft to a hardness that rivals stoneware.

Stoneware clays do not melt but undergo a chemical transformation when fired above 1000°C. This transformation used to be referred to as "vitrification" or the formation of glassy phase within the ceramic body. According to the Brussels Nomenclature (Fournier 1973), stoneware was defined as exhibiting vitrification resulting in a porosity of less than 5 per cent. With new research on ceramic body microstructure, it is now recognized that vitrification *per se* is not the crucial transformation but that the critical event is actual formation of a new mineral called mullite within the clay body (Kingery & Vandiver 1986: 229). Mullite crystals begin to grow when the temperature exceeds 1000°C, their chemical composition consisting of silicate and aluminum oxides (3A12O3 + 2SiO2) (Fournier 1973: 156). It is obvious that in order for mullite to form, aluminum and silica must be present in the clay body, and this is what distinguishes stoneware clays from earthenware clays.

Table 3.2 Wares resulting from different clays fired at high and low temperatures

## ![](_page_157_Figure_1.jpeg)

Conclusions based on Table 3.2 are that no matter how high earthenware clays are fired, they will not become stoneware because they do not have the correct chemical compositions. Earthenware clays can only be used to make earthenware; fired above 1100 or 1200°C, they will melt. In contrast, two kinds of pottery can be made using stoneware clays: earthenware and stoneware. Fired at temperatures under 1000°C, vessels made of stoneware clay have the same properties as those of earthenware-clay vessels: high porosity, similar degrees of softness and hardness, etc., and are therefore included in the earthenware category (Fournier 1973: 70). Without mineralogical analyses, these low-fired stoneware-clay earthenware vessels are indistinguishable from earthenware-clay vessels. High-fired stonewareclays become stoneware with the formation of mullite. However, stoneware cannot be distinguished from earthenware by determining firing temperature alone since its range overlaps with hard-fired earthenwares. Mineralogical analysis is necessary, and the identification of mullite is usually accomplished with a scanning electron microscope with microprobe. The significance of this discussion for Korean ceramic history is that current debates concerning the development of stoneware which are based on intuitive assessments of hardness, or measurements of firing temperature alone, are futile.

#### **Chinese stoneware production**

An important tenet of the Revisionist position on the advent of stoneware production in Korea is that such production could not have occurred until the development of the sloping kiln for firing stoneware in China in the 3rd century ad. However, it has long been recognized in the field of Chinese ceramics that individual pieces of stoneware were produced from the Shang period (ca. 1700–1150 bc) onwards (cf. Honey 1944); such vessels are often referred to as "proto-porcelain" (Rhodes 1959; Rawson 1980: 40; Shangraw 1978: 44–5, 98; Chang 1986: 26, 40) because the ability to produce stoneware is seen as the first step towards the production of the famous Chinese porcelains, exemplary for their high-fired technology at a time when similar kiln techniques were unknown in the west. For researchers interested only in the development of stoneware, not porcelain, it is difficult to get a grasp on the Chinese research because most investigations are oriented towards understanding the latter and the attendant high-fired glazes (cf. Medley 1976: ch2; Shangraw 1978: 44–7) rather than the former for its own sake.

It is recognised from the above discussion of clays that the production of such stoneware vessels required the use of special clays. Chinese "earthenware clays" are mainly loessic in derivation and are thus widespread and easily available as they occur over the surface of much of northern China; they have high iron, calcium, magnesium, potassium and sodium content (Nigel Wood, pers. comm. 1989). Yangshao, Longshan, Zhou and Han grey paddled wares and Han lead-glazed wares are characteristically made of these clays. Chinese "stoneware clays", on the other hand, derive from sediments buried deeply under the loess cover. Thus, they are more inaccessible and their exploitation first occurred along a band across south central China where they were exposed by geological processes (Wood 1987: 49). Vessels made of stoneware clay first appeared sporadically in the late Longshan and Shang periods and throughout the Zhou period. Two regional varieties of stoneware clays became exploited in the historical periods: aluminous clays from northern China which contain a high percentage of true clay minerals with very little quartz mixed in, and siliceous clays of southern China which contain very little true clay minerals but are mainly composed of mica and quartz (Wood 1987: 41). The amount of mullite which will grow in aluminous stoneware clays will be far greater than that in siliceous stoneware clays, but the mineral will form in either at temperatures above 1000°C.

In what kind of kilns were these early Chinese stonewares fired? Two basic kinds of kilns were used in the prehistoric and early historic eras: the up-draught kiln of the Neolithic and Shang periods (cf. Li 1985: figs. 4, 5, 6; Liu 1986: figs. 1, 2; Rhodes 1968), and the cross-draught kiln developed in the Zhou period (Li 1985: figs. 7, 8). Out of the latter developed A) the Mantou (meaning "steamed bun" because it resembles a hemispherical bread roll in shape!) kiln of northern China \(Figure 3.2: A), which could be constructed as either a simple cross-draught kiln or more complexly as a down-draught kiln, and B) the so-called Long (meaning "dragon" but also long in length!) kiln of southern China \(Figure 3.2: B), which remained a cross-draught kiln. Stoneware is known to have been produced in any and all of these kinds of kilns from the Shang period onwards. Despite Shin's hypothesis that stoneware could only be produced in sloping kilns, the attainment of high firing temperatures does not depend on the kiln type but on details and quality of construction. It can be seen from Figure 3.3 that such temperatures were first achieved in the Late Neolithic period in China.

In the Shang period, a technical advance on the simple Neolithic updraught kiln was made by constructing the kiln only half in the ground; with the upper half of the chamber built of clay above ground, the escape aperture could be narrowed down to provide better control of the air flow through the kiln (Liu 1986). By closing the aperture, higher firing temperatures, up to 1200°C, could be attained for the firing of stoneware; and the identification of actual pieces of stoneware demonstrates that these temperatures were actually accomplished. However, the number of pieces known remains extremely small, suggesting that these were firing "accidents" or unusual attainments of sporadic occurrence. Moreover, only an oxidizing atmosphere was produced in these kilns.


## **Figure 3.2**Chinese kiln types (after Liu 1986)

- B. The cross-draught Long kiln (on right) with vertical (upper) and horizontal (lower) cross-sections

## ![](_page_160_Figure_4.jpeg)

**Figure 3.3** Maximum firing temperatures achieved for ceramics from Late Neolithic to Post-Han times (after Li 1985)

This situation persisted throughout the Zhou period until the early Warring States period (475–221 bc), when the Long kiln began to appear in the Zhejiang area (Lao, Ye & Cheng 1986). Built on a slope, the height differential provided the draught through the chamber; temperatures of 1300°C were regularly achieved in a reducing atmosphere. The first Long kilns were actually quite short: 6m or so in the Warring States period, increasing to 10m in length in the Eastern Han, and 13m in the Three Kingdoms Six Dynasties periods. During this time, many experiments were made in the slope of the kiln floor for best penetration of the flame (Lao, Ye & Cheng 1986): the excavated example from the Warring States period has a constant gradient of 10°, but from the Eastern Han period, the floor was divided into differential slopes for the front and rear of the firing chamber. First, a steeper gradient was given to the front, then to the rear in the Three Kingdoms Six Dynasties period; finally, a combination of 20° chamber front slope and 10° chamber back slope was worked out as ideal in the Eastern Jin period. Subsequent to this adjustment, kiln length grew substantially, reaching 50–60m in the Song Dynasty. Lao et al. postulate that the Long kiln was developed in response to local conditions: the siliceous stoneware clays of southern China containing a high proportion of iron, and pinewood fuel which gave off rich volatile matter during firing. Liu adds that the Long kiln was extremely efficient for firing thin-walled vessels (Liu 1986).

The Mantou kiln of northern China was radically different from the Long kiln of southeastern China; it is said by Liu to have been suited for firing the thick northern "porcelains" (Liu 1986). The Mantou kiln consisted of a level-floored hemispherical chamber with no upper opening but flues at floor-level in the back; air which rose naturally to the ceiling during firing was thus forced back to floor level and out the flues, forming a semi-downdraught kiln (Liu 1986). In this kiln type as well, temperatures of between 1200–1300°C and a reducing atmosphere could be attained.

Thus, stoneware could be fired in either of these kiln types, which appeared in the Warring States period, as well as in the earlier Shang updraught kiln. Whether or not it was, however, depended on the type of clays available and consumer preference. Since stoneware clays were fairly inaccessible in the north without considerable effort at extraction, most northern wares produced were hard-fired earthenwares made of loessic earthenware clay. Moreover, the aluminous stoneware clays that are available in the north are fairly intractable and need very high firing, which was seldom achieved in the early Mantou kilns (Yan & Zhang 1986). In the south, the siliceous stoneware clays could be adequately fired to stoneware quality in the Long kilns; lower temperature firings in the same kilns, however, would have produced hard-fired earthenwares made of stoneware clays. It remains to be seen what the relative quantities were of low-fired and high-fired stoneware clays were throughout the Late Zhou, Han and Three Kingdoms period; these would reveal the consumer demand for stoneware as opposed to the mere existence of technical prerequisites for its production. Finally, a situation can easily be envisioned where only certain regional kilns, particularly in the "porcelain triangle" of modern Zhejiang, Fujian and Henan provinces (Feng 1986), regularly produced stoneware vessels; but until their wares came into demand elsewhere, their distribution would have remained fairly localized.

#### **Discussion**

The significance of these questions about Chinese ceramic technology and consumerism for the Korean peninsula cannot be overstated. Several inferences can be drawn from them to construct alternative hypotheses about stoneware development in Korea:

1. If knowledge of Chinese ceramic technology came primarily through the Lelang commandery, with its connections to the north Chinese courts, then it is probable that the Mantou kiln construction was imported. Depending on the types of clays available on the Korean peninsula, different kinds of wares might have been fired in these kilns. If earthenware clays or aluminous stoneware clays, such as were available in northern China, were used in making Korean vessels, then it is likely that only hard-fired earthenwares were produced. This would be consistent with the difficulty, also experienced in north China, of getting good enough temperature control in early Mantou kilns to actually produce stoneware from stoneware clay. However, if siliceous stoneware clays were fired in Mantou kilns in Korea, then it is possible that stoneware vessels could have been produced throughout the Lelang period since they did not have to be fired at quite so high temperatures as aluminous stoneware clays. If formation techniques had diffused with the kiln technology, then the resultant wares would have appeared much like the paddled Zhou and Han grey hard-fired earthenwares of northern China.

2. If the main avenue of informational input on ceramic technology led from southeastern China to the Korean peninsula, then it is likely that the Long kiln technology was transferred together with regional formation techniques. Stoneware vessels could have been produced from either aluminous or siliceous stoneware clays – whatever was available on the peninsula. Either clay fired in a Long kiln could have produced stoneware if sufficient temperatures were reached – which could be done more easily in a Long kiln. If formation techniques accompanied the diffusion of kiln technology, then the resultant wares should resemble local products of the southeastern Coast of China.

These two hypotheses are not mutually exclusive; transfers of technology could have occurred from both regions of China over a period of several centuries. The possibilities for information flow from north China through Lelang were many, but contact and interaction between the southeastern coast and various parts of the Korean peninsula have not yet been documented.

However, one clue to such relationships exists in the form of decoration on New Wajil pots. Korean archaeologists (Kim W. Y. 1982: 24) have identified a prototype for the hash-marked shoulder band in pottery from the Western Jin Dynasty of China (ad 265–316) \(Figure 3.4). This vessel was excavated from a tomb in Zhejiang Province (Qu-xian Museum 1974) on the eastern seaboard within the "porcelain triangle". If kiln technology was also transferred to the Korean peninsula along with decorative techniques, then kiln structures resembling Long kilns should be present on the peninsula. This is indeed the case, as all of the earliest kilns yet to be excavated in southern Korea are sloping rather than hemispherical in shape. Such kilns have been discovered at Taegok-ri in the Chuam Dam excavations near Kwangju and at Samnyong-ri and Sansu-ri in Chinch'on at road excavation sites (cf. Choi & Ch'oe 1988).

**Korean kiln excavations**

## **Taegok-ri kilns**

The Taegok-ri site, situated on a riverine terrace near Kwangju in Chŏllanamdo was discovered to have been occupied continuously from the 5th century bc to the 5th century ad. The dwellings are divided into four phases through the Late Bronze Age (5th century bc–ad 1) and Proto-Three Kingdoms period (ad 1–300). Several small pits containing Plain Pottery of the former period are interpreted as simple pit kilns because of their inclusion of burned earth and charcoal. These are very similar to the small pits which are suggested to have served as kilns at the Karako site in Nara, Japan (Barnes 1978), and it would seem that such simple facilities sufficed for the firing of soft earthenwares. Choi, Lee & Yun (1988: 147) estimate the firing temperature in the Plain Pottery pit kilns at Taegok-ri between 573– 870°C.


**Figure 3.4** Hash-decorated jar from a Western Jin tomb in Zhejiang Province, cited as the prototype for the New Wajil squashed-bodied jar (after Kim W. Y. 1982)

The Taegok-ri site has also yielded an elongated kiln \(Figure 3.5: A) which the excavators date to the end of the Proto-Three Kingdoms period at the terminal 3rd to early 4th century (Choi, Kwŏn & Kim 1988: 24). It measures 6.3m in length and 3.7m at the widest point where the fire box is expanded sideways; but the tunnel itself is 4.3m long with an average floorwidth of approximately 1m (cf. Table 3.3). The kiln was not built on a hillside but in the midst of the terrace settlement. Nevertheless, the floor of the kiln was slightly sloped by digging the firebox between 0.83–0.97m into the ground (the dimensions given in the profile and cross-section line drawings are in conflict) and angling the chamber from there up to the ground surface. There is a clear step of 15cm at the upper end of the chamber which was covered with black clay, and charcoal from the firebox occurred over the lower half of the length of the tunnel. The superstructure of the kiln was entirely destroyed through later agricultural activities and is unknown.


**Figure 3.5** Excavated kiln remains in southern Korea

A. Taegok-ri kiln near Kwangju, Chŏlla-namdo, ca. late 3rd century ad; horizontal plan (upper) and vertical cross-section (lower) (after Choi, Kwŏn & Kim 1988)

B. Sansu-ri #8 kiln, Chinch'on, Ch'ungch'ŏng-bukdo, ca. 4th century ad; horizontal plan (upper) and vertical cross-section (lower) (after Ch'oe B. H. 1988)

Artifacts from the kiln include greyish white earthenware with lattice and cord-paddled surfaces – that is, typical Wajil pottery – as well as reddish earthenware. Of interest are a ceramic anvil for holding inside the pot during paddling, a pottery spindle whorl, and a horn-shaped pot handle. Many pottery sherds were also found in an area 7–8m2 to the south of the kiln. The pottery types from within the kiln match those from Phase III houses (ad 1– 300) on the later end, especially house no. 56, and are therefore Proto-Three Kingdoms in date.

The excavator states that he sees more similarities between this kiln and what he calls the "round" kiln of the Chinese Warring States and Qin period (Choi, Kwŏn & Kim 1988: 17, 24) than between it and later sloped kilns. The reasons for this evaluation are unstated, but they might have something to do with the "waisted" character of the Warring States cross-draught kiln shown in this figure. They comment that the Taegok-ri kiln was used only to produce earthenware, which was all that was found in the kiln chamber and most of the house-pits (Choi, Kwŏn & Kim 1988: 25). However, among the eight sherds from the kiln they submitted for mineralogical analysis, one was determined to have been fired at 975°C and two others are noted as containing mullite (Choi, Lee & Yun 1988: 47, 149–50).

In contrast to the excavators' opinions, I would classify this kiln as a Long kiln, since it has a sloping floor and its chamber dimensions fall within the range of those for Long kilns in China (see above). And the presence of mullite, if identified correctly, definitely indicates firing temperatures of over 1000°C and the formation of stoneware.

#### **Chinch'on kilns**

On a hillside now terraced with paddy fields in the southwestern peninsula, eleven kilns (Table 3.3) at two sites were excavated in conjunction with expressway construction (Ch'oe B. H. 1988). All are longish ovals in plan but are referred to by the excavators as having "tunnels" and are categorized as "climbing kilns" \(Figure 3.5B). There were both underground tunnels (T)

(Table 3.3), and trench tunnels (1/2T) with specially made superstructures; the walls of both kinds were made or lined with clay. The ceramics discovered in the kilns were of all three major wares: soft-fired earthenware (Y = Yŏnjil), hard-fired earthenware (W = Wajil), and stoneware (K = Kyŏngjil). These kilns are thought to date to the 4th century ad.
