**Figure 6.6** Excavations of stone-line foundations (left) Chŏksŏng (after Song J. T. 1978); (right) Ch'ŏngpung (after Pak Y. B. 1984)

Most excavations of walled sites in Korea focus on the walls themselves in order to determine their method and date of construction. Very few of the interiors have been excavated. Two unusual examples, however, from the Han

River basin near Seoul give us good insight into the potential range in layout and function among walled sites of this proto-historic period. These are the sites of Mongch'on T'osŏng and Isŏng Sansŏng.

#### **Urban sites**

# *Wangxian/T'osŏng-ri* **\(Government General of Chōsen 1915; Chōsen Kaseki Chosakai 1938)**

In the late 1st millenium bc, a capital by the name of Wangxian of a polity called Weiman Chosŏn in the northwestern Korean peninsula is known from Chinese texts covering the Zhou period see [Chapter 1, this volume]. It is said that in 199 bc the king of that polity began to interfere with Han Dynasty communications to other chief-level figures in the region, inciting an invasion of Han troops that destroyed the polity in 108 bc and established military commandery rule over the northern peninsula. It is thought that the headquarters of the Lelang commandery was established at the old capital of Wangxian, and scholars identify this commandery with a walled site named T'osŏng-ri, still extant on the southern bank of the Taedong River just south of present-day P'yŏngyang. The earthen walls of this site enclose an irregular area of 42 hectares. The interior was investigated by the Japanese in 1935, and several building sites with corner stones, stone or brick gutters and drains, and brick-paved lanes leading up to them were discovered. On a rise in the centre of the site were excavated tiles including one inscribed with the characters for "Lelang Ceremonial Palace". Other Han objects recovered from the site included sealing clays, coins, coin moulds, bronze and ceramic kettles, bronze and iron arrowheads and much pottery. Pre-Han pottery and lithic artefacts were also present. These latter finds show the site was in use prior to the establishment of the commandery, but it is not at all clear to what period the extant walls should be attributed. Not until the site is re-excavated will we have the chance of knowing whether this was a walled site of the Weiman Chosŏn polity.

# *Hwando-sŏng and Kungnae-sŏng* **\(Government General of Chōsen 1915; Ikeuchi 1938)**

During the existence of the Lelang commandery (108 bc–ad 313), there lived a group of people named the Koguryŏ in the mountainous area of eastern Manchuria. The name of the Koguryŏ capital on the Yalu River (presently the border between China and North Korea) is known from both Chinese and Korean sources. In fact, there are two names: in Chinese, Wanducheng (or Korean, Hwando-sŏng); and in Chinese, Guoneicheng (or Korean, Kungnaesŏng). The last character in both implies that these names belonged to walled cities. Japanese researchers, who were the first to describe the Koguryŏ sites, had differing views on these names. K. Shiratori believed they belonged to one and the same walled city, an opinion adopted by H. Ikeuchi in his monumental study of Donggou. R. Torii believed they represented different walled cities. In both cases, Kungnae-sŏng was identified by these researchers as the squarish walled city on the north bank of the Yalu that was made the administrative city of Jian District when it was established in 1902. Torii postulated that Hwando-sŏng was located in an area 78km southwest of Kungnae-sŏng, but his own surveys revealed no walled city remains there.

One possibility overlooked by these researchers is that Hwando-sŏng was actually the large walled site in the mountains just two miles from Kungnaesŏng. This site, currently called Shanchengzi, is surrounded by an 8km wall running round the idges of a mountain valley encircling a small river's headwaters. The wall is therefore circular in plan, matching well the characters of the Chinese name Wandu, meaning "round capital". Moreover, the Koguryŏ king who founded Wandu in 207 ad is known in the Chinese chronicles as Shanshangwang, the "mountain-top-king"; the tomb of this king is also known as Shanshangling, "mountain-top-mound". When Wandu was sacked in 245 ad, the biography of the Wei General states that he "abandoned his horses and chariots and climbed the Wandu and captured the capital of Kao-kou-li" (Ikeuchi 1938: 12, in English).

This evidence argues strongly against Wandu being a square walled city located on low land. Thus it is difficult to believe that Wandu and Kungnaesŏng were one and the same city on the bank of the Yalu. It also discourages any consideration of Shiratori's suggestion that Wandu was the Chinese phonetic transcription of a Koguryŏ name which, when translated by meaning into Chinese, became Guonei (meaning "country interior"). "Wandu" seems to be an accurate Chinese description of the Koguryŏ capital and Shanchengzi is a good candidate for where it was located; what the Koguryŏ themselves called this mountain-top capital is unknown. Kungnaesŏng, however, can be understood as a second walled city established by Koguryŏ after Wandu had been sacked twice – by the Chinese Wei Dynasty expedition in 244–5 ad and the Yan forces in 342 ad – both incidents attested in the Chinese chronicles. If Wandu was the Koguryŏ capital between 207 and 342, Kungnae could have been the capital between 342 and 427. The name "Kungnae-sŏng" does not appear in textual references until after the 6th century ad and the name itself may well date from that period when the capital was located at P'yŏngyang, since it seems to be drawing a distinction between the walled city in the interior of the country and the one near the coast then serving as the capital.

# *Mongch'on T'osŏng* **\(Mongch'on 1985; Kim W. Y. et al. 1987\):**

This irregularly shaped earth-walled site, located in Seoul's Olympic Park, occupies a natural hillock on the south bank of the Han river (Figure 6.7). The earthen ramparts of Mongch'on T'osŏng follow the natural terrain of the hill promontory which forms the end of the foothills reaching down to river's edge; the bluffs had been artificially increased in height, steepened in angle, and connected together with earth-workings. This was accomplished by excavating a moat around the promontory; it is currently fed by a tributary of the Han. True tamped earth-wall construction of the Chinese style is thought to have been introduced in the Three Kingdoms period, but the earliest documented example is here at Mongch'on T'osŏng (Yun 1984). The ramparts, 2.3km in circumference, stand 10–17m in height and enclose 21.6 hectares. The walls are currently breached in nine places – at least three of which were for original gates on the north, east and south sides. A timber palisade accompanied the ramparts, enclosing 21.5 hectares; and four earthen platform foundations were laid at the highest points on the ramparts for watch towers (at 33.4m, 37.3m, 37.5m, and 44.8m m.s.l.). The bestpreserved platform was in the southeast, but no traces of buildings could be ascertained on it.

## ![](_page_239_Figure_1.jpeg)

**Figure 6.7** The walled site of Mongch'on T'osŏng (adapted from Kim W. Y. 1987)

By August 1987, 8 pit-buildings and 22 store-pits had been excavated at the site. From the presence of roof tiles, the existence of pillared buildings is also suspected, but no foundations have yet been identified. In addition to the structures were dug 4 jar burials, 5 cairns of river cobbles (underneath 4 of which were dug rectangular pits) and 2 pit-burials (one perhaps for two people), containing unusually shaped tripods, handled cups and covered pedestaled jars.

These architectural and burial facilities suggest both elite and commoner occupation. Buildings constructed with roof tiles would have functioned in elite activities, whether administrative, residential or religious. Decorated clay cylinders and a gilt bronze plaque ornament recovered from the site were perhaps used in such structures, and an inkstone clearly signifies administration. On the other hand, common domestic activities can be assessed from pit-building No. 85–1 (Table 6.3). The artifacts found in the lowest layer of earth filling the floor of the pit included a very large jar rim, a large steamer basin, the bowl of a covered bowl set, and whetstone. If these artifacts actually belonged to the structure, they suggest a domestic use of the building for food preparation and consumption and small tool maintenance.

Nevertheless, the excavators of the site hold that none of these pitbuildings served as dwellings in the normal sense (Kim W. Y. et al. 1987: 21). No. 85–1 is interpreted as a sentry house for the northeastern watchtower, being located adjacent to the latter's foundation platform. And the site director has proposed another pit-building was used as a stable, since it contained a horse skull (Im Y. J., pers. comm.). These views are based on the normative observation that pit houses were replaced by surface houses in the Proto-Three Kingdoms period; therefore, the nature of commoner occupation at the site might be postulated to be directly subservient to the elite population.

Other artifacts found throughout the site reconfirm this dualistic nature of occupation while emphasizing the economic and military activities which occurred there. The presence of elite militarists is indicated by all manner of horse equipment and weaponry: iron stirrups, horse-shoe and bit; iron spearhead and arrowheads; and bone-plate armour. Elite trade and/or contact with China resulted in several fragments of Western and Eastern Jin pottery and Six Dynasties celedon on site. Otherwise, daily subsistence activities are attested by iron sickle, knife and fishhook remains; clay spindle whorls and net weights. A sandstone mould fragment speaks of some craft production, probably of elite objects.

The date of Mongch'on is partially known from the artefacts recovered there, and partially from the early texts. Imported Chinese pottery pieces from Western Jin can be dated to the late 3rd and early 4th century, and no artifacts have been recovered from earlier periods. The texts state that occupants of the Han River basin during the 3rd to 5th centuries were the Paekche, who abandoned their capital in 475 under threat of Koguryŏ attack and moved southwest to the Kŭm River valley. Many scholars believe that Mongch'on is the actual capital of Paekche, named in the texts as Wiryesŏng. Although it fits the time period correctly, there is no archaeological way to know if this really was the Paekche capital, barring inscriptional evidence. Nevertheless, what archaeology can tell us is that Mongch'on was an active urban site of the period: urban less in the sense of population nucleation and enclosure than functional variety. The artifacts from the site include evidence for everyday subsistence activities by commoners, specialist craft production, and elite militarists. These, together with the class differences in architectural remains and the imported Western Jin ceramics, suggest there was quite a variety of people carrying on a broad range of activities at the site as would be expected at an economically and politically important urban centre.

#### **Special function sites**

# *Masan Sansŏng* **\(Munhwajae Kwalliguk 1976; Kim Y. K. & Chŏng J. W. 1971)**

Despite the early existence of Koguryŏ stone-walled sites and Chosŏn/ Lelang earthen-walled sites in the northern peninsula, no walled site yet discovered in southern Korea pre-dates the end of the 3rd century ad. Masan Sansŏng on the southern coast at Masan City is one of these. It consists of a roughly square earthen and stone wall enclosing 1.5 hectares on an isolated hill of 35–40m in height within the coastal plains. Underlying the walls are 3.5–4m of shellmound deposits dating mainly to the Late Iron Age (lst–3rd centuries ad); in the lowermost layer was discovered a Chinese wushu coin minted in the 1st century bc. The original earthen wall had been rebuilt in protohistoric times with Silla stone construction methods that are still visible in some parts of the wall which still remain. Kaya, Silla and United Silla ceramics are present in the surface layers of the site, and a coin minted in 621 ad was also recovered. The relationship between the wall and the shellmound deposits has been clearly assessed through excavation in 1974; a foundation layer of earth was packed on top of the shellmound and the wall built on top of that. This happened no earlier than 400 ad and was probably related to the hostilities accompanying the formation of the early states of Three Kingdoms period (300–668). The site was therefore fortified in its early stages and seems to have continued to have a military function in the early historic era.

## *Isŏng Sansŏng* **(Kim & Ch'im 1987; Ch'im K. J. 1988)**

Isŏng Sansŏng also shows evidence of substantial occupation but of a very different nature from the urban forms described above; it is a fortified site like Masan but much more elaborate in structure. This walled site is located high up on a mountain top overlooking the Han river valley, well placed strategically but of difficult access. In fact, this site is so inaccessible it could not possibly have functioned as a major economic and political centre, but it could have served a very important and strategic military function.

The wall is of the valley-type, encompassing 5.6 hectares, including the highest peak (209. 8m m.s.l.) to the northwest and the main watershed valley to the southeast (Figure 6.8a). There is little level ground within its confines, but by August 1987, two locations yielded two store-pits and four buildings, the latter exhibiting state architecture with foundation stones and roof tiles (Table 6.3). The two building foundations at Location E (Figure 6.8b) are notable for their regularity of construction and good preservation, in contrast to the buildings at Location C, which are of slightly rougher construction and have suffered greater destruction – perhaps cannibalization of foundation stones for other uses or downslope erosion in the more vulnerable location. All three rectangular buildings at the site are of astounding sizes: 5 by 10 to 18 bays, for total lengths of between 22 and 37m.

But even more surprising was the excavation of a nine-sided pavilion on this mountain top (Figure 6.9). It is thought by the excavator to have formed a unit with its adjoining rectangular building at Location E, and it may have fulfilled some kind of ceremonial, ritual and/or religious purpose. It is significant that the pavilion was constructed at the very edge of the site and may well have been visible over the wall to the north. Precious few artifacts exist from the site to indicate its various functions. An inkstone and inscription blocks attest to the conducting of administrative activities; but other than a pottery steamer among the ceramics, the only utilitarian items recovered were a talc spindle whorl, two whetstones, and a block of talc. These point to simple productive and maintenance activities, but there is no large artifactual component reflecting craft production, subsistence or trade as at Mongch'on T'osŏng.

The strategic nature of the site is reinforced not so much by the presence of one iron arrowhead but by the discovery of twenty-seven horse figurines. These occurred at two of four localities identified as ritual spots near large rocks. Ten were made of clay and seventeen of iron, the latter suggesting that the figures were integrated into a ritual system with substantial technical and financial support. Elsewhere in East Asia at this time, it is known that the military establishment was closely linked with ritual concerns (Teramura 1980), and it is not difficult to imagine that the deposition of horse figurines at Isŏng Sansŏng was related to its military importance.

When Isŏng Sansŏng was first excavated, it was interpreted as a Paekche site. However, the author of the second season's excavation report (Ch'im K. J. 1988) attributes the wall to the Silla period. Paekche pottery, and iron objects discovered in the second season – axe, sickle and crucibles – indicate that the subsistence and production component of the site is more comparable to Mongch'on T'osŏng than previously thought. However, an increased ritual component (stone mirror imitation and talc perforated pieces) and lack of trade goods still sharply distinguish it from the latter site. There are thus differing interpretations of Isŏng Sansŏng: that it was originally a Paekche site and was overtaken by Silla and made into a militaristic outpost with a heavy ritual significance; or that it was always a Silla site. Regardless, it might be said that the two sites of Isŏng Sansŏng and Mongch'on T'osŏng might have functioned within different settlement systems at different times and that Isŏng Sansŏng had a ritual component that was not present at Mongch'on T'osŏng.

#### *Puso Sansŏng* **(Yun M. B. 1982; Sŏng J. T. 1984\)**

Puso Sansŏng, also called Sabisŏng, is located in the Kŭm river drainage at the modern town of Puyŏ. It encloses 237 hectares including three peaks of an isolated mountain (106m m.s.l.), and is currently connected with long walls that extend to the southwest and far to the southeast (Figure 6.10 above). Just below the site at the base of the southern slope stands the Puyŏ National Museum and the preserved office of a Yi Dynasty local administrator; the town of Puyŏ spreads out beyond. Three gates led into the walled site on the east, south and west; while the northern wall is broken by gate and canal remains. The interior of the site is further partitioned by three more walls: one encloses the southeastern-most peak, which contains the remains of military storage granaries and a lookout tower; the second adjoins to that on the southwest; and a third encircles two peaks each occupied by a watchtower in the northwest (Figure 6.10 below). This walled site is said to be a combination-type construction (Song J. T. 1984), but it appears from the scale drawings that the internal partitions were built subsequent to the valleyencircling wall, rather than the walls being extended from original summitringing sansŏng walls.

![](_page_246_Figure_1.jpeg)

**Figure 6.10** The fortress remains at the Paekche capital of Sabi

## Solid lines = Walls

Excavations at building foundations in the southeastern precinct have revealed carbonized rice and red beans, and rows of foundation stones for four buildings (Table 6.3), two of which were also surrounded by low stone walls or barriers. These buildings have been interpreted as military stores; and from the roof tiles around the area, it is known that they were first built in the Three Kingdoms period but were repaired and reused throughout the historical periods. The foundations remaining today show their most recent phase of construction in the Chosŏn period.

Further excavations near the southwestern wall have uncovered 3 pitbuildings, 3 large store-pits and a double row of palisade postholes. The pitbuildings, 4x4m square, were equipped with stone facilities thought to be parts of hearths along one interior wall. Building No. 3 also had a stonelined flue arrangement leading from the hearth to the outside, and a set of two steps leading to the interior. The usage of the pit-buildings has been hypothesized by the excavators as militaristic by analogy with similar pitbuildings at Taga-jō Fort in northern Japan (cf. Kidder 1972; Tōkyō Rekishi Shiryōkan 1985). No direct evidence, however, exists to support this hypothesis except for their humble construction inside the walled site.

Southwest of the buildings, part of the wall was excavated, revealing three successive construction techniques and a double row of postholes just inside the wall. These are thought to have belonged to a palisade constructed prior to the wall, the latter of which basically consisted of a low rank of stones laid as the front foundation and several layers of clay and sandy earth tamped on top. In the ground by the wall were discovered an iron sword with bronze pommel and fragments of iron-plate armour. These contrast greatly with the ornamental nature of two items found in the nearby store-pits: a glass curved bead on a copper thread and a gilt bronze sculpture of a creature's head mounted on a wood support. The site also yielded a clay inkstone fragment. All the above objects are associated with aristocratic occupation and represent military, administrative and decorative functions.

It is striking, however, that as of the late 1980s no productive artifacts had yet been found at Puso Sansŏng and no artifacts are related to daily subsistence activities. Granaries at the site indicate it performed the function of a stronghold, integrated into the administrative and military network of the Paekche state, but questions remain about the actual occupation inside the walls. In the least, there is no evidence that Puso Sansŏng formed a main economic center. This suggests a very restricted use of the walled site, and indeed, despite the elite ornaments suggesting residence and palace architecture, archaeologists believe that the Paekche palace of Sabi was located south of the walled site on the terrace where the museum and historic building now sit. Excavations there revealed lotus corms which are thought to indicate a pond in the palace gardens. It is also possible that most economic activity occurred beyond the palace, in the area today covered by the town of Puyŏ.

#### **Gidded cities**

The walled site served its several purposes in early state society on the Korean peninsula until the late 6th or 7th century when the Tang style of gidded city plan was imported from China. In imitating the layout of the Tang capital of Chang'an, it was discovered that all the necessary elements of a Chinese gidded city could not fit inside the traditional walled site of the early Korean state. Archaeological and historical studies both point to the traditional walled site as becoming a secondary element in capital layout from this time onward. Two sites illustrate this process: the Paekche capital at Puyŏ and the Silla capital of Kŭmsŏng.

The Paekche state had its capital in the Han river basin between ad 300 and 474, but then moved it south to Ungjin (modern Kongju), and from there in 538 to Sabi (modern Puyo) – both in the Kŭm river basin. The Sabi capital was positioned in a bend of the Paengma River, a tributary to the Kŭm River in the southwestern peninsula. The street gid was laid out on the flat land encompassed by the river bend on the western side, but it was sandwiched among numerous small hills, each of which was topped by an encircling fortress wall. Long walls akin to the Great Wall of China stretched between these mountain fortresses to encircle the capital on its northern, western and eastern sides. Thus, although the gidded city itself did not have a city wall that defined its rectangular perimeter, it was protected by both the mountaintop fortresses and the interconnecting walls. This was the beginning of the "dual defensive system" that characterised city layouts up until the modern era in Korea.

A similar layout of the Silla capital can be seen near the modern city of Kyŏngju. A central walled site of the early Sillan state sits in the center of the Kyŏngju basin on an isolated hill promontory beside the South Stream, a tributary to the major Hyŏngsan River. This walled site, named Panwŏlsŏng meaning "half moon fortress", is presumed to have been the early Sillan capital. Its interior has never been excavated – although soil marks from foundation stones can be seen in spots of bare ground – and no inscriptions clearly identifying it have ever been found. Recent excavations have concentrated on the areas abutting the site's walls on the outside. Foundation stone alignments of very large buildings have been recovered to the north, and the remains of a United Silla bidge have been excavated at a river crossing on the site's southwestern edge. The 7th-century gid city was laid out northwest of Panwŏlsŏng, and Kyŏngju today retains some of the street layout. In contrast to Puyŏ, the Sillan capital had no surrounding walls but mountain fortresses at each of the basin's major access points served to protect it.

#### **Three Kingdoms settlement patterns**

Among all the sites yet excavated, Mongch'on T'osŏng conforms most closely to our expectations of an economic and residential center. The earthen wall is thought to have been built under the influence of the Chinese commandaries in north central Korea, except that it follows natural topographic contours rather than geometric shapes. It seems that the urban pattern developed here was similar to Chinese political centers of the Zhou period, incorporating both public buildings and economic activity within the walls. When the Paekche capital was removed to the southwest, however, economic and administrative functions appear to have been separated, with only the latter enclosed within the walls. Below the walled site on Mt. Puso, we can expect to find the diversified economic and productive activities which accompanied state-level political organization.

It is not yet known whether Mongch'on T'osŏng was also a Paekche "capital". It was clearly an important site in Paekche's early stage of existence, but no "palace" remains have been identified. The importance here of determining the presence of elite females in order to assess the location of a palace should be stressed. Long term occupation by elite females would be expected at polity centers and sub-centers only; short-term occupation may occur at defensive refuges, but no elite females would be expected at military outposts (although other females might be present). In contrast, male elite presence would be expected at any of these sites except for enclosed villages.

The absence of elite female objects at Isŏng Sansŏng argues against its use as a royal refuge as has been postulated in the literature (Bacon 1961). However, an argument can be made on the basis of location and the ceremonial remains found there that this site performed an extremely important symbolic and administrative function for the Silla state. The fact that the nine-sided pavilion could be seen from the north, the direction from which incursions by Koguryŏ occurred in the 5th century, may have been significant in Silla's establishment of identity and territorial claims in the face of a hostile neighbor. The horse ceremonialism indicates the strategic military importance of the site, while some daily production activities suggest occupation by a certain number of individuals. This site can thus be interpreted as subsidiary to the capital, perhaps a sub-center with ideological, administrative and military functions but no economic role to speak of.

Through this analysis, then, we have determined that walled sites served as economic and administrative centers and sub-centers in the Three Kingdoms period; but without data on elite female residence and actual palace architecture, it is difficult to determine whether some of these were polity "capitals". Nevertheless, it is clear that their specific functions have to be interpreted from artifactual repertoires in addition to the location of the site and the presence of a wall per se. It has been shown that such walled sites neither have single determinable functions nor follow one spatial pattern in layout, and the examples known and discussed here probably do not represent the entire universe of walled sites either in structure or function. Each must be investigated individually; ultimately, they cannot be interpreted outside their historical contexts.

## **Chapter Seven**

# Introducing Kaya History and Archaeology\*

#### **Abstract**

Kaya is an enigma in Korean state formation since the Kaya chiefdoms never seemed to coalesce into a strong, centralised state. Nevertheless, the economic position of the early Kaya polities in stoneware and iron production meant they were alternately courted and fought over by the more powerful states surrounding them. This chapter examines the origins of the Kaya from historical and archaeological sources, and provides the context for viewing the industrial data.

#### Introduction

As with all the early states on the Korean peninsula, one of the major problems in reviewing Kaya history is knowing when it began. This problem derives from Kaya being "protohistoric", that is, overlapping the boundary between prehistory and history. The very word "Kaya" comes from a historical source, and it is not clear how far back into prehistory it can be extended. Of course, the Naktong river valley and the southern coastal region around modern Pusan – the historical locus of Kaya in the southeastern peninsula – have an archaeological history going back thousands of years; however, when and how the Kaya emerged from this prehistorical background is a matter for conjecture.

Before diving into the historical and archaeological data, one qualification should be made. Most of the historical sources do not discuss Kaya as a holistic entity; from the earliest historical records, different regional groupings of Kaya are recognised. Thus, much of the discussion is cast within the context of specific socio-political entities. Nevertheless, the material culture over much of the Naktong river valley and south coast forms a coherent unit that can be contrasted with Paekche culture in the west and later Silla culture in the east. Thus, objects can be termed "Kayastyle" without too much difficulty; but when talking of Kaya people or politics, regional entities are usually used. It is unclear from historical sources whether the Kaya thought of themselves as a homogeneous ethnic group, but it is certain that the various regional groupings never did coalesce into a strong, single, centralized state comparable to the main Three Kingdoms of Silla, Paekche and Koguryŏ.

As outlined in an earlier chapter, the southern Korean peninsula had a rather different developmental trajectory from the northern half. Iron came to it late; thus the Bronze Age lasted longer, until ca. 0 ad. During the first three centuries ad, the southern peninsula was dominated by the Chinese commandery of Lelang and seems to have been divided into three ethnic districts – which we read about in the Chinese chronicles as Mahan (southwest), Chinhan (southeast) and Pyŏnhan (directly south). It is the archaeology of this Three Han (Samhan) period that reveals the emergence of the historical entity of Kaya. However, since the traditional founding date of Kaya is stated in the later native histories to have been ad 42, Samhan archaeology is often treated as belonging to Kaya itself. By equating Samhan archaeology with Kaya history – or alternatively, by excluding Samhan archaeology from Kaya history – the nature of Kaya as a historical entity is greatly altered. This is a matter of scholarly interpretation, and so there exist two major schools of thought concerning when Kaya appeared and how long it lasted.

#### The Samguk Yusa account of Kaya origins

## The "Karak Kukki" chapter

The main historical source on Kaya is the *Karak Kukki*, "Karak State Record", an 11th-century document presumably written by a magistrate of Kumgwan County (near Kimhae, just west of modern Pusan) during the reign of the Koryo King Munjong (r. 1046–83).2 The original text of the *Karak Kukki* no longer exists, but its content was partially incorporated as a separate chapter into the 13th-century *Samguk Yusa* by Ilyon.3 The *Karak Kukki* chapter contains two versions of the legendary establishment of the Karak State: one brief version quoted from a stone stele inscription, and another longer more elaborate version. The stele inscription possibly dates to the mid-5th century, and the stele itself appears to have been still extant in the 11th century for the inscription to be read by the magistrate and incorporated into his text. The more detailed version was recorded by the magistrate in the 11th century and probably contains many historical elaborations on the Karak legends. It is not clear how much of an editorial hand Ilyŏn exerted 200 years later within the *Karak Kukki* chapter; he states at the beginning that the text is given in an abbreviated form, and some annotations appear in the running text. The text itself, however, gives some alternatives, for example, stating that the first Karak king was called Suro or Suryŏng and that Karak was also called Kaya. By the 11th century, then, it seems that Karak and Kaya were used interchangeably.

The majority of the Karak Kukki chapter is devoted to the miraculous birth of King Suro and another five Kaya kings (hatched from six golden eggs delivered from heaven in a golden basket on a purple rope), and the affairs of King Suro and his obtaining of a wife. Thereafter, the text relates United Silla and Koryŏ-period events concerning the upkeep of King Suro's tomb, and it tells of the festival (continuing into the 11th-century in the old Kaya region of Kimhae) commemorating the arrival of King Suro's future wife by ship. The region's change of name through the centuries is also recorded: from being called Kümgwan-kyŏng in 681, to Imhae-hyŏn in ca. 940, to Imhae-gun or Kimhae-pu in ca. 988. During the Koryŏ period, the area served intermittently as navy and army headquarters. Very little is written about the events of King Suro's successors except that in 453 the eighth King Kimchil4 had a temple erected to the memory of King Suro's Queen, and the stele on which the inscription mentioned above was written apparently stood on the abandoned site of that temple. A king list is appended to the Karak Kukki, giving reign dates, genealogies and alternative names and some historical information for the nine kings succeeding Suro. In this list, some information is incorporated from the Chinese Kaihuangli.5 Finally, the document ends by quoting two Chinese sources, the Kaibuanglu6 and the Sanguozhi, 7 on the Silla conquest of Karak.

Some things to note about the *Karak Kukki* chapter are that:

1 The name of King Suro's kingdom is usually given as Karak-guk (with Kaya-guk given as an alternative only once). A nearby mountain, however, is called Mt. Kaya, and King Suro's contemporaries are referred to as five

Kaya kings. It thus appears that Kaya might have been a local place name and ethnic identity, whereas Karak was specifically chosen by King Suro as the name for his own kingdom. Another explanation is that Kaya is simply a later derivation of Karak with no difference in meaning (see below).

- 2 The traditional founding date of Karak in ad 42 is taken from the day according to the Chinese dynastic calendar (Jian-wu 18 of Emperor Guang Wu's reign in the Late Han Dynasty) when the heavenly voice called down to nine chiefs telling them to "dance and meet a great king", with the golden basket descending thereafter. King Suro subsequently is said to have married in ad 48.
- 3 Predecessors of King Suro are mentioned in two places in the *Karak Kukki* chapter. The beginning of the document specifically says that the country was originally without a name or ruler, but then it goes on to list the names of nine original "chiefs" (*kan*) who controlled an apocryphal 75,000 people of 100 surnames living in 100 households in the region. These "chiefs" are discussed later in the text as government officials within the Karak state. Thus if these people existed at all, they were not ancestors but contemporaries of King Suro.
- 4 Under King Suro, court titles were revised, both to modernize the "nine chiefs" titles and adopt new titles from the Court of Kyerim (Silla), such as Kakkan, Ajilkan and Kŭpkan. It is possible that the *-kan* ending was adopted at this time and added post-facto to the original native titles. In any case, these sorts of titles were not prominent in Silla until the reign of King Naemul (356–402) when Naemul adopted the title of Maripkan (Lee K. B. 1984: 30), making their appearance at the time of King Suro anachronistic unless of course, they were adopted into Silla *from* Kaya.
- 5 King Suro's capital was built in a narrow valley at Kŭmyang and is said to have consisted of outer city walls, royal palaces, government offices, armouries and storehouses. The boundaries of Karak were considered to be Hwangsan River on the east (the Naktong River?), the ocean on the south, Ch'anghae (location unknown) on the southwest, Mt. Chiri on the northwest and Mt. Kaya on the northeast (Figure 7.1). The historical name of Kŭmgwan probably was used officially in the United Silla period for the old Karak capital.

6 King Suro's wife is said to have come from India, in particular from "Ayut'a", an attribution mentioned several times in the text (see below).

7 The king list in the *Karak Kukki*, gives the surname of Kim (meaning "gold") to the nine kings succeeding Suro. In the entry for the second King Kŏdŭng, it states that the Chinese document, the *Kaihuangli*, explains the surname as deriving from the gold egg from which hatched the king's ancestor. However, we know from the text of the *Karak Kukki* chapter itself that the royal Karak line was absorbed into the Kim royal line of Silla upon the defeat of Karak (Ryu H. R. 1989); the 30th Silla King Munmu claims descent from the Crown Prince of the last Karak King Kuhŏng. It is not clear from these data whether the Karak kings called themselves Kim at the time; the surname may well have been applied retroactively after King Munmu's ascension to the Silla throne.

8 An important point to note here is that nowhere in the *Samguk Yusa* does any other Kaya name appear as a "country" (-guk, the Korean pronunciation of guo) as does the original Karak-guk.

#### The "Five Kaya" chapter

Interestingly, the *Karak Kukki* chapter itself does not give the names of any other Kaya entities, even though there were five other Kaya kings mentioned in another short chapter elsewhere in the *Samguk Yusa* entitled "The Five Kaya"; this chapter gives two different versions of the names and annotations on their supposed locations (cf. Table 7.1).

![](_page_260_Figure_0.jpeg)

**Figure 7.1** Distribution of the classical "Five Kaya" and Karak (Kŭmgwan-Kaya) with their geographical referents (after Ryu 1989)

1 Sŏngsan-Kaya; 2 Tae-Kaya; 3 Ara-Kaya; 4 Koryŏng-Kaya; 5 So-Kaya; 6 Kŭmgwan-Kaya

The "main" list may have been extracted from the *Karak Kukki* to stand alone in the "The Five Kaya" chapter, but the *Samguk Yusa* does not specifically say so. In any case, Karak is not mentioned on the main list presumably because it was not one of the Five Kaya – it was additional to them (there were 6 eggs!) – or because, as suggested above, Karak designated a political entity but Kaya referred to ethnic groups. However, by the Koryŏ period, the Kaya list included Karak as Kŭmgwan-Kaya and a different set of other Kaya but still to total Five Kaya. Today, most scholarship refers to Karak as Kŭmgwan-Kaya or as Pon-Kaya, meaning the "original Kaya".

That chronological chart has less information on each king than the list in the *Karak Kukki* chapter, but an entry has been added on King Suro himself for the chart. The new entry on King Suro places him in Kaya at Kŭmju (Kimhae), suggesting a different 13th-century name for that area. A simplistic rendition of the six Kaya can be seen in Figure 7.1, although the scholastic situation is much more complicated and will be discussed again below.

## A critique of King Suro's Queen

Archaeologist Kim Byung-mo has offered a proposal for the origin for King Suro's queen which, if true, relies on an amazing concordance of archaeological, historiographical and ethnographical data. Beginning with the "twin-fish" motif occurring on historical monuments throughout the present-day Kimhae region, including the stone monument of the queen's alleged tomb, he has followed up the *Karak Kukki's* attribution of the queen to India and found that the twin-fish motif was prominent in the design repertoire in Kosala in Uttar Pradesh in the early centuries ad. He equates Ayut'a with Ayodhia, a common place name of that region and time. Also on the queen's tomb monument was the epithet "Princess of Chinju"; Kim traces this title together with her name as stated in the *Karak Kukki*, Hŏ Hwang-ok, to the ancestral home of the Hŏ (C. Xu/Hu) clan in Sichuan Province of China – located in a prefecture which was called Chinju (C. Jinzhou) from the Zhou to Song periods.

When Kim travelled to Chinju, he met members of the Xu/Hu families who guided him to the ancestral tomb and was told of the yearly ceremonies carried out there. Moreover, he documented the distribution of the twin-fish motif throughout the Yangtze basin and postulates that the Xu/Hu clan was originally a Brahmin family from India which took refuge to the northeast when Kosala was attacked by Kushan in the 1st century ad. Finally, he cites two rebellions in Jinzhou recorded for ad 47 and 101 in the Late Han period. One of the leaders of the rebellion was a holy man of the Xu/Hu; and, judging on the expulsion of the rebels in 101, Kim postulates that the earlier Brahman leader was also banished.

From here he speculates that the family moved down the Yangtze to the Shanghai delta region, whence they might have departed on a trading ship that deposited them on the southern Korean coast. It must be remembered at this time, as described by the later Weizhi, the Pyŏnhan area was a large trading centre for iron with many ships coming and going from all directions, so it would not have been impossible to arrive from the Yangtze delta in time for the daughter to marry King Suro in ad 48. The name chosen by Suro for his kingdom, Karak, is said to be an old Dravidian word for "fish", with Kaya as the new Dravidian form (Kim. B. Y. 1987, 1992, 1994).

This incredible coincidence of data might well reflect the reality of a high status foreign family arriving in the Kimhae region at the date described. If so, foreign beads and ornaments might be expected to be discovered from Pyŏnhan burials in future. It is also possible that the daughter's presence was remembered through myth and legend. However, whether or not this woman married the man to become King Suro is still conjectural. It should be noted that the twin-fish motif is at present only known from historical monuments in the Kimhae region and it has yet to be found on any archaeological materials dating to the Kaya period. Also, the tomb that is maintained as belonging to the queen of King Suro is a late construction (as is likely for King Suro's tomb), located in a 6–7th century tomb cluster (Nishitani 1992a: 2). It is possible that after the Silla conquest of Kaya in 532, the Silla Court honoured the ancestors of the Kaya Crown Prince who joined the Kim royal line by building new a new tomb for the queen; and a tombstone with the twin-fish motif could have been added any time thereafter. Fish forms are known in Silla goldwork, which is also thought to have roots in Indian gold-working technology. Thus, there is more than one route for the "twin-fish" motif to have entered the Korean peninsula.

Another problem with Kim Byung-mo's neat scenario is that there is no archaeological evidence, as stated above, for the establishment of a "kingdom" in the 1st century ad. If King Suro's time was instead the mid-3rd century, and particularly if he were a refugee from Lelang or the Puyŏ (C. Fuyu), it is still possible that later Kaya dynasts put together a surviving myth of an Indian woman together with stories of the first king's exploits and made them man and wife. This would be in the nature of myth-making to give a history and therefore legitimacy to a new ruling class on the southern peninsula.

Before delving into further historical references to Kaya, let us look at the emergence of Kaya from an archaeological point of view.

#### An archaeological definition of Kaya emergence

Most archaeologists working on the protohistoric cultures of the peninsula accept and work within the ethnic names and social concepts derived from the early documents. They do not insist on the anonymity of the protohistoric record even though there are, for example, no inscriptions found during excavation that declare "this is a Kaya site". Nevertheless, the assignment of material remains to ethnic groups is done at the discretion of the individual archaeologist, and interpretations do vary.

Even if one accepts the transformation of the Samhan-period Kuya-guk into the Three Kingdoms-period Kaya, it is difficult to accept the *Karak Kukki* foundation date and the ascension of King Suro in the mid-1st century ad because there are no archaeological remains from that time in the Kaya region that indicate the emergence of an elite ruling class capable of producing a king. Not until the late–3rd century does such evidence appear, and archaeologist Shin Kyung Cheol has argued for the emergence of Kümgwan-Kaya at that time (1992a, b). The evidence consists of the appearance of a certain type of wooden chamber burial in the Kimhae region, as known at the Taesŏng-ri, Pokchŏn-dong and Yangnae-ri cemeteries excavated in the 1990s. These new chambers differ from the earlier wooden chamber burials not only in construction but size, location and richness of grave goods. For the first time, status distinctions can be seen in the interment of individuals, including the deposition of sacrificed persons.8

To construct his view of Kaya emergence, Shin relies on his excavations at Taesŏng-dong in Kimhae, which is considered the royal cemetery of Kŭmgwan-Kaya (1995). He distinguishes between wooden-board burial chamber construction of the Lelang style, which he assigns to Pyŏnhan peoples, and the round-timber chamber construction, which he recognises as a northern type. The former, Type I wooden chamber burials yield Chinese and Kyūshū-made mirrors and date at the earliest to the late 2nd century ad. The latter, Type II wooden chamber burials contain both human and horse sacrifices, horseider equipment and armour. Moreover, when Type II burials were constructed in the various cemeteries around Kimhae, many Type I burials were intentionally destroyed. From the northern-type two-handled pots appearing at this time, Shin dates the appearance of Type II burials to no earlier than ad 280. He goes so far to identify the source of this northern culture as the Fuyu (K. Puyŏ) in the northern Manchurian basin (modern Jilin Province). Furthermore, he argues for the migration of peoples rather than the spread of material culture through trade. Specifically, he notes that the new culture arrives suddenly in the Kimhae area and is not the product of a gradual transmission overland. He equates the initiation of Type II burials with the emergence of Kaya, and consciously gives a northern derivation to the Kŭmgwan-Kaya ruling line.

Shin's thesis on the northern source of Kŭmgwan-Kaya leaders is not well accepted in archaeological circles despite the fact that the Puyŏ (C. Fuyu) peoples are also claimed to be the source of Koguryŏ and Paekche ruling groups on the Korean peninsula. Opponents do not dispute the northern nature of the material goods, but they argue for trade and/or influence instead of migration. Ch'oi Byŏng-hyŏn, for example, suggests that the round-timber structure and the northern grave goods are influences from Silla, which served as a funnel for northern influences through the southern peninsula (Ch'oi B. H. 1994). This thesis, however, requires the primacy of Silla over Kaya at this time, which is controversial in itself see [Chapter 8, this volume]. Another objection is that the appearance of round timbers in burial chambers is not a sudden occurrence; they occur in the Choyang-dong cemetery in Kyŏngju (l–3c ad) and elsewhere sporadically before Taesŏng-dong. To this can be added my observation that round timbers require much less work than making boards; their use may just represent a re-deployment of labour and/or expense in other directions. Furthermore, the intentional destruction of earlier burials is widely seen in southern cemeteries. The cutting into of old grave pits by new burials is common to both Type I and Type II chambers and indicates a lack of concern on the part of early Kaya peoples to keep ancestral burials intact. The overlapping of graves is an important tool for constructing the chronology of cemetery formation, and most Yŏngnam archaeologists do not see a conscious political intention of destruction in such burial successions. Finally, Shin's chronology does not add up for a coordinated invasion. Though the earliest Type II chambers date to the terminal 3rd century, northern-type horse trappings do not appear among the grave goods until third quarter of the 4th century (Shin 1994: 283–4). This is approaching the time period of Koguryŏ's invasions of the south coast, and the relationship between these burial goods and Koguryŏ must be more thoroughly investigated before an independent and prior Puyŏ/Fuyu presence can be established.

Finally, in addition to the northern goods found in the burials at Taesŏng-dong, objects imported from the Japanese islands have been excavated: bronze staff ferrules, bronze spiral bosses, and talc imitation objects consistent with the 4th-century Yamato grave goods repertoire in the Kinai region. These point to interaction between the Kŭmgwan-Kaya rulers and the Yamato kings rather than with Silla. It is significant that Shin does not equate these artifacts with invading Yamato forces as he does the northern artifacts with Puyŏ/Fuyu populations. However, one Japanese scholar, Suzuki Yasutami, has tried to turn Shin's thesis around and actually argues that the Taesŏng-dong burials are evidence of Yamato presence on the peninsula – migration in the opposite direction (Suzuki 1992). However, the Yamato artefacts at Taesŏng-dong are low frequency ornamental goods that cannot reasonably be equated with either a migrating population (except perhaps by marriage) or military incursion.

Despite these various attributions of Kaya ethnicity, it is clear that the Type II wood-chamber burials at Taesŏng-dong embody a shift in both economic and social relations in southern coastal society. Trade affiliations were extended and the incoming goods belonged to elite prestige good networks. The participation of the Kaya iron-traders in these networks served to enhance their status, allowing them to trade as peers with the emerging elites in the surrounding pen/insular societies. This must necessarily have marked a change from the earlier socio-political dynamics of iron supply from a coastal manufacturing site under the heavy scrutiny of the Lelang commandery which was obligated to supply iron to China as tribute. Thus, it is reasonable to define the emergence of Kaya, as different from P'yŏn-Han, on the basis of this change in the archaeological culture.

#### The development of Kaya according to T. S. Kim

Kim T. S. has proposed that Kaya history consists of early and late components (1987). In his scheme, Early Kaya (lst–4th centuries) centered on Kümgwan-Kaya and equates with the Pyŏnhan *guo* as well as the shellmound and iron production remains on the southern coast. Late Kaya (5–6th centuries) equates with the mounded tomb remains of the upper Naktong River valley. Kim sees a shift in power and territorial importance between these two phases, thus giving considerable time depth to the importance assigned to the two Kaya polities of coastal Kümgwan-Kaya and inland Tae-Kaya. In developing his scheme, he has collated a tremendous number of historical references to Kaya and has tried to correlate these with archaeological remains in different regions of the Naktong River basin.

In addition to the Samguk Yusa, scattered references to the Kaya area exist in a number of different sources: the medieval Korean Samguk Sagi, written in Chinese in the 12th century; the *Nihon Shoki*, compiled in Japan in the 8th century in Chinese; the Chinese Weizhi, written in the late 3rd century, the Kwanggaet'o stele of 414, and the mid-15th century Koryŏsa, etc. These sources all give outsiders' views of Kaya and there is no historical documentation – except for the above mentioned stele inscription at the temple site – from any of the Kaya polities themselves. A general problem in correlating these sources is determining equivalencies in names. Kim Tae-sik has contributed the main analyses of these sources, giving tentative locations for some of the 13 Pyŏn-Chin -guk (countries) as listed in the Weizhi (Kim T. S. 1991: table 6), identifying 13 different transcriptions for the name "Kaya" using Chinese characters and their frequency of occurrence in different sources (ibid.: table 1), equating names and places for the 6 Kaya given in the *Samguk Yusa* (ibid.: table 2), providing sources for the name "Imna" (ibid.: table 3), and listing the locations for 13 different Late Kaya -guk (countries) (ibid.: table 8).

Among these various names, Nam-Kaya and Imna deserve special mention. In the *Nihon Shoki* of Japan, Kŭmgwan-Kaya is referred to as Nam-Kaya (South Kaya), presumably in contrast to Tae-Kaya in the north, which it refers to simply as Kaya. Thus, by the 6th century when historical records began to be kept in Japan, the main Kaya entity dealt with was the later manifestation of Tae-Kaya. Imna is a name that appears most frequently in the Japanese sources (215 times in the *Nihon Shoki* and 7 times in the *Shinsen Shoji-roku*, "A record of select clans" compiled in ad 815), but it appears earliest on the Kwanggaet'o stele of 414 as "Imna-Kara"; it also appears on the Chingyŏng Taesat'ap stele and in several Chinese sources (Kim T. S. 1991: table 3). The *Song-shu* treats it as a separate country from Kaya, and Kim concludes that it is a 5th–6th century name of one of several small countries on the southern Korean peninsula that were affiliated with neither Silla nor Paekche (1991: 14–5).

The fruits of Kim's labour can be seen in Figures 7.2 and 7.3, showing the distributions of polity names and sites. The first map, including the Samhan period names of the Pyŏnhan and Chin-han areas includes Kaya under the Samhan name of Kuya in Kimhae. Only 9 of the 13 Samhan names are locatable in the Yŏngnam region, but 18 cemeteries dating from the 1st to 4th centuries ad are seen to be distributed in several of these polity areas. Kim's data for Late Kaya are set out in Figure 7.3; here all 13 Late Kaya (5–6th c. ad) polities are mapped against 26 cemeteries known of this date. Many of the cemeteries are assigned to particular Kaya polities on the basis of geographical coherence, but these affiliations should be tested or thoroughly scrutinised rather than taken at face value.

It should be noted that the number of Late Kaya polity names far exceed the original Five Kaya recorded in the *Samguk Yusa*. Four of the original names are now listed under their original or new names (see Table 7.1, right-hand column): Ara-Kaya, Kŭmgwan-Kaya became Nam-Kaya; Tae-Kaya became Kaya; So-Kaya became Koja-Kaya. Also, Pihwa-Kaya has been elevated to independent status as Pijahwa; and Koryŏng-Kaya and Songsan-Kaya have disappeared. Beyond these, 9 new names have been added. It is apparent that many of these new names are drawn from the Japanese chronicles, the *Nihon Shoki*, compiled in the 8th century. Although these chronicles postdated the Late Kaya period by two to three centuries, records of interaction between the Late Kaya polities and Yamato have been preserved therein. The role of the Chinese court documents, so important for earlier windows onto Samhan and Early Kaya society, had ended by this time; and the indigenous Korean documents of the early historical period (Samguk Sagi, Samguk Yusa, Poncho Sarak, and Koryŏsa) tended to continue the use of traditional names in opposition to the Japanese tendency for re-naming. Kim draws upon the Nihon Shoki to provide the broadest, most varying view of Late Kaya polity distribution.

## **Refinements on Early" and "Late" Kaya**

It can be seen from the above discussion of the Taesŏng-dong cemetery composition that Kim's Early Kaya period can be refined to distinguish between the Pyŏnhan iron-producing society under Lelang influence and the Kaya society which arose after the fall of Lelang in the early 4th century. A further refinement can be made towards the end of the Kim's Early Kaya period in that at the end of the 4th century, the Kimhae region where the Taesŏng-dong cemetery was located declined and activity shifted to the east bank of the Naktong River in the area of modern Tongnae within Pusan City. This area supports the Pokch'ŏng-dong cemetery which was used for several centuries during Kaya development. It particularly becomes flourescent in the 5th century with the construction of large wooden chamber tombs yielding iron armour see [Chapter 5, this volume].

#### ϕ unmapped

Like the larger Three Kingdoms, most of our information about Kaya comes from burials, which give us yet more definitions of "Kaya" to work with. Two burial attributes are particularly important: the appearance of Kaya-type stoneware and the new tradition of pit-style stone-chamber construction. The study of each of these categories has fueled considerable controversy over their dating schemes, so that there can be wide differences among scholars in the interpretions of the burial remains.

The advent of stoneware production in the Kaya region is based on the excavations of the Puwŏn-dong shellmound site. Here there occurs a new shape repertoire of hard-fired grey ware that became the Three Kingdoms stoneware tradition in Yŏngnam. Analyses show that this grey ware is not yet fully fired to be stoneware see [Chapter 4, this volume], but the beginnings of the tradition are evident. The earlier Kimhae type of pottery, now incorporated into the Wajil category as the representative ceramics of the Samhan period, was considered by some archaeologists to include stoneware firing; but in general, Wajil is a hard-fired earthenware of a quite different shape repertoire than Three Kingdoms stoneware see [Chapter 3, this volume]. Different groups of archaeologists have postulated the advent of stoneware anywhere from the early 1st century bc to the terminal 3rd century ad. Based on my investigation of the problem, the latter date will be adopted for the discussion here.

There is also debate concerning the stimulus for Kaya-type stonechamber construction. These chambers can be built in a variety of ways, with upright stone slabs to form a stone box (a cist), or with horizontally laid stone slabs in a dry-wall construction, or with boulders serving as wall stones and infilled with smaller stones. Studies of these types of burial structures in Yŏngnam find a surprising unity of construction (Kang H. S. 1990). Despite different floor treatment (paved or unpaved) and wall construction (slabstone or cist-style), 4th- and 5th-century stone chambers are of a kind quite distinct than that used after the beginning of the 6th century (with plastered walls and pitched roofs). In any case, the walls in pit-style stone chambers are intentionally constructed (Figure 7.4) and therefore differ greatly from the Silla tradition of mounded-stone burial see [Chapter 8, this volume]. Early burials in the Silla tradition are constructed as a pit in which a wooden chamber is placed, and then the pit is filled with stones and the whole burial mounded over with stones. Later burials have only a shallow depression in which the wooden chamber is placed or it is placed on level ground; then the chamber is mounded over with stones and then earth.

Choi B. H. postulates that Kaya-type stone-chamber construction results from the Silla influence of mounded-stone construction (1990, 1994). Here a northeastern Chinese derivation is proposed (Hong 1994). In none of these discussions has Japan been cited as a possible source even though pitstyle stone chamber tombs were widespread in the western Japanese Islands in the 4th century. Conversely, Yŏngnam archaeologists believe the Silla tradition to post-date the Kaya tradition by up to a century, and Shin K. C. (1993) postulates that the Silla burial type actually receives the influence of contemporary Yŏngnam wooden chamber construction for use in its stonepiled tombs. The different dating schemes for the Silla burials have been reviewed by Lee C. S. (1991), resulting in three different groupings of archaeological dating. The earliest stone-mounded wood-chamber burial in the Kyŏngju region is universally agreed to be Hwangnam-dong #109, which happens to be designated a royal tomb. Early chronologies made no distinctions between the different graves in #109, but recent datings distinguish between #109–1, 2 (late) and #109–3, 4 (early). 10 Current dating estimates for the early graves, based purely on relative and intuitive chronologies, vary between ca. 320 (Group 1 dating); ca. 410–20 (Group 2), and ca. 350–410 (Group 3). Thus, the early graves can be dated anywhere between ad 320 and 410 see [Chapter 8, this volume]. In the following discussion, a date of early 5th century will be adopted, based on other evidence that the emergence of a Silla elite in the visible archaeological record did not occur until the early 5th century. In contrast, the appearance of Kaya-type stone burials happened earlier, in the mid-4th century.

![](_page_280_Figure_0.jpeg)

**Figure 7.4** The Kaya pit-style stone chamber of tomb #38 in the Kyesŏng Tomb Cluster Loc. B in Ch'angnyŏn; the top has been destroyed through land-use activities (after Pusan University Museum 1995: fig. 120)

Burials which have both Kaya-type stoneware and pit-style stone chamber burials are considered to be Kaya; these occur throughout the Naktong River basin but not in the Kyŏngju area which has the former but generally not the latter. Within the Naktong River basin and south coast, some regional distinctions are made between south (early) and north (late), and east versus west. In general, the Naktong River below Taegu City is seen as a barrier between east and west; but above Taegu, Kaya culture is indistinguishable across both sides of the river.

The transition between Samhan and Kaya has been discussed above, but it is also necessary to identify the end of the sequence and the disappearance of Kaya. The historical documents make it clear that Silla conquered Pon-Kaya in 532 and Tae-Kaya in 562. Thereafter in these areas, one can see a shift in the archaeological record to the adoption of Silla material culture, especially Silla-type stoneware. By this time, stonemounded tomb construction had been superseded in the Kyŏngju region by horizontal stone-chamber construction with a side entrance. This burial construction replaces Kaya-type pit-chambers in the 7th century with the expansion of Silla power. One problem in viewing political relations from the standpoint of material culture is that political boundaries and stylistic boundaries are not necessarily congruent, which gives rise to differing interpretations of whether the entire east bank of Naktong River basin belonged politically to Silla or to Kaya, or how and when Silla extended its political control into this eastern Kaya cultural region before the conquest of the western polities.

Table 7.2 Proposals for royal Kaya cemeteries (after Nishitani 1992b)

## Kŭmgwan-Kaya Taesŏng-dong Tomb Cluster, Kimhae

## Tae-Kaya Chisan-dong Tomb Cluster, Koryŏng

## Sŏngsan-Kaya Sŏngsan-dong Tomb Cluster, S'ŏngju

## Ara-Kaya Misan-ri Tomb Cluster, Haman

## Tara-guk Okchŏn Tomb Cluster, Hapch'ŏn

## T'aksun-guk Imdang-dong Tomb Cluster, Kyŏngsan

The shift of Kaya cultural and political centres from a concentration on the south coast in the 4th century to a more diffuse settlement on the west side of the Naktong in the 5th and 6th centuries can clearly be seen in the distribution of Kaya cemetery sites (Figure 7.3). By the mid-6th century, the area east of the Naktong is considered to belong to Silla. Among these early and late Kaya cemeteries (kobun-gun) are many which have been proposed as royal cemeteries for several of the known Kaya groupings (Table 7.2).

If these evaluations are to be relied upon, then in each of these regions, there should be a hierarchy of cemeteries for the associated elite and commoners in these different Kaya societies. Comparative distributional studies of cemeteries in the Haman basin and the Ulsan area near Kyŏngju by Lee S. J. show striking differences in organization between the former, which has a centralized focus, and the latter which does not. He interprets the difference as that between a centralized polity (Ara-Kaya) in Haman and an outlying territory of the Silla polity without its own central government focus in Ulsan (Lee Sŏng-ju, pers. comm., Aug. 1996).

All the traditional founding dates for the early states of Silla, Koguryŏ, Paekche and Kaya fall around ad 0, and scholars divide into two groups:

those who use the dates at face value and those who reject them as being approximately two and a half to three centuries too early. In the case of Kaya, if ad 42 is used as is, then the six traditional Kaya entities would have existed in the Samhan period and we would expect them to be named in the Chinese chronicles. Instead, the Weizhi speaks of the twelve states of Pyŏnhan region of Samhan, including the one named (K.) Kuya. Many scholars consider that the Samhan polity of Kuya is an early manifestation of Kŭmgwan-Kaya, with supposed continuity in name and place (near modern Kimhae) (e.g. Kim D. S. 1993). A parallel situation exists in Silla historical studies where the Samhan polity of Saro is considered the forerunner of Silla (Lee J. W. 1982) see [Chapter 8, this volume]. Thus, a general chronology of Kaya history can be compiled from the above archaeological sources that does not rely on the early foundation date or Karak king list as given in the Karak Kukki chapter or the traditional founding date. If, as seems likely from archaeology, Kaya political development actually took off in the mid-3rd century or even early 4th century, the Karak king list can be compressed between ad 250/300–532, giving an average of approximately 30 years for each of ten kings.

From the 5th century, Shin believes, the Kaya polities can be divided into three groups: those which affiliated with Silla, those which affiliated with Paekche, and those that remained independent (Shin K. C. 1992b). 11 In particular, it was the lower Naktong valley Kaya groups which came under the cultural influence of Silla, as shown from the sudden spread of Sillatype stoneware through the Pusan and Kimhae regions to Changnyŏn, Taegu, Kyŏngsan, and Sŏngju – as especially apparent at the Pokch'ŏn-dong and Chilsan-dong tomb excavations. By the mid-5th century, Shin postulates that the lower Naktong Kaya also came under the political influence of Silla, though he maintains that regional leaders were recognised and left in place by the Silla rulers in Kyŏngju as attested by continued Kaya tomb building. The independent Kaya region consisted of Haman, Hapch'ŏn, Kosŏng, Chinju, Kochŏng and Koryŏng – stretching along the southern Korean coast to the west. 12 According to Shin, the Tae-Kaya polity, located in the region of Koryŏng, became the dominant actor in this region in the mid-5th century, and Koryŏng-type pottery spread throughout the independent Kaya areas except for Haman by the end of the century. At this time, the region split into two, with Tae-Kaya and its dependents following Paekche but the Anra-Kaya polity in Haman, which had been the strongest Kaya polity in Yŏngnam in the 4th century, remaining untouched.

Kŭmgwan-Kaya was conquered by Silla during the reign of King Pŏphŭng in 532, and the Tae-Kaya was conquered under the Silla King Chinhŭng in 562. Thus by the late 6th century, Kaya had ceased to exist, although the royal house of Kŭmgwan-Kaya was apparently absorbed into the Kim line of Silla kings, known as the "new Kim line" with true-bone rank (Lee K. B. 1984: 50).

#### **Notes**

1The spelling and/or pronunciation of the placenames that occur in the early texts are extremely problematic. Here I have followed standard practice in giving all placenames in the modern language of their locations, e.g. Kaya is the modern Korean reading of the Chinese characters that occur in the early historical and medieval texts. Most of the texts not only used Chinese characters but were actually written in the Chinese language; however, the authors and/or compilers usually tried to choose characters for names (both person and place) that mimicked the phonetic values in the original languages.

2As stated by Ilyŏn in the *Samguk Yusa* at the beginning of the *Karak Kukki* chapter (Choi N. H. 1996; see below).

3The *Samguk Yusa* was written or compiled in the Koryŏ period (916–1392) by a Buddhist monk named Ilyŏn [M-R: Iryŏn] (1206–89). Most of the original sources he relied upon in compiling the document have since been lost. An English translation of the entire *Samguk Yusa*, including the *Karak Kukki*, can be consulted in Ha & Mintz's translation (1972); but this must be used with care in conjunction with an original version. There are two versions of the original text: one which has survived in two places in Japan but is missing a few pages (e.g. the Myŏngmundang publication, Anon. 1993), or the text as it has survived in Korea (e.g. Choi N. H. 1996). All the originals are written in Chinese, but there exist several translations into modern Korean for comparison.

4Also called Kŭmjil or Chilji.

5The *Kaihuangli*, the Kaihuang era (ad 581–600) calendar in the Chinese Sui dynasty (581–617).

6The Kaihuanglu, the "Records of the Kaihuang era" as above.

7The Sanguozhi, compiled in the 3rd century (Gardiner 1973).

8The Chinese characters used for these sacrifices means "followers in death" and it is not clear whether they were voluntary or not; for an overview of sacrificial burials, see Kwŏn O. H. 1992.

9See Chapter 1 (this volume) the section on Kaya, for a discussion of the name "Mimana" as applied to the Kaya area.

10Cf. Lee C. S. 1991: chronological chart.

- hi-Paekche/hi-Silla-kei Kaya (with hi meaning "not").
