## **Chapter Eight**

# The Emergence and Expansion of Silla from an Archaeological Perspective\*

#### **Abstract**

As ultimately the most powerful early Korean state which unified the peninsula in 668, Silla is often seen as an independent and separate power arising in the southeastern peninsula. This chapter reveals that Silla's material culture roots are indistinguishable from Kaya, arising from the early stoneware and tomb traditions of the Yŏngnam region. The emergence of an elite lineage claiming separate Silla identity is reflected in the material culture only late in the protohistoric period from the 5th century onwards.

#### **Introduction**

The historical entity of Silla is well known as a strong state which defeated its Three Kingdoms-period rivals (Koguryŏ in the north; Paekche in the west; and Kaya in the south) in order to unify the Korean Peninsula under one administration. The origin and development of the Silla state, however, are highly controversial issues with opinions divided not only between historians and archaeologists but even among archaeologists, as we shall see below. The historical extremist view is represented by Lee Jung-wook, who accepts the traditional founding date of 57 bc as given in the Samguk Sagi and projects back the existence of the Saro polity, mentioned in the Weizhi as one of the Late Iron Age polities of Chinhan (Lee J. W. 1982). Despite his reliance on the Chinese document for the general historical context of the pre-Silla political entity, he rejects the implications of its chronology, relying exclusively on the legendary founding dates for the Three Kingdoms given in the 13th-century Samguk Sagi. By viewing the change between Sarŏ and Silla as merely the adoption of a new name, we are led to believe that Silla was a major player on the southern peninsula from as early as the 1st century bc, with continuity both in the ruling class and in polity identity. Surely this is exactly what Kim Pusik, compiler of the Samguk Sagi, would have wanted us to believe, since a major aim of his writings was to legitimise the Koryŏ Dynasty under which he lived and worked as an inheritor of Silla authority and culture. Through the chronicles he compiled,

Silla had to be shown to be dominant over the other major states not just from the 7th century but from the beginning of state formation on the peninsula.

However, if one looks at the archaeological record of the Kyŏngju basin, home of the Silla state, in comparison with the rest of the peninsula, it suggests that Silla is a developmental latecomer. The southeastern coast (the Kaya region) developed its ceramic and metal-working industries much earlier than the basin, and the mid-western (Paekche) and northern regions (Koguryŏ) show social stratification earlier than in the southeast (Yŏngnam) region. The archaeological details of these phenomena will be examined below, but they form the basis for my viewing of the emergence of Silla, including state formation, as a process occurring in the late 4th to early 5th century ad.

The sections below follow the chronological scheme shown in Table 8.1. Note that the Early Iron Age overlaps with the Late Bronze Age; this is due to the introduction of iron from China (or possibly Northeast Asia) into the northern peninsula while the southern peninsula remains without it for awhile. Thus, the latter partakes only of the Late Iron Age. I have chosen here to use the period name of Late Iron Age for ad 0–300 rather than the Proto-Three Kingdoms, another name by which this timespan is known, because Proto-Three Kingdoms seems teleological. At the time, the communities on the peninsula did not know they were headed towards consolidation into three major states, and I believe that using the Proto-Three Kingdoms appellation only serves to foster such claims of continuity as those invoked by Lee J. W. for Sarŏ-Silla. In contrast, if the two periods (Late Iron Age and Three Kingdoms) are kept conceptually separate, we are more likely to look at the transition with a critical eye. Finally, the Late Iron Age is also referred to by some as the Samhan period, referring to the occupation of the northern peninsula from 108 bc by the Chinese at the Lelang Commandery near P'yŏngyang; and the references to the southern peninsula in the Chinese documents as the region of the Three Han (Samhan): Mahan, Pyŏnhan; and Chinhan. The use of the term Samhan here will be avoided because it gives primacy to Chinese interpretations of the situation.

## **Table 8.1** Chronological divisions utilized

Bronze Age (BA) = 1st millennium bc

## Early Iron Age (IA1) = 300 bc-ad 0

## Late Iron Age (IA2) = ad 0-300

## Three Kingdoms (3K) = 300-668

#### **Pre-Silla Kyŏngju**

In the Late Iron Age (1st to 3rd centuries ad) the archaeology of the Kyŏngju basin is virtually indistinguishable from the rest of the Yŏngnam region (southeastern Korea). This means that on the basis of material culture, we cannot see a major distinction between the areas that later become Kaya and Silla. The burial record of Yŏngnam in the Late Iron Age is characterised first by wooden coffin (mokkan) burial, then by wooden coffins contained within wooden outer chambers (mokkwak).2 The more elaborate double wooden structures yield narrow bronze swords and iron materials plus Wajil pottery (a hard-fired, grey earthenware). The inclusion of Han Dynasty prestige goods from China and fine, locally made bronze swords in some of the graves suggests that social hierarchies enabled certain persons to gain access to specially made objects either through trade or local production. However, the cemeteries of this time do not show any spatial separation or elaborate facilities for those persons buried with such goods. Therefore, it is difficult to see the kind of social stratification postulated by Lee J. W. for the emergence of a strong and predatory Sarŏ state at this time.

Among the important pre-Silla sites in Kyŏngju, Choyang-dong serves as the type site for Wajil pottery, which now is recognized as the major chronological marker of the Late Iron Age in Yŏngnam. Prior to the Choyang-dong excavations between 1979–81, Wajil was subsumed under the former category of "Kimhae ware", and no distinction was made between high-fired grey earthenwares and stonewares [see Chapter 3, this volume]. The identification of Wajil allows a more accurate separation of Late Iron Age remains from proper Three Kingdoms-period remains and thus allows a more accurate assessment of the stages of Kyŏngju area development. Two Wajil dating schemes are available: a relatively detailed six-phase division (Ch'ŏi C. K. 1982), and a grosser two phase division which is more generally useful (Figure 8.1) (Shin K. C. 1989). Early Wajil overlaps with Plain pottery of the Bronze Age tradition, and Late Wajil pottery occurs together with early Kaya-type stoneware, which appeared in the 3rd century and marks the beginning of the Three Kingdoms pottery tradition.


**Figure 8.1** Wajil pottery, the main burial ceramics of Late Iron Age Yŏngnam (after Shin K. C. 1989: figs. 1, 2) 1–6 Old Wajil (ca. 1st–3rd c. ad); 7–10 New Wajil (ca. ad 250–420)

The Choyang-dong site itself is a cemetery positioned on a gentle slope southeast of Kyŏngju; several kinds of burials were discovered there: wood coffins, wooden chambers, stone-lined pit chambers, and jars ranging in date from the 1st century bc to the 5th–6th centuries ad (Ch'ŏi C. K. 1995). Notable among these finds was #38 Type II wood coffin burial yielding four Early Han-dynasty mirrors and eight of the earliest Wajil pots. Dated by the excavator to the early 1st century, this burial was the first to be discovered which demonstrated trade links and social relations between the southern peninsular peoples and the Han Dynasty Court.

From the 2nd century onwards, burials in Kyŏngju tend to contain more and more iron, especially flat iron "axes" such as in Type III burials at Choyang-dong or at Sara-ri, west of Kyŏngju, where they were used as floor pavements (YBCPRI 1996). The Weizhi states that about this time, the Pyŏnhan area of the southern coast was a major iron producer, and the Han, Ye and Wa peoples came to obtain iron and used it in their markets. 3 It is possible that the Weizhi might have been referring to these flat iron "axes", which could have been used variously as raw material ingots, as currency bars, and as wealth. The materials from Sara-ri burial #130, located west of Kyŏngju and excavated in 1995–6 (SS 1996), also suggest considerable integration into such trade networks in the 2nd century ad. In addition to the iron axe pavement, it contained iron and bronze swords, some bronze buckles, a large iron pot from northeast China, a small bronze mirror probably made in Kyushu, a 50cm-long narrow bronze sword, 3 iron swords and a 20cm-long Chinese-style bronze tiger-shaped belt hook. Iron axes have also been recovered from the Oksŏng-ri site in P'ohang City, Kyŏngju's northern coastal outlet. 138 burials were excavated there in 1995, most of them wood-chambers dating to the Late Iron Age. Iron tools and knives, and distinctive barbed blades of ceremonial shapes, were recovered there along with Wajil pottery and many beads of faceted amber and crystal, round blue glass beads, and crystal and turquoise curved beads (magatama) – the last suggesting possible contacts with the Japanese Islands.

However, it is not clear whether the iron at Sara-ri was, in fact, imported from the south coast or whether it was made nearby. The unprecedented discovery in 1994 of an iron-smelting site at Hwangsŏng-dong, in northern Kyŏngju City, proved that the south coast was not the only iron producing area in Yŏngnam. Indeed, with its discovery, more attention is being paid to the Weizhi statements that combine references to Pyŏnhan and Chinhan as "Pyŏn-Chin", i.e. all of Yŏngnam, not just the south coast. The Hwangsŏngdong site consists of Bronze Age to Three Kingdoms settlement, including a cemetery (Yi K. M. & Kim H. 1985) and an iron-smelting furnace (HTCET 1994). The majority of burials were wooden chamber burials (38 in all); among them are the first recovered burials in Kyŏngju dating to the 3rd century. Wooden chamber burial #51 contained slag from the furnace (500m distance away), and it is thought that the ironworkers were buried in the cemetery. Thus, the Kyŏngju Basin was on its way to establishing its own iron industry which would serve to maintain the Silla state later.

At the end of the 3rd century, another product appears in the local burial sequence that is thought to be an import from the south coast: Kaya-type stoneware, which heralds the beginning of the Three Kingdoms period. It occurs in some of the later wooden chamber burials at Hwangsŏng-dong and at the site of Kujŏng-dong, southeast of Kyŏngju. In 1982, a pair of wooden chamber burials were excavated at Kujŏng-dong on the summit of a small circular hill and one more on the slope off the summit (Ch'ŏi C. K 1995); although the burials were unmounded, the natural hill appears as a small tumulus. The different-sized chambers on the summit are interpreted as belonging to a couple (Han B. S. 1992), and the contents of the three chambers together yielded Wajil pottery, two stoneware jars of Kaya-type Puwŏn-dong C, 25 iron spearheads, knife, axe and arrowheads in addition to the earliest iron cuirass yet recovered on the peninsula. The site has been variously dated to the terminal 3rd or early 4th century (Han B. S. 1992: 9; Ch'ŏi C. K. 1995: 236).

![](_page_294_Figure_2.jpeg)

**Figure 8.2** Histogram of wooden chamber burial shapes (length/width) at Hwangsŏng-dong cemetery, showing a shift to long thin shapes exceeding a ratio of 3.0. Burial numbers are in histogram boxes (after An C. H. 1995: 124)

The wooden chamber burials at Kujŏng-dong are part of a indigenous trend in changing wooden chamber shape. From squarish and deep in the 1st century, the pits became shallower in the 2nd and 3rd centuries, and deposition of Wajil began. Then towards the 4th century, the chamber lengthened to accommodate an additional box containing grave goods at the foot (Figure 8.2\) (An 1995).

These long graves contain the most iron of any Kyŏngju burials and the beginning of stoneware deposition. This trend has been interpreted as indicative of the hierarchization of Kyŏngju area society (Lee H. R. 1993). Particularly, Kujŏng-dong is viewed by some Kyŏngju archaeologists as a high-ranking burial in contrast to the lower ranks at nearby Choyang-dong. Many long wooden chamber burials have recently been excavated in the valleys surrounding the Kyŏngju basin (see Figure 8.3\).

However, this indigenous trend appears to have been transcended by the adoption of a completely different burial structure using stone in the Three Kingdoms period. The addition of stone to Yŏngnam burial construction is somewhat of a throw-back to Bronze Age traditions.4 Many of the stone cist and stone-lined burials of the Bronze Age produced elegant examples of decorated and unusually shaped bronze objects. The bronze tradition itself and stone-constructed burials are thought by many scholars to have been introduced through a migration of Tungus language speakers from Northeast Asia in the early 1st millennium bc. With the subjugation of the northern peninsula by the Han Dynasty court in 108 bc, these Bronze Age traditions seemed to have died out and were replaced by wood coffin and wooden chamber burial, as discussed above, under Chinese influence. Then, with the destruction of the Lelang commandery in 313 by Koguryŏ, a second wave of northeast Asian traditions can be seen on the southern peninsula. The institution of stone-piled burials, imported styles of horse trappings, and gold-working are the main manifestations of that influence in the Kyŏngju region.

## The use of stone in Yongnam burials

The typologies of stone burials in Yŏngnam used by East Asian scholars are extremely difficult to work with; there is no standard classification scheme, with a multitude of types and even different names used for the same type in the literature. The major distinction is between "pit-style stone chamber" burials and "stone-piled wood chamber" burials (Table 8.2). These constructions show significant differences in regional and status attributions (Figure 8.4). It is significant that the "stone-piled wood-chamber" burial is only present in the Kyŏngju region and it is completely different from the "pit-style stone chamber" in the rest of Yŏngnam. There is also some variation in the literature whether these are termed "tombs" (*pun* or *koburi*) or "graves" (*myo*). Logically it seems there should be a concomitant difference in size, with "tombs" having a large mound and graves not; however, there does not appear to be such a regular and strict distinction in the Korean terminology.

The general Yŏngnam pit-style stone chamber is intentionally and carefully constructed of stones in a variety of ways: by placing flagstones on edge to form a box-shaped chamber, by laying slabstones flat in dry-wall construction, by using boulders as walls and filling the intertices with drywall stone construction, etc. Finally, the chamber is capped with wooden boards (early) or ceiling rocks (late). It is termed pit-style because the chamber is always built in a pit, never above ground.7 Sometimes that pit might be filled with stones, but the distinctive aspect of this type is that the stone chamber itself is an architectural construction.

In contrast, the stone-piled burial uses stone in a less architectural manner. Early burials are constructed as a pit in which a wooden chamber is placed, and then the pit is filled with stones. Later burials have only a shallow depression in which the wooden chamber is placed or it is placed on level ground; then the chamber is mounded over with stones. In 1990, Ch'oi Byŏng-hyŏn published a definitive thesis on the appearance of Silla tombs, and he determined that the piled-stone burial style was adopted from Kurgan cultures in north Central Asia (Ch'oi B. H. 1990). His assessment, reaffirmed in 1994, is that the features of wooden chamber, piled stones, high earthen mound and outer kerb stones were adopted together as a set from a foreign culture (Ch'oi B. H. 1994). This view is currently being challenged by Lee Sung-ju, excavator of the Chungsan-ri site southeast of Kyŏngju, who has proposed an evolutionary scheme from wooden-chamber burial through a newly identified type of "stone-surround wood-chamber burial" to what he calls a "wood-chamber piled-stone burial" (in an inversion of Ch'oi's term) (Lee S. J. 1992a). 8 In his most recent research, Lee S. J. proposes that two types of stone-piled burial should be recognized (Lee S. J. 1996; Kim H. G. 1996). Type A (wooden chamber in a stone-filled pit) appears in the terminal 3rd century, for example at Chungsan-ri in Ulsan (Lee S. J. 1992b), and continues throughout the Three Kingdoms period; 9 and Type B (stone-mounded wooden chamber covered with a high earthen mound) is the type of the royal Silla tombs and only occurs in central Kyŏngju (Lee S. J., pers. comm., Aug. 1996).

Such is the current debate over the definition of stone-piled burials. This is an important debate since Ch'oi B. H. has framed his definition as applying only to the royal Silla tombs and the intentional imitation of or even migration from the Kurgan culture of Kazakhstan and the Altai. Whether stone-piled burials existed in a lesser form and earlier in time than the royal tombs, and whether they are continuous with or just reflective of the royal tomb construction is the substance of that debate. The definition of a stone-piled wooden chamber also affects the dating of its appearance, as we shall see below, and assessments of its distribution. Everyone agrees that the royal Silla tombs, as defined by Ch'oi B. H., occur only in the Kyŏngju Basin; and earlier identifications of them far afield are now rejected.10 But the lesser stone-piled burials defined by Lee S. J., as they appear in Kyŏngju's only access points to the ocean – in P'ohang in the north and Ulsan in the south – are no less interesting; they represent the maximum territorial distribution of the stonepiling custom which did not spread into greater Yŏngnam during the Three Kingdoms period despite the expansion of Silla political territory. 11

It should be emphasised that such stone-piled burials, royal or lesser, did not exist in isolation but co-existed within Silla territory during the Three Kingdoms period with the more traditional burial structures of woodenchamber burials, jar burials, pit-chamber burials, and plain pit burials used by various levels of society. However, studies have yet to be done on how all these burial styles integrate into territorial, social, and political hierarchies for the emerging Silla state.

Towards the end of Three Kingdoms, an entirely different kind of tomb chamber is adopted: a full stone-built room with coridor entrance all mounded over with earth (Table 8.2) (Han B. S. 1992: 5–21; Ch'oi B. H. 1994). 12 In the middle 6th century, this replaces the stone-mounded burial of the Silla aristocracy and becomes the premier burial type through the transition between Three Kingdoms and the United Silla period; but soon thereafter, the Buddhist practice of cremation contributed to the gradual abandonment of tomb burial. The unifier of the peninsula, King Munmu, was the first to be cremated in 681, but many subsequent kings chose to be buried in mounded tombs. From the late 7th century, Chinese-style tomb accoutrements began to be used, as at the mounded tomb of King Sŏngdŏk; (r. 702–27) (Nishitani 1981). Carved stone statues of men and animals, in the style of the Chinese "spirit path", were placed near the tomb; and carved stone slabs depicting the twelve zodiac figures were erected against the tomb footings.

## **Silla tomb chronologies**

In addition to typology, a second difficulty in assessing Silla archaeology is that different schools of archaeologists disagree in their dating of the Silla tombs, varying by nearly a century. In the past, this disagreement has centred around the position of the Japanese-excavated tomb of Hwangnam #109 (chambers 3/4) (Saito 1937). Lee Chong-sŏn has described the disagreement as based on the use of different artifacts and/or constructions and the chronologies derived from these limited materials (Lee C. S. 1992).

In his review of opinions on the datings of the Silla tombs, scholars in Group 1 base their chronological evaluations on the inner construction of the tomb as well as the grave goods; scholars in Group 2 place primary emphasis on the grave goods alone; while scholars in Group 3 utilize just the pottery. 13 Recent datings of Hwangnam #109–3/4 put it between ca. ad 320 (Group 1 dating); ca. ad 350–410 (Group 3), and ca. ad 410–20 (Group 2). It would be easier to deal with these schemes if the datings could be simply shifted back and forth 100 years or so, but the situation is more complex than that. Not all scholars give datings for every tomb, and even among those who give the same dating for Hwangnam #109–3/4 (e.g. Haraguchi, Fujii, Satome), other tombs are reversed in chronological order in their individual schemes.

New shifts in the basis of the chronology have included the discovery of stone-piled tombs thought to be earlier than Hwangnam #109–3/4 in the Wŏlsŏng-no cemetery, located in Hwang'o-dong, Kyŏngju (KNM 1990). Burials Ka–6 and Ka–13 both yielded Kaya-type stoneware; Ka–29, a wooden coffin burial without stones, yielded a green tuff radial bracelet from the Japanese Islands, iron ingots possibly from Kaya, and armour lamellae possibly from Koguryŏ; and Ka–31 contained several mini-jar and stand combinations seemingly copied from Japanese-style Haji ware. These burials date from the 4th century and show not only the transition in Kyŏngju proper from wooden chamber burial (in Ka–29) to stone-piled burial (in Ka–6) but also the establishment of contact with other states in the region at this time. The utilization of the central Kyŏngju plains represents a new trend, suggesting both geographical centralization and spatial distancing from commoners in the burial of elites. This is the beginning of the process of social stratification, which is completed in the late 4th or early 5th century with the addition of large earthen mounds to the stone-piled tombs as known in Tumulus Park, the royal cemetery popular with present-day tourists located just 500m west of the Wŏlsŏng-no excavations.

In terms of accommodations in the dating, Ch'oi Byŏng-hyŏn, the foremost advocate of 4th-century royal tomb dates, thinks Wŏlsŏng-no Ka– 6 is now the earliest royal stone-piled tomb, earlier than the more common candidate of Hwangnam #109–3/4 (Ch'oi B. H. 1990: 344; Ch'oi B. H.

1993). In contrast, Lee Sung-ju does not consider #109–3/4 to be a royal tomb but a lesser stone-piled burial; he instead believes the earliest example of a royal Silla tomb to be Hwangnam #98 South Mound (pers. comm. Aug. 1996). The shift of focus from Hwangnam #109–3/4 to Hwangnam #98 is due to interest in the fabulous goldworking and other exotic artifacts excavated from the Hwangnam #98 North and South Mounds in 1974–5 (KMY 1985, 1996). 14 This shift is somewhat welcome since this is a tomb excavated recently with modern techniques, as opposed to the Japanese excavations of #109–3/4 in the early 20th century. Since #98 South Mound is the first in the chronological sequence to display Silla goldwork, the controversy over its dating is fierce, ranging between the middle 4th and the late 5th century. 15

The controversy between Early and Late schools involves the dating of certain types of stirrups – those with long shanks versus those with short shanks (Ch'oi B. H. 1990: part 2, Chapter 4). Previous research identified the short-shanked version as an Old type and the long-shanked version as a New type (Onoyama 1966). 16 Shin Kyung Cheol, leader of the Late school of dating, believes in the evolution of short to long and a corresponding time lapse; since the Hwangnam #98 South Mound contains both, the tomb can only be the second half of the 5th century by his reckoning.

However, new discoveries particularly from northern Chinese tombs have scholars dividing into two groups: 1) those who date the stirrup to the 5th century with reference to the chronology of horse trappings and Sue stoneware occurring in the Japanese Kofun-period tombs, as correlated with the discoveries of the same object categories from the Kaya tombs on the south Korean coast (e.g. Lee H. J. 1995, Kim D. C. 1996); and 2) those scholars who date the stirrup to the 4th century by utilizing only the Kyŏngju burial data, with reference to excavations of horse trappings from northeastern Chinese tombs (Ch'oi B. H. 1990: Chapter 4). Kim D. C. (1996) has accused the latter group of totally ignoring the Kaya data and basing their chronology on unsubstantiated relative dating schemes for the poorly known Chinese materials. In this criticism, he has a valid point, since the Japanese scheme is extremely well developed and reliable in comparison to the sparsely excavated and sparely constructed Chinese artifact chronologies for this period.

These relative datings can be contrasted with radiocarbon dates obtained from the North and South mounds and followed by the Middle school:

Table 8.3 Radiocarbon datings of the Hwangnam #98 North & South mounds (KMY 1996: 346-9)

| Lab No. | Mound (material dated) | Date |
|------------|------------------------|-------------|
| KAERI–151 | South (wood) | 1545±150bp |
| KAERI–152 | South (wood) | 1645±150 bp |
| KAERI–135 | South (wood) | 1972+120 bp |

These have been translated into ranges of ad 305–405 for the South Mound (male) and ad 365–410 for the North Mound (female). 17 If one considers that a married couple was probably buried in Hwangnam #98, it is plausible that the female burial should be later. The excavators mention five Silla kings to whom the South Mound could have belonged, beginning with King Naemul (ad 351–402); their conclusion is that the South Mound represents the rise of the Kim clan to great power in Silla (KMY 1996: 365). Despite the half-century difference in dating, these conclusions are not so different from those of Ch'oi B. H., who terms the royal tombs as the "burial system of the Maripkan period" (Ch'oi B. H. 1994: 28). 18

Such is the state of controversy over the emergence of the earliest royal Silla tombs. If this chronological and typological impasse is a frustrating situation for archaeologists, it must be far more so for those who wish to use archaeological data to reconstruct an accurate view of history. Unfortunately, there is no solution in sight for unifying these schemes, so one must be aware of which is being used when speaking chronologically and retain flexibility in assessing interpretations.

## **Silla clan cemeteries**

The royal Silla cemeteries (kobun-gun) with stone-piled burial construction are limited to the Kyŏngju Basin. Ch'oi Byŏng-hyŏn notes that most of these cemeteries are built on the central flat plains, with the notable exceptions of Pomun-dong tomb cluster (in a hilly area at the eastern basin edge), and Kŭmchok-ri tomb cluster (in the valley leading out of the basin to the west) (Ch'oi B. H. 1990: 93–6). The central Silla cemeteries are named after their current administrative addresses within Kyŏngju City: Kyo-dong, Inwangdong, Onung, Hwangnam-dong, Hwang'o-dong, Nosŏdong and Nodongdong. But these different names give a misleading impression that they were once coherent groupings. If one looks at the early map of the tomb distributions, one can see that the tombs spread out from the foot of Mt. Namsan in the south across the high ground to the northwest (cf. Figure 8.7\). This wide grouping includes the famous named tombs excavated by the Japanese in the decade between 1916 and 1926: Kŭmgwan-ch'ong (Gold Crown Tomb), Kŭmryŏng-ch'ong (Gold Bell Tomb), Sik-i-ch'ong (Ornamental Shoe Tomb), Sŏbong-ch'ong (Swedish Phoenix Tomb) and Kŏm-ch'ong (Sword Tomb), etc., as well as the fabulous discoveries from the recent Korean excavations at Hwangnam #98 discussed above and Ch'ŏnma-ch'ong (Flying Horse Tomb) (MK 1975).

Within these royal cemeteries, scholars distinguish several types of structures (Figure 8.5; see Lee J. W. 1986 for some details). The first division is between large and small tombs; thereafter, they focus on the number and independence of the individual burial chambers. Many typological schemes have been presented,19 but in general, tombs built with single chambers (with or without a separate accessory chamber for grave goods) are distinguished from others with multiple burials. Within the latter category are 1) those seemingly designed with adjacent multiple chambers (such as those found in Mich'u Locality 12 – Figure 8.5b), and 2) those which simply show successive burials through time like Hwangnam #109 itself (Figure 8.5c), which has two different layers of burials: the original early set of burial and accessory chambers at the base of the mound (chambers 3–4) and a later set of burial and accessory chambers higher up in the mound (chambers 1–2). Regardless of whether single or multiple, many of these burials show overlap and reuse of the space available, a situation that has social implications beyond the scope of this chapter.

In 1976 Kim Wŏn-yŏng attempted to identify the Sarŏ "six villages" with six mounded tomb groupings within the Kyŏngju basin (Kim W. Y. 1976). Lee J. W. has subsequently attempted to identify the tomb clusters of the Silla clans by assessing "the description of clans and their important members in historical accounts" (Lee J. W. 1986).

Suprisingly, Lee J. W. – despite his emphasis on continuity between Sarŏ and Silla – objects to Kim Wŏn-yŏng's geographical assignments, arguing that it is more likely that the six Sarŏ villages were not all located within the Kyŏngju Basin itself but some in the surrounding countryside. His rationale is the supposed continuity of the six Sarŏ villages exactly with the six *bu* divisions of Silla, then becoming the six *bu* of the Koryŏ Dynasty. Despite the number six being continuously invoked as a geographical organizer, there is no inherent reason that the six Sarŏ villages would automatically have become *bu* under Silla; and in fact, it could be questioned whether the six villages might be a "back formation" (a linguistics term meaning a "later creation") from the Silla territorial hierarchy in order to appease the local residents and provide the appearance of autochthonous development.

**Figure 8.5a** The mature style of royal Silla single burial; note that the stone mound has collapsed into the wooden chamber under the weight of the overlying earthen mound (after Ch'oi B. H. 1992: fig. 33)

The above schemes of matching clan to tomb cluster are viewed from an archaeological point of view as fraught with difficulty and unprovable without documentary evidence. However, the developing archaeology of the Kyŏngju basin and adjacent valleys is beginning to provide support for the idea that pre-4th-century settlement was concentrated mainly in the valleys leading out of the basin rather than on the central basin plain. When one reviews the geography of the Kyŏngju plain, one can possibly see why (cf. Figure 8.7). Three rivers meet in the basin: two tributaries flow from southeast to northwest into the main Hyŏngsan River, which flows south to north along the west side of the basin. The entire plain can be seen to be an alluvial fan, oriented east to west, of the tributaries; this means that there would have been many changes of river course through the centuries with flooding a constant danger. The South Tributary now passes between Panwŏl-sŏng, the fortress considered to be Silla's 4th-century palace, and Mt. Namsan; but contour line placement suggest it once also flowed along the north of Panwŏl-sŏng. This course had been abandoned by the time of tomb construction since the Tumulus Park tombs sit right it its old path. Current excavations east of Hwangnyŏng-sa Temple indicate that that area of plains remained mud flats until the late 6th century. 20 It had to be landfilled in order to make it ready for establishing the gid capital from the late 7th century. In contrast to this uncertain and changing topography with limited area available in the central basin, the adjoining valleys might have seemed safer with their steeper drainage patterns.


**Figure 8.5c** Hwangnam #109 (after Oda et al. 1993: 145)

Thus, it is quite possible that the main Sarŏ population centers were located in the outlying areas. However, being much earlier in time than the central plain occupation, they are to be sought not in the distribution of high-mounded tomb clusters but in the distribution of wooden chamber cemeteries \(Figure 8.3). The major areas to be considered are the arterial exits from the Kyŏngju Basin: the southeastern coridor towards the port outlet of Ulsan seems to be one likely focus (especially between Choyangdong and Chungsan-ri), as does the western coridor leading to Taegu (represented by Sara-ri), and finally the valley leading out to the

P'ohang coast (including Oksŏng-ri). In the Kyŏngju Basin, but on the north side of the North Tributary, is Hwangsŏng-dong.

The centralization that the establishment of the 4th-century tomb clusters on the central plains represents was a centripetal political force, as described in the traditional documents; but the question remains what the stimulus was to centralization and the extent of hiatus or transformation from the earlier Sarŏ occupation.

## **Silla origins**

Ch'oi Byŏng-hyŏn equates the adoption of stone-mounded burial with the reign of King Naemul (r. 356–402), the corresponding rise of the Kim clan, and the institutionalization of the royal rank of Maripkan (Choi B. H. 1990: 372; 1994). This idea is not far-fetched, since the tombs represent a massive advance in resource allocation dependent on a significantly high status and authority for amassing and redeploying those resources. The reign of Naemul also represents a turning point in Silla organisational history as seen from the documentary evidence. The source of status of the Kim clan may well have included restricted access to gold, since gold objects are among the new types of precious goods deposited in the royal tombs. As referred to above, Choi thinks the stone-piled burial used for the Silla royal tombs "appeared through the arrival of some groups of ... horse iding people from the northern Asia at Kyŏngju." (Choi B. H. 1990: preface). And Lee Insook (pers. comm. July 1996) is currently of the opinion that Alan peoples' gold-working techniques were extremely close and important to the institution of Silla gold-working.

The northern origin of the stone-piled tomb structure – if not the actual migration of kurgan horsenders to Kyŏngju – is widely acknowledged (Shim B. K. 1991). But Choi's ideas represent an inversion of the usual understanding of Silla's northern connections. Two known historical events have, in the past, been used to explain Silla's sudden florescence: the alleged destruction of the Lelang commandery in 313 by Koguryŏ, and the southward advance of Koguryŏ at the end of the 4th century. In the past, the former event provided the context for postulating the transfer of Chinese Han-dynasty gold-working techniques from Lelang to Silla by refugees.

However, Choi B. H.'s new hypothesis of the actual migration of kurgan culture horseiders to Kyŏngju disregards the role of Koguryŏ altogether in Silla state formation. By coincidence, it parallels Shin K. C.'s new hypothesis that the original Kaya peoples were also migrants from Northeast Asia, though he pinpoints the Fuyu in central Manchuria as the source for Kaya (Shin K. C. 1993, 1994).

This invocation of horseiders to explain the rise of 4th-century polities in Yŏngnam is reminiscent of the Horseider Theory of 5th-century Yamato state formation postulated by Egami Namio for Japan (Egami 1964). However, there is one important difference: in Japan one does not see a hiatus in the burial record with new tomb constructions accompanying the postulated invaders. Instead, there is continuity in tomb structure with changing artifact repertoires which clearly show close interaction with the peninsula, especially the introduction of horse-iding equipment from Kaya. In contrast, both in the Silla and Kaya areas, foreign artifacts are associated with what some archaeologists assess as foreign styles of burial – arguing forcefully for the presence of foreign peoples, not just exotic items.

Archaeological theory for assessing the evidence of migrations is especially weak. Traditional cultural history as practiced in mid-20thcentury Europe was in the habit of invoking the spectre of migration to explain every change in artifact style. In a backlash reaction, migration as an explanatory mechanism has since gone out of style, and studies have come to focus on identifying and understanding the mechanisms for indigenous change and development. Thus, theoretical expectations for migrational situations such as East Asia may have experienced in the 4th century are terribly under-developed. In the least, however, it seems reasonable to postulate the advent of new burial styles with the presence of foreign peoples under certain circumstances. One must consider whether the foreigner is one or many, is integrated into the local culture (and therefore adopts the local material culture) or establishes a ghetto where inherited cultural practices are continued in a new locale. Migration does not necessarily entail military conquest, since economic factors are often mutually attractive; and the status level of the immigrants and the level at which they are integrated into the receiving society must be taken into account.

Without acceding to conquest theories, what can be said about the historical circumstances that might have encouraged the possible presence of Northeast and Central Asians in Kyŏngju or Kimhae (the respective heartlands of Silla and Kaya emergence)? The obvious answer is iron. As stated above, we know that the Pyŏn-Chin area of 3rd-century Yŏngnam was famous for its iron products and many peoples from the surrounding regions came to obtain them. With the fall of Lelang in 313 and the opening up of trading possibilities further afield in the 4th century, it is not surprising to find evidence – on the south coast first (in Kaya) and later inland in Kyŏngju – of contact with Inner Asians. A large question looms as to how horse-iding peoples might have arrived at the coastal outlets to trade: was their shipping technology adequate for overseas routes? Or did they arrive overland? Or were their traditional objects and ideas passed on through Koguryŏ? Regardless, the sudden availability of horseider goods and practices must have been very attractive to the Yŏngnam peoples after losing the Lelang buffer that had interceded between them and Northeast Asia in the previous centuries.

The positioning of the Hwangsŏng-dong iron works in the northern Kyŏngju Basin must have been crucial to the economic underpinning of the emerging Silla polity. As mentioned above, some of the 4th-century Wŏlsŏng-no tombs yielded materials from the Japanese Islands, indicating possible trade of iron in that direction as well. Therefore, as with Kaya, the emergence of a strong and centralized Silla was probably predicated on the iron trade. In such a scenario, the appearance of foreign traders was probably capitalized on by the central basin Sarŏ in terms of adopting elements of exotic foreign material culture and possibly even the foreigners themselves into their social system in order to differentiate themselves from the other Sarŏ "villages". 21 Such a material and ethnic differentiation increased their status and power, leading to the emergence of the Kim clan as the first Silla rulers. In this scenario, indigenous continuity accompanied by gradual change through the 3rd century is capped with radical transformation in the late 4th century.

In summary, during the 1st to 3rd centuries, the special nature of the Kyŏngju region is virtually invisible in the generalized Late Iron Age culture of Yŏngnam. Traditions such as wooden coffin burial and Wajil pottery were used throughout the region. The first aspect of Silla's separation from Kaya and the rest of Yŏngnam occurs in the development of the long wooden coffin burial. Second is the appearance of the lesser stonepiled burial, third is the development of a particular style of stoneware out of the Kaya style, and fourth is the adoption of the royal tomb style and gold-working tradition from Inner Asia. The first three currently occur across the temporal boundary between the late 3rd and mid-4th centuries, and the fourth somewhere between the mid-4th and mid-5th centuries.

By the time of the construction of the royal Silla stone-mounded tombs on the Kyŏngju plains, replete with gold and silver crowns, girdles and ornaments for the rulers, not only do we see the full stratification of Kyŏngju society but material representations distinguishing the Silla ruling class from the mass of Yŏngnam society. Because these material objects are part and parcel of Silla identity – because they were used to give an identity to the Silla rulers, it is impossible to speak of a "Silla" before the manifestation of their physical means of identification. On these grounds, then, does the archaeologist strictly distinguish between Silla, both in terms of ethnicity and political visibility, with whatever kind of regional and ethnic forms of organization might have preceded it? The 4th century is the time period in which the infrastructure for the Silla state was laid, with Silla material and ethnic identity fully formalized in the 5th century.

## **Silla expansion through archaeology**

In the art historical literature, the stoneware of the Three Kingdoms period is commonly known as Silla ware. However, it is now clear that stoneware was not invented in Silla, and the typical shapes identified as Silla ware actually began in the Kaya area (Figure 8.6\). 22

![](_page_314_Figure_0.jpeg)

**Figure 8.6** 5th-century Kaya and Silla stoneware compared: note the perforations offset from vertical in the Silla pedestals and their straight angle of flare as opposed to the curved flare of Kaya pedestals. From the early 6th century, Silla stoneware was adopted in the Tae-Kaya region in Naktong-west following the conquest of 562 (after Sadamori 1992: fig. 3)

The Kaya shapes are undecorated, and pedestaled bowls have perforations that are arranged vertically in tiers. The defining characteristics of later Silla ware, in contrast, are a profusion of comb-marked and incised decoration; pedestal perforations that are offset from the vertical between tiers; and a long-necked jar that has a banded shoulder and straight projecting flared rim. 23 The appearance of Silla-type stoneware and its spread outwards from Kyŏngju have traditionally been interpreted respectively as reflecting the formation of the Silla state and its political expansion.

A particular problem in this expansion has been the status of the east bank of the Naktong River, especially the Pusan region. Since this area displays Silla-type stoneware from the beginning of the 5th century, many archaeologists (e.g. Takesue 1992) have postulated that Silla expanded its administrative territory first into this region. In fact, the Pokchŏn-dong tombs in Pusan are a source of great friction between Silla and Kaya scholars who each want to claim them for their own area. Typical of the tug of war is Lee Hee-Joon's recent reassessment of the cultural and political significance of the emergence of Silla-type pottery in Naktong-east (Lee H. J. 1996). Out of a general background of Kaya-type pottery which reaches all the way to Kyongju in the 4th century, Naktong-east shows the incremental appearance of unstandardized forms of Silla-type pottery in the late 4th century. From these conditions, Lee H. J. postulates that this area was part of the locus of development for this pottery style, which occurred without implications of political administration. By the time Silla pottery became standardized in the early 5th century, he describes the Pusan region as leaning more towards Silla culturally but remaining Kaya in affiliation until the 6th century.

In contrast, Silla archaeologists usually insist that the presence of Silla pottery is a clear indication of Silla administration. The problem is that cultural spheres are not necessarily congruent with political territories. While it is possible to postulate the replacement of pottery styles with the imposition of new administration systems which destroy local supply patterns (as probably happened after the Silla conquest of Kaya), such radical change did not apply to Naktong-east. In any case, it is extremely difficult to postulate political affiliation from cultural remains, since a powerful state often encourages emulation, as can be seen in the Naktongwest region.

West of the Naktong River, there was a shift in focus within Kaya culture from the southern coast during the 4th century to the interior Naktong basin (near Taegu City) in the 5th century. The role of Silla in this shift is thought to have been less important than the Koguryŏ invasion of the south coast in the late 4th century, but it does mean that the geo-political configuration within Yŏngnam between the Kaya and Silla regions was radically different in these two centuries. Lee H. J. notes that there were massive changes in the nature of the Kaya burials with the emergence of a strong Silla in the 5th century. Silla influence stimulated the construction of high earth-mounded tombs and an interest in gold-based ornamentation. Pure gold ornaments are generally found only the Kyŏngju region, while the Kaya areas rely on gilt bronze, silver, and even gilt iron. Moreover, the standard set of personal status goods – crown, belt and sword – first seen at Hwangnam #98 in Kyŏngju rarely occurs as a whole set in the outlying regions, but some tombs have crowns and others belts, etc. Thus, it seems that bits and pieces of Silla material culture are copied by regional Kaya chieftains for use in their own status systems.

The high earthen-mounded tombs of the Kaya regions surrounding Kyŏngju to the south and west contained very large pit-style stone chambers fitted out with wooden chambers inside. The 5th century was the florescence of this kind of stone pit-chamber, and the goods contained therein show a high level of social stratification – not only gold and silver ornamented objects, but massive amounts of stoneware, and also iron armour and weapons. It is interesting that few tombs in Kyŏngju have yielded armour, the early Kujŏng-dong burial and Wŏlsŏng-no Ka–13 being rare exceptions; however, armour is common at 5th-century sites in the outlying regions, including Pusan. The implications of this distributional pattern are still being pondered, since it is well known that Silla had the military strength in the mid-6th century to conquer the main Kaya areas of Pon-Kaya in Kimhae on the south coast and Tae-Kaya in the Naktong Basin interior. The coridor-chamber tomb cluster of Kwisan-dong in Kimhae was probably built by the Silla bureaucrats installed as governors of the newly established Kŭmgwan lesser capital of the expanded Silla state (Nishitani 1992).

On its northern edge, Silla pushed into the Han River basin in the mid-6th century; one outstanding archaeological site deriving from this push is the Isŏng Sansŏng fortress site on the summit of a mountain outside of Seoul see [Chapter 6, this volume]. Excavations in the late 1980s revealed two buildings within the fortress wall: a hexagonal pavilion and a long rectangular bureaucratic structure, both mounted on stone pillar bases and having tiled roofs (cf. Figure 6.9). Small iron figurines of horses were also found at the site, suggesting that Silla military rituals involved horse figurines much like those found at the Nara palace site in Japan a century later. Wood tablet writings have also been discovered here as well as at Anap-chi Pond in central Kyŏngju (Lee D. H. 1993). These northern exploits were also accompanied by coridor-chamber tomb construction: at Maeryŏng-ri in the upper Han River valley as Silla repelled Koguryŏ in 551, and at Chunggok-dong in the modern Seoul vicinity in 553 as Silla challenged Paekche (Nishitani 1981: 119).

A thousand or more Three Kingdoms period fortresses were built across the peninsular landscape during the unification wars of the 6th–7th centuries (cf. Figure 6.1). Not all their names are known, and the locations of the fortresses mentioned in the documentary sources have not all been identified – such is the mismatch between archaeological and historical data. However, at least 160 fortresses have been identified from documentary sources as belonging to Silla (Pak P. L. 1992). 24

Pak Pang-Lyŏng sees the major characteristics of Silla fortresses progressing chronologically (Pak P. L. 1992: 110):

- 1. Being built of earthen walls or wooden palisades in the early phase; small in size and located near rivers or streams.
- 2. Next, walls are built with rubble interiors and tamped earth exteriors.
- 3. Stone walls are not built until after the late 5th century.
- 4. In the 7th century, fortresses with wall circumferences of more than 2 km were built at high locations in the landscape, often over 400m m.s.l. (Isŏng Sansŏng would fit in this category).
- 5. Offensive and defensive facilities were constructed separately.
- 6. Fortress sites are often accompanied by tomb clusters of the same date.
- 7. Projections in the fortress walls take advantage of lookout points afforded by the landscape.
- 8. The fortress interior was variously levelled or built up to accommodate building foundations.
- 9. The exterior base of the wall widened considerably to provide a good support.

Surrounding the Kyŏngju Basin are several fortress sites which protected the emerging Silla state, which is thought to have had its capital at the central fortress of Panwŏl-sŏng. These forts were built between the late 6th and early 7th centuries, as known from the stele of Namsan Sansŏng construction in 591. Thus was the Silla capital fortified during its wars of unification. Another spate of fortress construction occurred in the late 7th century as Silla began to occupy its conquered territories: Poksan-sŏng in 663, Pukhyŏng Sansŏng in 673, Pungwŏn lesser capital in 678, Kŭmgwan lesser capital in 680, Hwajang-sŏng in 672, and Chungwŏn Kungwŏn-sŏng in 673 (Nishitani 1981: 121).

## **Silla's capital development**

According to the specifications laid out above, Panwŏl-sŏng,25 the most obvious walled fortress in the Kyŏngju Basin \(Figure 8.3F, 8.7), can be seen as a very early construction: it is located at low altitude near a river and has simple earth-mounded walls. It presumably functioned as the palace (Pak P. L. 1992; Tanaka 1995; Kim C. H. 1995) of the 4th–5th century Sarŏ-Silla entity until a gidded city was built modelled on the Tang Chinese plan on the plains just north of the fortress. Excavations have been recently conducted at both, showing Panwŏl-sŏng to have had a water access point on its northern side and revealing many large buildings with pillar foundation stones in the gidded city beyond (KKPC 1985). The Silla capital has traditionally been referred to as Kŭmsŏng, but it is not clear whether this name applied to Panwŏl-sŏng, the gidded capital or both.26 The moat itself yielded 30 new wooden tablets, one of which was inscribed with "eighth street" (Cho YJ. & Nan SJ. 1990).

![](_page_319_Figure_0.jpeg)

**Figure 8.7** Silla mounded tomb clusters and the outline of the 7th-century gidded Kŭmsŏng capital and palace (after Azuma & Tanaka as reproduced in Kim C. H. 1995). Key to 8.7:

#### Tomb clusters:

| 1 | Kumchok-ri |
|---|---------------|
| 2 | Pomun-dong |
| 3 | Hwangnam-dong |
| 4 | Nosŏ-donq |
## | 5 | Nodong-dong |

- 6 Hwang' o-dong
- 7 Inwang-dong
- 8 Onung

Unfortunately, the interior of Panwŏl-sŏng has not been excavated to any meaningful extent. The name of Chae-sŏng was inscribed on a roof tile discovered at the site, and the location of the "East Gate" was excavated in 1979–80 (Pak P. L. 1992: 67–9). However, this gate faces northeast, due to the fortress walls following the irregular, crescent-shaped hillock on which it is situated. Thus, it is likely that many references to directional gates in the Samguk Sagi refer instead to the later gidded city and palace on the plain rather than this earlier structure.

As known from the Samguk Sagi, construction of the gided capital (Figure 8.7) proceeded apace during the reign of King Munmu (r. 661–681). One of the first things built was Anap-chi Pond in 674, suggesting that the canal system for supplying water to the capital and also draining the mud flats was a basic pre-requisite. The actual positioning of the gid has been reconstructed by Yun Mu-byŏng on the basis of limited excavations by the Kyŏngju Museum and Institute. 27 The size of the gid square has been determined as 140m NS x 160m EW, with 36 gid squares making up the capital; this differs greatly from the figures given in the Samguk Yusa ranging from 360 to 1360 gid squares (Kim C. H. 1995: 86).

The central avenue (Phoenix Boulevard?) is positioned as running from the centre of Panwŏl-sŏng in the south directly north to the palace quadrant. 28 The modern road corresponding to this avenue is not directly oriented true north-south; the east-west cross-streets conforming to it, which form the city streets of Kyŏngju today, are also tilted a bit to the northwest. In Tanaka Toshiaki's imposition of Yun's gid onto the plains topography (Tanaka 1995) this tilt has been ignored and the gid imposed with polar north-south referents (Figure 8.7). However, the tilt might indicate that magnetic north was used to lay out the gid, and magnetic north might have been different then than today. The palace quadrant at the north center of the gid unfortunately overlaps with the current North Tributary river bed. Whether the river had a different course at that time or the palace only utilized the available southwest corner of the quadrant is unknown. The archaeological site of Sŏngdong-dong is thought to equate with the palace from the terminal 7th century. The stone bidge that crossed the South Tributary just west of Panwŏl-sŏng, whose footings are still present today, conforms to this gid and was built at the time of the gid capital.

The administrative system of the Six Divisions was established in the early 6th century, and as Tanaka Toshiaki writes, "to be a resident of the Six Divisions was to be a resident of the Capital." (Tanaka 1995: 139–40). He emphasizes that the distinction between Capital residents and outsiders was the basis for Silla's social status system and deeply connected with the kolp'um ranking system. If it was indeed the case that the Sarŏ villages in the adjoining valleys, whether originally six or not, were incorporated into the Silla conception of "central place", it is possible that we should not expect to see nuclear urbanization of the Kyŏngju plain before the establishment of the gidded city. In a decentralized "central place", the Kim clan palace at Panwŏl-sŏng would have formed the political focus, with the aristocracy scattered in their traditional settlements. The Kyŏngju plain to the northwest of Panwŏl-sŏng was already being used as a royal cemetery, leaving little stable land for permanent urbanization. However, there was plenty of open space for the gathering of the military forces that Silla needed for its unification expeditions. With a decentralized aristocracy, communication routes would have been of utmost importance to link the Silla king at Panwŏl-sŏng with his advisors and generals.

Thus, in future studies of the Kyŏngju Basin, we should be prepared for the possibility of finding little evidence of flat land occupation before the building of the gided city. And excavations in the outlying valleys will be crucial to developing the overall picture of the Silla capital. Currently, three different routings of the new high-speed railway are under consideration – all passing through the area west of the Hyŏng River. With the archaeological excavations accompanying the railbed construction, in the next few years our understanding of west Kyŏngju Basin archaeology and the development of central Silla should be greatly increased.

#### **Notes**

- 1In old archaeological reports, burials are often referred to as "pit-burials" (*fogwang-myo*) without any reference to the coffin structures within the pits due to poor recovery techniques. These days, the term *t'ogwang-myo* may be used to preface any of the terms for burials utilizing a dug-out pit, such as *fogwang mokkan-myo* ("pit wood-coffin burial").
- 2The double wooden construction was adopted from Han Dynasty China, where the structures are referred to as "inner coffin" and "outer coffin". However, the so-called "outer coffin" (*mokkwak*) can be extremely large and substantial in size, such as at 5th-century Pokch'ŏn-dong in Pusan, where the largest wooden chamber burial measures ca. 6m long, 4m wide and 1.5m high – tall enough for a short person to stand up inside. Thus, in this paper, the term "chamber" rather than "coffin" will be adopted for the outer wooden structure.

- 3See Azuma 1995 for a discussion of this reference.
- 4In the past, some scholars have postulated direct continuity between the Bronze Age and Three Kingdoms stone-constructed burials of Yŏngnam, but this idea is no longer given any credence (see Ch'oi B. H. 1990). Nevertheless, the similarities are striking, and the disappearance of stone-built burials in the Late Iron Age southern peninsula is enigmatic [see Chapter 2, this volume].
- 5Which may have a wooden coffin or chamber within.
- 6It is not always clear from a perusal of excavation report drawings which kind of burial chamber is being illustrated, since the stone structures that remain are superficially similar to the casual eye. Thus, the excavator's opinions in the text are of crucial importance, though some scholars may have differing individual opinions of which type a particular burial chamber belongs.
- 7Regional variants such as the "side-opening stone chamber" (*hoenggu-sik sŏkkwak-pun*) and "long stone chamber" (*cbangpanghyŏng sŏksil-pun*) will not be dealt with here; see Ch'oi B. H. 1994.
- 8"stone-surround wood-chamber burial" = *oesŏk mokkwang-myo*; "wood-chamber piled-stone burial" = *mokkwak chŏksŏk-pun*.
- 9According to Lee S. J. (pers. comm. Aug 96), Kim H. G. 1996 has identified the earliest Type Al burials to be Chungsan-ri #74 and 75; Type A2 can be seen in Chungsan-ri #VIII–14; A4 at Chungsan-ri #IA–51. Since Kim's thesis was published, however, the excavators have determined that #39 is perhaps the oldest Type Al (Pak Mun-su, pers. comm., Aug 96).
- 10Several cemeteries have in the past been thought to contain stone-piled burials (Angye-ri in Wŏlsong, T'ap-ri and Tae-ri in Uisŏng, and Pokchŏn-dong in Pusan); Ch'oi B. H. says these determinations were premature and they do not fit his category of [royal] stone-piled tombs (Ch'oi B. H. 1990: 95). However, others argue that at least some (Okchŏn #54 and Pokch'ŏn-dong #26, 32, 73) are similar to one type of stone-piled tomb found in Kyŏngju and therefore should be considered as such (Song K. H. 1992).

- 11Burial #12 at Kyo-dong in Changnyŏng had a stone-mounded cover; Ch'oi B. H. (1990) assesses this as the probable result of the Silla advance and the dispatch of elites to different parts of the country. He emphasises this is an unusual case and so reinforces the impression that stone-mounded burials are limited to Kyŏngju.
- 12As Ch'oi notes, there are many problems concerning the source of the tomb structure from Lelang, or Koguryŏ, or Paekche and also problems with the typology from "side-opening" type (*hoenggu-sik sŏksil-pun*) to how the roof is constructed (flat stones or corbelled ceilings).
- 13Scholars in Group 1: Pak Chin-uk, Kim Ki-ung, Im Se-yŏng, Lee Insook [I Insuk], Ch'oi Byŏng-hyŏn, Morikatsu Toshihiko; Group 2: Itō Akio, Anazawa Wakou, Ch'oi Chŏng-kyu, Shin Kyung Cheol [Shin Kyŏng-ch'ŏl], Pak Po-hyŏn; Group 3: Lee Chong-sŏn, Kim Wŏn-yŏng, Haraguchi Shōzō, Fujii Kazuo, Satome Masahiro.
- 14This tomb is a double mound with a female burial in the north and a male burial in the south. The entire double unit is designated Hwangnam-dong #98, but it is commonly referred to in speech as Hwangnam Taech'ŏng. In speaking of it, one must be careful to distinguish which mound, North or South, is being discussed.
- 15Currently, the dating groups can be described as: 1) Early: Ch'oi B. H. and his followers (including Lee Insook, a leading gold-working analyst), postulating a middle 4th-century date for #98 South; 2) Late: the Pusan school – following Shin Kyung Cheol and including Pusan National University, Kyŏngsŏng University, and their graduates such as An Chaeho at Dongguk University in Kyŏngju, and Kim Doo-chul [Kim Tu-ch'ŏl] – who advocate a late 5th-century date; and 3) Middle: the Kyŏngju school – including Lee Hee-joon (Kyŏngbuk National University, Taegu) and Lee Sung-joo himself, and archaeologists at the Kyŏngju National Museum and Kyŏngju Cultural Properties Research Institute – who assign it to the early 5th century.
- 16Onoyama's 1966 work discussed the earliest stirrups not only in Japan but in all of East Asia; it has been superseded by finds at the 4th-century Jin-dynasty tomb of Xiaomin-tun #154 in Anyang and the

- Eastern Jin tomb of Yuantaizi in Chaoyang, China (see Kim D. C. 1996).
- 17KAERI forms are represented on pp. 346–9, and translated dates on p. 365 of KMY (1996).
- 18In this case, his datings of the Maripkan period follow those of the Samguk Yusa rather than the Samguk Sagi, citing King Naemul (r. 356–402) as the adopter of the Maripkan title.
- 19For a sense of the problem, see Lee J. W. (1986).
- 20Yi Unsŏk, Kyŏngju Cultural Properties Research Institute, pers. comm. Aug. 1996.
- 21I have previously made the same point for Yamato, where the 5thcentury Ōjin Dynasty is thought to have had foreign roots \(Barnes 1990\). The phenomenon is well known in Europe, where elite members of royal families are exchanged between polities not only through marriage alliances (witness the present Queen of England's husband, of German stock marrying in from Greece); but also in direct co-option to the throne (for example, William of Orange of Holland who was given the throne jointly with his wife Mary in the late 17th century, or George III from the House of Hanover in what is now northern Germany, who was brought in to succeed Queen Anne in the early 18th century).
- 22As mentioned above, the earliest stoneware repertoire is recognized at the shellmound site of Puwŏn-dong on the south coast. (cf. Kim W. Y. 1982). Although this lineage of pottery is commonly called "stoneware" because it eventually became stoneware and maintains the same shape repertoire, the early examples are commonly not fired high enough to be considered true stoneware see [Chapter 4, this volume].
- 23According to Lee Hee-joon's demonstration of the takeover of Tae-Kaya by Silla; Yŏngnam Archaeological Society Meetings, July 1996, Taegu.
- 24Typical Silla examples are Talsŏng in Taegu, Sunji-ri T'osŏng in Yangsan, Samnyŏn Sansŏng in Chungbuk Po'on-gun, Chŏksŏng in

- Tanyang, Sŏngnyŏng Sansŏng in Hongwŏn-gun, Onori Sansŏng in Sankich'ŏn-myŏn, Kwisan Sansŏng in Chinhae, and Ch'ŏnsaeng Sansŏng and Kŭmdol Sansŏng in Kyŏngbuk.
- 25The name of this hillfort occurs both as Panwŏl-sŏng ("half-moon"-fort) and Wŏl-sŏng ("moon"-fort); both probably refer to its crescent shape as a natural hillock on the north bank of the South River tributary in Kyŏngju. It is also known as Sinwŏl-sŏng and Chae-sŏng (Pak P. L. 1992).
- 26Pak P. L. (1992: 60–6) reviews current interpretations derived mainly from the historical sources.
- 27Yun's map is reproduced in Kim C. H. 1995: fig 2.
- 28Today it runs along the west side of Sŏndŏk Girls' Middle and Commercial High School at the very eastern edge of modern Kyŏngju City. Kim C. H. (1995: 84) notes that the real name of this boulevard is not known for Silla, but since it was called Phoenix Boulevard in both Chang'an and Nara, he is amenable to its use for Silla.
 - \*This chapter was first presented as an invited paper at the conference, Silla and the *Samguk Sagi*, 15–19 February 1997. It will appear in the conference volume, *Silla and the Samguk Sagi*, edited by J. E. Shultz & H. W. Kang, University Press of Hawaii, forthcoming.

# **Index**

This index is organized hierarchically. Entries in ALL CAPS indicate composite collections of entries such as archaeological objects, burials, chronology, historical sources, metals, etc. Many proper names, in bold, are grouped under entries of archaeological sites, capitals, cities, geography, historical persons, historical placenames, and legendary persons, mountains, polities, rivers, etc. The seven candidates for statehood discussed in Chapter 1 are in bold ALL CAPS, and the main pages discussing each case study are emboldened. See other state/polity names under 'polities'. Crossreferences and name alternatives or romanization differences are given in square brackets. Periodizations are given under the CHRONOLOGY entry on a separate page (see page 237).
